# John Amos Comenius’s
# OUTLINE OF
# UNIVERSAL WISDOM,
## A Ground-plan
### and architectural
### drawing

->*Sketching out the breadth, dimensions, and practical uses of the entire future Work.*<-

A scroll entwined around the tree trunk bears the motto "Do not stray beyond the olive trees" <note>original: "NE EXTRA OLEAS"; this was the trademark of the Elzevir publishing house, a proverb meaning to stay within the boundaries or stick to the subject.</note> At the base of the tree, on a small patch of ground, sits an owl, a traditional symbol of wisdom.</image-desc>

<summary>This title page introduces Comenius's plan for "Pansophy," a systematic organization of all human knowledge. He uses architectural metaphors—the ground-plan and elevation—to describe how his "Universal Wisdom" will be structured and applied.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Universal Wisdom, Pansophy, Elzevir Press, 17th-century philosophy, educational reform, Amsterdam</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1645 Elzevir edition. It contains the printer's preface, explaining the reasons for this new edition of Comenius’s work. The "bleed-through" mentioned in the OCR refers to the title "PANSOPHIAE DIATYPOSIS" from the reverse side of the paper.</meta>

# TO THE BENEVOLENT READER
## THE PRINTER
->Greetings.<- <note>original: "S.", an abbreviation for *Salutem dicit*, a formal Latin greeting wishing the reader health.</note>

*Since the rarity of copies of this Outline* <note>original: "Diatyposeos"</note> *had kindled a desire in many to see it, and there were those who, stirred by its reputation, were demanding that a copy be made available to them; I decided, with the Author’s consent, to reprint the booklet for the sake of those who love these studies. My hope is that by doing so, the rationale and method of the Pansophic* <term>Universal Wisdom; from the Greek 'pan' (all) and 'sophia' (wisdom)</term> *proposal might be more correctly understood, and that the weight of such a noble subject might urge others to contribute their own studies and efforts to this cause. Use these pages then, Reader, and support the Author’s endeavors as much as you are able.*

<vocab>
**Reader** <note>original: "Lector"</note>,
**Printer** <note>original: "Typographus"</note>,
**Of the Outline** <note>original: "Diatyposeos"</note>,
**Pansophic** <note>original: "Pansophici"</note>,
**Author** <note>original: "Auctor"</note>
</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the printer Louis Elzevir explains that he is reissuing Comenius’s "Outline" due to popular demand and the scarcity of previous copies. He hopes the publication will clarify Comenius's vision for universal wisdom and inspire other scholars to collaborate on the project.</summary>
<keywords>Louis Elzevir, book trade, publishing history, Pansophy, Jan Amos Comenius, 17th-century education, scholarly collaboration</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page begins the main text of Comenius's work, following the printer's preface. It establishes the central metaphor of the project: building a "Temple of Wisdom" requires a careful architectural blueprint.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ali- (likely continuing to "alio" or "aliorsum")</meta>

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'A' at the beginning of the text, featuring an ornate background of stylized floral motifs and scrollwork within a rectangular border.</image-desc>

As we labor (no longer in secret imaginations alone, but in the open) for the honor of the Great God, the common benefit of the human race, and as a prelude to the future life, upon the structure of the **TEMPLE OF WISDOM**, it is fitting to imitate the skill of wise Architects. These craftsmen are accustomed to never begin the construction of any major work without a previous Outline <note>original: "Delineatione," referring to a preliminary architectural sketch or plan</note> of the future work. This practice, besides the delight it provides (for our mind, being carried toward the future by its own nature, is charmed by the foresight of a coming thing), has a remarkable use in preventing the loss of expenses, time, and reputation. Indeed, once a model or idea <note>original: "simulacro, aut ideâ"</note> of the future work has been erected, it is easy to see clearly whether the fullness of the Whole, the Symmetry <term>The proportional and harmonious arrangement of parts in a building</term> of the Parts, and the beauty of individual elements are sufficiently consistent. And then, if anything is missing, or in excess,

<vocab>
**Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientia"</note>,
**Temple** <note>original: "Templum"</note>,
**Architect** <note>original: "Architectus"</note>,
**Outline** <note>original: "Delineatio"</note>,
**Symmetry** <note>original: "Symmetria"</note>
</vocab>

<summary>Comenius introduces his project as the construction of a "Temple of Wisdom," arguing that such a massive intellectual undertaking requires a preliminary architectural blueprint to ensure harmony and prevent the waste of resources.</summary>
<keywords>architectural metaphor, Temple of Wisdom, Pansophy, intellectual planning, Jan Amos Comenius, divine honor, human progress</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural planning as a metaphor for intellectual and spiritual labor, picking up from the previous page's discussion on avoiding errors by using a preliminary sketch.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Pri-</meta>

*...is noticed to be otherwise than it should be, it remains possible to add, take away, or change things, according to necessity or whim. Likewise, a more exact calculation of expenses can be undertaken when, from such an **Outline** <term>Sciagraphia: a technical term for a cross-section or architectural profile</term>, the length, width, and height—along with the required **Materials**, **Form**, **Workers**, **Tools**, and **Time**—are foreseen. By comparing these with his own resources, the **Architect** judges whether he can safely undertake the **Work**. This is done so that what Christ warns of in the parable does not happen to him—that he be mocked by those who might say: This man began to build, and was not able to finish (Luke 14).* <note>The biblical reference is to Luke 14:28-30, where Jesus speaks of the cost of discipleship and the importance of planning.</note>

*Architects are accustomed to conceive the form of a future work in the mind and express it in a model <note>original: "typo"</note> in three ways. First, in the **ground plan** <note>original: "in plano"</note>, as the foundations are laid; second, in the **elevation** <note>original: "in fronte," referring to the front view or facade</note>, as it will be seen in the raised walls; and finally, most fully, as all things will hold together through all **dimensions**, both inside and out.*

**Vocabulary**
Outline <note>original: "Sciagraphia"</note>, Architect <note>original: "Architector"</note>, materials <note>original: "materia"</note>, form <note>original: "forma"</note>, workers <note>original: "operarii"</note>, tools <note>original: "instrumenta"</note>, Luke 14 <note>original: "Luc. 14"</note>, ground plan <note>original: "planum"</note>, facade/front <note>original: "frons"</note>, dimensions <note>original: "dimensiones"</note>

<summary>Comenius explains how a detailed architectural plan allows for adjustments and budgeting before construction begins, citing a biblical parable about the necessity of finishing what one starts. He then outlines the three essential stages of a design: the ground plan, the elevation, and the three-dimensional integration of the whole structure.</summary>

<keywords>architectural planning, project management, Luke 14, ground plan, elevation, dimensions, construction metaphor, wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the architectural metaphor established on page 2, where the author compares the planning of an intellectual system to the drafting of a building. The previous page concluded by identifying three ways an architect visualizes a project; this page defines those terms and applies them to the author's "Pansophic" project.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

They call the model of the first kind **Ichnography** <term>Ichnographia: a ground plan or footprint of a building showing the arrangement of foundations</term>; that of the second, **Orthography** <term>Orthographia: an elevation or vertical drawing showing the front facade</term>; and that of the third, **Scenography** <term>Scenographia: a perspective drawing that shows depth and three dimensions</term> and **Scale Model** <note>original: "Proplasma," a Greek term for a physical model, often made of clay or wax</note>, in Latin called a **Module** <note>original: "Modulum"</note> or **Model**. It pleases us to imitate all these things in this **General Description** <note>original: "Diatyposi"</note> of ours: and first, to delineate only the bare foundations of the **Pansophic Temple** <term>Templum Pansophicum: the "Temple of All-Wisdom," a central concept in 17th-century philosophy representing a universal and harmonized system of all human knowledge</term>; showing what kind of **Work** it is that we desire, and why it is now necessary to undertake thoughts regarding its construction? This will be our **Ground Plan** <note>original: "Ichnographia"</note>.

Next, we shall explain its requirements, both in terms of **Material** and **Exterior Form**: this will represent the upright face of this Temple and shall be called the **Elevation** <note>original: "Orthographia"</note>.

Finally, we shall attempt to prefigure all the **Parts**, as well as their number, order, and use: which will provide a complete **Model** <note>original: "Proplasma"</note> of the Pansophic Temple. But in all these things we shall observe that rule of **Architects**,

<meta>catchword: who [qui]</meta>

<summary>The author defines the architectural terms Ichnography, Orthography, and Proplasma, explaining how they serve as a structural metaphor for his proposal to build a universal "Temple of Wisdom."</summary>
<keywords>Pansophic Temple, architecture, Ichnography, Orthography, Scenography, Proplasma, universal knowledge, planning</keywords>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>A 3</sig>

*...who wishes the **Model** <note>original: "Modulum"</note> to lack any exquisite artifice: that is, for it not to be polished, disguised with colors, or decorated with paintings, but naked and simple: so that the **Work** itself may be judged more sincerely.*

*You, O God, who alone have discovered all the paths of understanding and the underlying principles of all things, who alone know them, and who alone can reveal them to anyone: have mercy on us; and command that which we undertake for Your glory to be such that the future builders of Your **Temple of Wisdom** <term>Sapientiæ Templi: the author's vision for a universal system of knowledge that mirrors the divine order</term> may consider what You once said to Your servant **Moses** to be spoken to themselves:*

> *Look and act according to the **P A T T E R N** <note>original: "EXEMPLAR"</note> shown to you.*
> *Exodus 25:40.*

<summary>The author advocates for a simple, unadorned model of his philosophical system to ensure its structural integrity is judged fairly, concluding with a prayer for divine guidance to follow the heavenly "pattern" of wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Model, simplicity, Temple of Wisdom, Moses, Exodus 25:40, divine revelation, architectural metaphor</keywords>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>A 4</sig>

# THE TEMPLE OF WISDOM
## GROUND PLAN <term>Ichnographia: a technical term for a ground-plan or architectural drawing, representing the foundation of a building</term>

->*Comprised in*<-
->*Aphorisms.* <note>original: "Aphoriſmis"</note><-

->I.<-

We call the **TEMPLE OF WISDOM** <term>Templum Sapientiæ: the author’s vision for a universal system of knowledge that mirrors the divine order of the universe</term> a **BOOK**, in which all things that ever are, will be, or have been are offered for contemplation to Man, as a **Creature** made for the purpose of contemplating the **Works** of his **Creator**. This is done with that specific end in mind, and with means and order so disposed toward that end, that by the help of all these things he may be led and guided to Him—from Whom, through Whom, and in Whom all things exist <note>original: "Ex quo, Per quem, & In quem ſunt omnia"; a citation of Romans 11:36</note>—**GOD**, and to eternal **HAPPINESS** <note>original: "Beatitudinem"</note> in Him.

->II.<-

For this, finally, is what it means **TO BE WISE** <note>original: "Sapere"</note>: *to understand and provide for one's own final end* (as God speaks in Deuteronomy 32:29): lest Man stray from his goal, **Happiness**, for the enjoyment of which he was created along with his Creator.

<vocab>Temple of Wisdom, Ground Plan, Aphorism, Happiness (or Blessedness), Creature, Creator, To be Wise, God</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "Temple of Wisdom" as a metaphorical book of all existence intended to guide humanity toward God and eternal happiness. It asserts that true wisdom consists in understanding and preparing for one's ultimate spiritual destiny.</summary>
<keywords>Temple of Wisdom, Ground Plan, Aphorisms, Creation, Creator, Happiness, Wisdom, Deuteronomy 32:29, Romans 11:36</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Page 7 regarding the nature of human happiness and the definition of wisdom. It transitions from the internal desire for well-being to the necessity of education and mutual enlightenment to overcome the "lethargy" caused by the fall of man.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIÆ"</note></header>

...just as that natural desire indicates to him, by which every **Human being** wishes to be **well**, and indeed without end. For he dreads death and destruction, but desires life to be as pleasant for himself as possible, and therefore flees from all adversity.

->III.<-

But because through the terrible fall into sin, and through depravity continued through the ages and always increasing, mortals have become so dull-witted <note>original: "obrutuerunt," suggesting being overwhelmed or buried under a weight</note> that they do not understand the **divine plan** <note>original: "divinum consilium"</note> regarding themselves as created beings, nor those desires of their own nature—much less the **Ways** to a blessed end—or even care to understand them; they must be awakened from their lethargy by all modes and methods that can ever be devised.

->IV.<-

For we are all bound to be ministers of the **Divine goodness**, and however much light anyone has received from God, he ought to desire to pour back just as much into others: so that by the rays being multiplied, and variously reflected, refracted, and blended back and forth among themselves, the light in minds may be increased, for the purpose of seeing and finding the **Father of lights** <note>A reference to James 1:17; God as the source of all intellectual and spiritual illumination</note>, and walking in his light.

<sig>4</sig>
<meta>catchword: V. God</meta>

<summary>This page argues that while humans naturally desire eternal happiness, sin has clouded their minds, requiring them to be "awakened" through a collective sharing of divine "light" and knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Wisdom, desire for immortality, the Fall, divine plan, enlightenment, Father of Lights, education as awakening</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 8, where the author described humans as "ministers of Divine goodness" who must share their light with one another. Here, the text transitions from the human duty of education to the underlying theological principle: that God typically works through "ordinary means" and the mutual cooperation of his creatures to maintain the universe.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

<header># OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS," a term meaning a formal description, sketch, or schematic representation of a subject.</note></header>

<sig>A 5</sig>

->V.<-

For although God himself sustains the **World** he created by his own **Power**, illuminates it with his own light, and rules it by his own **providence**: yet because he acts in an ordinary way through ordinary **means**, it happens that by his arrangement some **Creatures** are formed, reformed, and nurtured by others, and directed toward their ends.

->VI.<-

The entire chorus of **Creatures** bears witness to this, as do all the works of **Nature**, joined together in such a tight order that nothing among them all exists or happens for itself, by itself, or through itself; but each one depends on another, serves another, and is sustained by another. For this reason, he distributed various virtues <note>original: "virtutes," referring to the inherent powers or qualities that make a thing effective</note> among various Creatures, so that each one (in that part by which it is what it is, and acts as it does) might represent the role of its **Creator**, serving its neighboring Creatures, and thereby preserving the **Universe** itself.

->VII.<-

Therefore, because God has also trained some through others within the human race up to this point, in order to gradually multiply the light of the **knowledge** of himself and his works; and since now the **Last times** of the **World** <note>original: "Ultima Mundi... tempora," reflecting a common 17th-century belief that the world was entering its final, most enlightened age before the apocalypse</note> seem to be at hand...

<meta>catchword: gitan- [likely leading to "giganteis" or "gitantibus"]</meta>

<vocab>
<term>Diatyposis</term>: Outline or vivid description.
<term>Creaturae</term>: Creatures, or anything created.
<term>Virtus</term>: Power, efficacy, or inherent strength.
<term>Providentia</term>: Providence; God's guidance of the universe.
<term>Natura</term>: Nature.
<term>Creator</term>: The Creator (God).
<term>Cognitio</term>: Knowledge or recognition.
<term>Mundus</term>: The World.
</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that God governs the world through "ordinary means," using the interconnectedness of all creatures to sustain the universe. It argues that humans have a duty to share knowledge with one another, especially as the author believes the "last times" of the world are approaching.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Interconnectedness, Divine Order, Nature, Education, Last Times, Mutual Service</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 9, concluding the sentence regarding the multiplication of knowledge in the "last times" of the world. The previous page ended with "cogitandum est" (we must consider) partially broken across the page break.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

<header>UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PAN SOPHIAE," the genitive form of Pansophia, meaning "Of Universal Wisdom."</note></header>

[we must] consider how we may also advance ourselves toward the final degree of knowledge (that which can indeed be hoped for in this Life). Namely, so that we may **make our knowledge** (1) entirely UNIVERSAL, not disfigured by any harmful gap; (2) entirely TRUE, not contaminated by any mixture of the false and the vain; (3) entirely EASY, not hindered by any thorns or rough paths <note>original: "salebris," referring to rugged or uneven ground that jolts a traveler</note> of obscurity and ambiguity, but flowing into minds of its own accord.

VIII. If it is granted to obtain these three things, our human Knowledge will be in some way perfect, as a beautifully shining image within us of the divine **Omniscience**: disposing us so that, as we strive along the same paths, through the same means, toward the same ends, we may unlearn our usual discords and the vanities of any trivialities <note>original: "parergorum," from the Greek for side-tasks or non-essential matters</note>, and being wise, all of us may know, do, and speak only those things that are best.

IX. Therefore, we propose that a **Book** must be constructed which contains ALL things necessary for a human being for this and the future life: in what must be known and done, and what must be believed and hoped for.

<margin>Universal Wisdom, Universal Knowledge, Cognition, Divine Omniscience, The Book</margin>

<meta>catchword: neat:</meta>

<summary>The author defines the three requirements for perfect human knowledge—universality, truth, and ease—and proposes the creation of a "Book" (Pansophia) that encompasses everything necessary for human existence and salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, universal knowledge, divine omniscience, education, unity, the Great Book</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of the proposed "Book" of Universal Wisdom (Pansophia) described on page 10. It picks up from the catchword "neat:" to complete the description of the book's contents and its epistemological standard of truth.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

<header>OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS," a Greek term for a draft, sketch, or schematic outline.</note></header>

[...namely] an exact account <note>original: "enarrationem"</note> of those things which we already know, with an accurate index everywhere of those things which we are ignorant of: whether because knowing them is simply not granted, or because they remain to be investigated. And indeed, all these things are for that purpose, and by that **Method** <note>original: "Methodo"</note>, so that from the first and lowest things, through the middle, to the last and highest, the minds of men—as if by a certain skillful ladder—may be elevated even to Him from whom, through whom, and for whom all things exist <note>A theological reference to God as the source and end of all creation, echoing Romans 11:36.</note>.

X. But so that the same book may teach all those things **TRULY**, and demonstrate them solidly, so that it clearly appears they are not the mockery of illusions <note>original: "phantasmatum," referring to deceptive mental images or figments of the imagination</note>, but the immovable foundations of the things themselves: and so that we may know that we know, and not merely hold an opinion. Therefore, this Book will contain nothing that would either offend the mind's agreement <note>original: "assensum," the intellectual act of accepting something as true</note>—as things that are openly false, vain, unproven, or improbable do—or merely tickle and invite agreement—as likely or "plausible" things do—but rather it shall compel it. This is the power inherent in the invincible and always victorious **Truth** <note>original: "Veritati"</note>, provided it is skillfully brought into the light.

<sig>A 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: XI.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the "Book" must provide a complete account of known and unknown things to lead the mind toward God, and emphasizes that it must offer certain, undeniable truth rather than mere opinion.</summary>

<keywords>ladder of knowledge, divine source, certainty vs opinion, Method, Truth, phantasms, intellectual assent</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "Book" of Universal Wisdom (Pansophia). Having established the requirement for absolute truth on the previous page, it now addresses the "Method" of presentation and defines the book’s scope using the classical philosophical framework of the four causes.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIÆ"</note></header>
<sig>A</sig>

XI. Furthermore, it will be necessary for this Book to be conceived using a **Method** perfectly tailored <note>original: "ex-a-mussim attemperatâ," an idiom meaning "adjusted according to a builder's level," implying extreme precision.</note> to human intellects, so that whoever reads it may understand it immediately, being guided everywhere by a clear light. In this way, even when translated into the common languages of the Nations, its use may become as universal as God Himself, the World, and our own common sense. For it is nothing other than a true and genuine transcript <note>original: "apographum," a copy or direct reproduction of an original source.</note> of those things which that one God has revealed thus far through His **Works** <note>Nature</note>, His **Voice** <note>Holy Scripture</note>, and the **Notions** impressed upon the mind of every human being. It shall be a true and common torch and guide for all of us toward the future life, and a true standard and directory for everything to be done in this life.

XII. That such a Book should be called **PANSOPHIA** <note>meaning "Universal Wisdom"</note> is suggested by its *matter, form, efficient cause, and end.* <note>These are the four Aristotelian "causes" used in traditional philosophy to provide a complete explanation of what a thing is and why it exists.</note>

XIII. For its *matter* shall be **The All** <note>original Greek: "τὸ πᾶν" (to pan), meaning the totality of existence.</note>, the Universe as it is, which ought not to be unknown to a wise person. That is, whatever good, beautiful, or useful thing is found anywhere in Books; or even those things which do not yet exist anywhere in writing, and yet [must] be had...

<meta>catchword: pof- [Likely the beginning of "possunt," meaning "can"]</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the pedagogical necessity for Pansophia to be accessible to all people in their own languages, serving as a direct reflection of divine revelation in nature and mind. It begins to define the project using the four Aristotelian causes, identifying its "matter" as the entire universe.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, Method, Universal Language, Divine Revelation, The All, Aristotelian Causes, Education</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Book of Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia). It concludes the section on the "Matter" (content) of the book, stating it should encompass all knowledge, and moves into the "Form" of the book, which the author describes as a "Universal Harmony."</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>THE OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS"</note></header>
<sig>A 7</sig>

...can be gathered (both new discoveries and those yet to be discovered) we wish to be collected here: so that concerning whatever matter a mention or thought might arise, sufficient information about it may be found here.

XIV. *The **Form** of this Book ought to be **pan-harmony** <note>original Greek: "παναρμονία" (panarmonia)</note>, that is, Universal Harmony, or the consonance and agreement of all things with all things.* For we must not simply pile up observations about things here in a heap, however useful they may be or wherever they were gathered; rather, the innermost nature of the things themselves must be revealed. This must be done with such craftsmanship <note>original: "artificio"</note> that, just as there is no dissonance in God (the author of all things) or in His **Works** <note>Nature</note> and His **Words** <note>Scripture</note>, so too no discrepancy shall remain in our concepts. This is achieved by finding the central points of things, where even extremes and opposites meet and cease their conflict. By this means, it is hoped that whatever various people think and do in various ways (provided they are rational)—even if they are unaware of one another, misunderstand one another, or even oppose one another—they may here come to agreement and harmony. This will happen by leading the small streams <note>original: "rivulis"</note> of all thoughts and actions back to their true and pu-

<meta>catchword: ros [The word continues on the next page as "puros," meaning "pure sources."]</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Form" of the proposed Universal Wisdom as a "Universal Harmony" where all conflicting human thoughts and observations are reconciled by uncovering their underlying natural truths. The author argues that because God's creation is consistent, human knowledge should also be a unified, non-contradictory system.</summary>

<keywords>Universal Harmony, Pan-harmony, reconciliation of opposites, unity of knowledge, craftsmanship of nature, rational consensus, philosophical method</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 13, completing the sentence about leading thoughts back to their "pure sources." It introduces the logical method of reconciliation and defines the three sources of knowledge (Theaters of God) used in the Pansophic method.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIAE"</note></header>

pure sources, where people, whether they wish to or not, may recognize that they agree with one another, once the windings of the streams and the mixed-in filth have been left behind. For it will happen that most contradictions and controversies will be resolved through *Neither* <note>original: "Neutrum"</note> or *Both* <note>original: "Utrumque"</note>. This is done in the same way that Christ settled the dispute between the Jews and Samaritans, who were arguing over the proper place of prayer, by saying: *Neither on this mountain, nor in Jerusalem, but everywhere in Spirit and in truth.* And elsewhere: *Do this; and do not omit that.* (Matthew 23:23).

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;XV. The **Efficient Cause** <term>Efficient Cause: in philosophy, the agent or force that brings something into being</term> of such great fullness and harmony can be nothing other than **pan-hyperchia** <note>original Greek: "πανυπερχία" — Comenius uses this term to mean a supreme, all-encompassing governance of principles</note>, that is, the *Universality of the Principles of Knowing*. This ensures that whatever aids have been divinely provided to Man to increase and refine the light of knowledge should be employed in their entirety. On God’s part—as He reveals Himself to us from outside Himself—this includes those **Theaters** in which He has displayed what He willed: namely, the *World*, with the whole workmanship of Nature; and *our own Mind*, with all the Notions inscribed within it; and the *Divine Scripture*, with all the mysteries revealed therein. On our part, however,

...all the instruments granted to us for grasping those things which God has revealed: namely, the *external and internal Senses, and the sound power of reasoning*; and finally, *Faith* to be applied to the divine oracles <note>In this context, "oracles" refers to the Holy Scriptures as the direct word of God</note>. XVI. The goal of the Book will be **universal utility** <note>original Greek: "παγχρησία" (panchresia) — meaning "all-usefulness" or "all-purpose application"</note>: *namely, Universal Use, of All things for All purposes*: so that whatever a Human must do or endure, in time and in eternity, may be learned here at once. Here, let the human **MIND** be formed for a sufficient knowledge of all things; and the **HANDS** and the rest of the limbs, for the useful performance of any good works; and the **TONGUE**, for the fitting expression of all the mind’s thoughts; and the **AFFECTIONS** <term>Affections: the emotions, desires, or inclinations of the soul</term>, for the pursuit of all useful things and the flight from useless ones; and finally, let the **HEART** be directed toward God, and in Him alone so...

XX. He subjected the human race to this same condition in all things. Observing this, <term>Augustine</term> <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian philosopher whose views on history deeply influenced Comenius's own theories of education and progress.</note> wrote thus: *“With divine providence beautifully moderating all things, the entire series of generations from Adam until the end of the age is managed just like a single Man, who, from childhood even to the old age of his own time, marks the span of his life by stages of age. XXIV. What, then, forbids us to hope that one day those things which have grown in parts might coalesce into a whole? XXV. If such a thing may be hoped for, why should we not also desire and strive for it? For what would this be other than us acting as the ministers of divine goodness—a goodness that is accustomed to communicating itself to us step by step, and spreading itself ever more widely?

XXVI. Therefore, regarding those various sparks of light—which later became torches—that the Father of Lights <note>original: "Luminum Pater." A reference to James 1:17. XXVII. Yet the *Necessities* of the human race also compel us to do this same thing. I will explain these briefly: first in general terms; then according to specific ranks of men; and finally by looking at these present times in which we find ourselves.

<header>**A GENERAL SKETCH** <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS." This is a Greek term used to describe a vivid outline or a blueprint of a project.</note></header>

Regarding the perpetual confusion of things, the complaints of wise men in every age are well known. No one is unaware of them, unless they are either a stranger to the World—such as infants who have only recently entered it—or a mere stump and block of wood—such as the dull-witted, who pay no attention to what is happening, much less care about it. The source of these confusions, however, is that almost all mortals (even those who seem wise to themselves and to the world) do everything, or certainly their most important tasks, not by counsel and reason, of which they are ignorant, but by chance. *In this sense, Augustine said that most men are fools (Book 1 of On Free Choice of the Will, chapter 9). And Cicero: Nothing is so utterly common as to know nothing. Likewise: I believe a mule has more often given birth than a truly wise man has existed.* <note>Cicero is referencing a common ancient proverb; because mules are sterile, a mule giving birth was considered a nearly impossible omen. He uses this to highlight how vanishingly rare true wisdom is among men.</note> Since, therefore, Wisdom—the **Governor of Things** <note>original: "Rerum moderatrix." Comenius personifies Wisdom as the guiding force that should ideally regulate human behavior and natural order.</note>—has abandoned mankind, is it any wonder that everything is conducted without moderation and through tumult? And that our affairs are not carried toward their proper goals but rush beyond their bounds, casting us down into various precipices? The salvation of the human race, then, would lie in uncovering the true **Ends** <note>In a philosophical sense, "Ends" refers to the ultimate purpose or goal of an action.</note> of things, the certain **Means** leading to those ends, and the legitimate **Methods** for using those means...

XXIX. For because the things that people encounter as objects in this life are numerous and, in a way, infinite: both outside themselves (things natural and man-made, moral and spiritual, good and evil); within themselves (various inclinations, desires, and emotions); and finally alongside themselves (the vastly different opinions, interests, efforts, and actions of other people). It is inevitable that they are pulled in different directions and confused by all these things. # OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIÆ"</note>

...est—if you remove even one, you] would mutilate the use of the whole. Since, therefore, all things that exist come together into the composition of the entire Universe, as if into a single **Commonwealth** <note>original: "Reipublicæ"</note>, with a **concatenation** <term>concatenation: a chain-like connection where things are linked together in a specific order.</term> that is never interrupted: if even one significant thing is ignored, it is necessary that the truth of many others be obscured at the same time. Certainly, what Seneca said regarding the writings of the greatest men <margin>(Epistle 33)</margin> applies here: *They must be inspected in their entirety, and handled as a whole; for they weave their work through the outlines of their own genius, from which nothing can be withdrawn without ruin.* This may be said far more truly of the entire structure of Reality and Learning: that nothing can be withdrawn without ruin. And what that same author said elsewhere regarding life’s plans and the errors of those plans <margin>(Epistle 72)</margin>: *We fail because we all deliberate over the parts of life, but no one deliberates over the whole:* this fits most appropriately with the search for Truth. *Thus we wander in various ways because we deliberate over the parts of Truth; yet no one is concerned with the whole, universal, and all-encompassing Truth that connects itself on every side.* Francisco Sanchez <note>Francisco Sánchez (c. 1550–1623) was a skeptical philosopher and physician. His work "Quod Nihil Scitur" (That Nothing is Known) argued that perfect knowledge is impossible because of the interconnectedness of all things.</note> also saw this and expressed it thus (in his book, *That Nothing is Known*, page 47): *Such is the link between things...*

# OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS"</note>

[...interconnectedness among] all things, so that none is idle, without either hindering or helping another. Indeed, each single thing is born to harm many and to help many. Therefore, it is necessary to know all things for the perfect knowledge of any one thing.

And a little later: *You will recognize this through the very familiar example of a common **Clock** <term>Horologii: a mechanical clock. In the 17th century, these were the ultimate symbols of complex, interdependent systems where every gear matters.</term>. For if you wish to know how it strikes the hours, you must inspect all the wheels from the first to the last: what moves the first, and how this one moves another, and this one moves others, all the way to the last, etc.* 

The same must be thought regarding the **Great World** <note>original: "Magno Rerum orbe," referring to the Macrocosm or the entirety of the universe.</note>: in which you will find nothing that does not move and is moved, change and is changed, act and be acted upon <note>original: "agat & patiatur," a standard philosophical phrasing referring to the relationship between an active agent and a passive recipient.</note>. 

Accordingly, with sound judgment, **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: "Pansophia"</note> undertakes the consideration of all things as a whole, so that it may be most clearly revealed how the smaller are subordinate to the larger, the larger to the largest, the earlier to the later, and the later to the last; the Infinite to the finite, and the finite to the One. That is to say, it reveals how all **Visible things** are subordinate to Man, temporal things to eternity, and the created to the **Creator** <note>original: "creata Creatori"</note>; and how they should be so ordered. Nothing of all things is to be left behind that is not compelled to serve our ultimate end. By this...

XXX. However, no less care and concern—indeed, rather greater—must be given to **Truth** than to **Universality**. From this it follows that it is better not to know than to know poorly; because **Ignorance** is less harmful than **Error**, just as a neutral state of health is more tolerable than a bad one, and unrefined manners are better than those polished for craftiness and malice. <meta>This page continues the transition from the previous page's final sentence. The text moves from the necessity of "Universality" to the necessity of "Truth," arguing that a small amount of certain knowledge is superior to a vast amount of uncertain opinion. ->### PLAN<- 
<note>original: "Diatyposis," a Greek-derived term used by Comenius to describe the foundational outline or "general design" of his universal wisdom project.</note>

...how desirable it is to have certain and infallible standards for the **true** and the **good**! For although our knowledge may be more limited if we pursue only those things that are certain: yet that very small amount, which is of proven **Truth** and yields infallible use, will be worth more than all the most widespread opinions and empty delights of the imagination. Because it is infinitely better to know one thing than to have opinions about infinite things. And better for a sick man is one little drink that relieves him of his illness than infinite, most subtle discourses about the disease, or any remedies whatever, sought out and administered with however much display, which nonetheless do not shift the disease from its place. Rightly, indeed, does **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose works bridged classical philosophy and Christianity.</note> say: *Any truth whatsoever is better than all that can be imagined at will.* <note>original Latin: "Melius est qualecunque verum, quàm omne id quod pro arbitrio fingi potest." From his treatise "On True Religion" (De Vera Religione), chapter 5.</note> Since, therefore, the wiser among men recognize, confess, grieve over, and deplore the mixture of **Errors** with **Truth** in **Philosophy** and **Theology**, and everywhere else: **Pansophy** <note>The "Universal Wisdom" system proposed by Comenius, which sought to harmonize all human knowledge under a single divine framework.</note>, which aims to gather only those things that are irrefutably **True**, and to demonstrate them for secure possession...

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...demonstratively (apodicticè)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that a small amount of certain, practical truth is superior to a vast expanse of mere opinion or theoretical speculation. It positions Pansophy as a method for filtering out errors in philosophy and theology to find irrefutable facts.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophy, Truth vs. Opinion, St. Augustine, Certainty, Philosophy, Theology, Infallibility, Scientific Method</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
**PANSOPHIAE**
<meta>This page continues the argument for "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophy) as a practical and certain science. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on seeking truth over mere opinion. The word "practicè" (practically) begins at the end of the previous page.</meta>

...to demonstrate in a practical way, he seeks a thing that will certainly be most beneficial to the human race. For what is it, I ask, why we should wish to be perpetually deceived by uncertainties, however plausibly they are put together? **Doctrines** <term>Dogmata: The established principles or teachings of a school of thought.</term> created to please are like the feasts of **Heliogabalus** <note>Elagabalus or Heliogabalus (r. 218–222 AD), a Roman Emperor infamous for his decadence; the author references a story where he served guests "foods" made of wood, wax, or stone, or merely painted on a surface to mock their hunger.</note>, who delighted his guests with painted meals, then sent them away hungry. That feast is the best where the guests are fed with healthy and nourishing foods, even if they are few, rather than one where the palate is irritated and ensnared by varied and contradictory dishes. 

In the first case, a moderate pleasure is followed by good **concoction** <term>concoctio: In early modern science, this referred to the "cooking" or initial breakdown of food in the stomach.</term>, good sleep, good **digestion** <term>digestio: The process of the body absorbing and distributing nutrients.</term>, a healthy complexion, and a good condition of both body and soul. In the second case, however, there is surfeit, heavy sleep, delirious dreams, vomiting, or stomach pains, diseases, and deaths. 

And just as a portrait of a person is not considered good because it is beautifully painted, but because it exactly represents the living features, whatever they may be; likewise, a **Geographical Map** is not good because it is adorned with colors and pictures (such things only move children), but because it represents the true and genuine distances of places...

It emphasizes that the goal of Pansophy is utility and truth rather than mere aesthetic or intellectual pleasure.</summary>

...displays [its features], it is recommended: just so, the Book that feigns or paints nothing merely to please, but represents all things exactly as they are in themselves, will be the best. That **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: From the Greek roots for "all" and "wisdom." Comenius’s lifelong project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system that reflects God's order.</term> might become such a thing—a perpetual map of Truth—is precisely what we seek.

XXXI. But in truth, there will also be a need for the **light of Method** <term>Methodus: The systematic arrangement of information to ensure it is logical, easy to teach, and easy to learn.</term>, by which alone there is hope that all things, being truly handed down, may be both pleasantly read and easily understood. Through this, let this Book become a healthy lure for gifted minds, and a ladder happily erected for climbing more easily to the heights of knowledge; and finally, an effective remedy for removing the greater part of the world's disagreements. It must be shown that these three things can be expected from the **Pansophic Method**, so that it may be clear that we are laboring for something truly useful to the human race.

XXXII. It is futile to hope that human affairs, which have collapsed under the weight of universal errors, can be restored without the universal help of all. # UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original header: PANSOPHIAE</note>

...that **all people** do whatever they do only by reason, those who are subjects as well as those who are leaders. However, they cannot begin to do so before they have been taught to know the reasons how and why it is necessary for each thing to happen. Once all these things have been sketched out according to the standards of **Truth** itself, so that they may be desired and known by everyone, there will be a need for certain enticements: specifically, a **Method** <term>Methodus: The systematic arrangement of information to ensure it is logical, easy to teach, and easy to learn.</term> that is gentle and everywhere "mixes the useful with the sweet," <note>original: "utile dulci miſcente" — A famous phrase from the Roman poet Horace, suggesting that the best teaching both instructs and delights.</note> exposing everything to clear light. By this Method, this **Amphitheater of the Universe** <note>original: "Univerſitatis hoc Amphitheatrum" — In the 17th century, "amphitheater" or "theater" was often used as a title for books that provided a comprehensive view of a subject.</note> will seem to no one a labyrinth or a thicket of thorns, but to everyone a garden of delights. But **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: From the Greek roots for "all" and "wisdom." Comenius’s lifelong project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system that reflects God's order.</term> proposes this for itself: to unfold the whole universe of things before the eyes of all in such a way that it is a pleasure to contemplate each thing in itself, and a necessity to extend one's desire from some things to others.

XXXIII. This same skillful Method promises to ensure that whoever wishes to read these things may also be able to understand them at the same time. This is certainly a most desirable thing. For the common paths of the **Sciences** <term>Scientiarum: At this time, "science" referred to any organized body of knowledge or branch of learning, rather than just the natural sciences.</term> are overgrown with thorns and rough patches; common...

### OUTLINE
<note>original header: DIATYPOSIS — A Greek term used by Comenius meaning a "sketch," "outline," or "vivid description" of his philosophical system.</note>

...complaints, and the examples of the very few who finally struggle out of their difficulties, bear witness. For they <note>Referring to traditional teachers or authors of the time.</note> pass most things on in such a way that it is not so much the **loftiness** of the subjects, or their **subtlety**, but rather the tangled and obscure **manner of teaching** itself that entangles the mind. 

Therefore, if **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: From the Greek for "all-wisdom." Comenius's project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system that reflects the divine order of the universe.</term> seeks shortcuts and strategies—and with God's help finds them—by which everything is tempered in a certain proportion to the capacity of **average minds**, why should it not be believed to propose a matter of great public utility? 

For it unfolds everything according to the very **order of things**, so that each thing stands in its own place, just as it follows from what went before and gives birth to what follows from itself. In this way, it is impossible for all the preceding parts not to bring light to those that follow, and for the following parts not to add strength to those that came before. 

If this work is woven together from beginning to end with equal diligence and success, it will be impossible for all things not to be equally grasped by the mind. Indeed, there is no tower so high, nor any cliff so inaccessible, that anyone provided with feet cannot reach its summit, provided you set up a steady ladder or carve out the proper steps:

# UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: PANSOPHIÆ</note>

...since without someone to guide the attempt, he would sooner find precipices than grasp the heights he ignorantly reaches for. Thus, whoever approaches the study of things with no order, or a crooked one, easily suffers dizziness <note>original: vertiginem; a metaphor for the mental confusion caused by poor teaching methods.</note> and falls back; or he slips into confused, backward, and monstrous opinions. But whoever proceeds by the proper order and step-by-step can easily raise himself to the very highest points.

XXXIV. Finally, it is notorious that disagreements, lawsuits, and quarrels reign everywhere and disturb human affairs. Nor is it unknown to the wiser sort that these contradictions and battles come from diverse and contrary pursuits, because we do not learn to think the same things, do the same things, or speak the same things; instead, we whisper different things to one another, or each of us is carried here and there by our own impulse. If, therefore, it could be achieved that all people—just as they were made by the One God in His image, destined for one end of a blessed eternity with God, sent into the same **School of the present life**, and provided with (or at least capable of being provided with) the same requirements of **necessary supports** <term>subsidia: This refers to the tools, books, and resources necessary for education and survival.</term>—should likewise be guided in common by...

-># PLAN OF THE WORK<-
<note>original: DIATYPOSIS; a Greek-derived term referring to a sketch, outline, or general description of a project.</note>

...a common principle, through common means, toward common ends, and if they allow themselves to be guided by the hand, why should we not hope for more serious study regarding serious matters, and less noise about trifles? Through this, we might expect more harmony and fewer disagreements. When **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> desires, seeks, and attempts this, it surely seeks a result that will be collectively profitable to the human race.

XXXV. But let us look at the specifics, and briefly touch upon what hope Universal Wisdom offers for improving the condition of the *Common People* as well as the *Learned*; the *Statesman* and the *Churchman*; and finally, all the *nations of the whole world*.

XXXVI. Does everyone not see what matters occupy the *Common People*? They are busy with frivolous and vain things, or certainly external things that extend only to this fleeting life. They do not care for more solid goods—those that are internal and eternal—because they do not know them. Furthermore, they do not know them because they have never learned them; therefore, they delight in their own trifles and trash <note>original: tricis & quisquiliis; implying worthless or insignificant matters that distract from higher learning.</note>. For what else should a person do who has not learned the internal **Cultivation of the Soul** <term>Animæ cultura: The idea that the mind or soul must be "tilled" like a field through education to produce virtue.</term>, and has not tasted the sweeter **fruits of the Virtues**?

### UNIVERSAL WISDOM

### XXXVII.

...has tasted, he has not looked upon the more precious **treasures of Wisdom**, nor has he been admitted in any way to the secrets of Heaven and Earth: unless it be to sweat over tilling the soil, gathering harvests, scraping together meager coins, and poking into the business of his neighbors? For it is not in **Human Nature** to remain entirely idle or to grow sluggish; it desires to be busy, or it finds business for itself. He who does not do one thing must necessarily do another; and he who does not direct the natural impulse of his heart toward better things will surely slide toward the worse. 

It would therefore be a beneficial undertaking if even the **Common People** <note>original: Vulgus; referring here to the general public or those without formal education who are often excluded from higher learning.</note> were at last invited into the shared amphitheater of God's wisdom. With the veil pulled away from the **Reality of Things** in the sight of everyone, the divine treasures could be shown to them as they truly are, and as they should be valued according to the truth of the matter. Thus, having caught the scent of better and truer goods, they might learn to value the passing lures of the world less, and to rank the shadows of things below the Things themselves. Finally, even those of the lowest station—men condemned to toil and hardship—might grow accustomed to soothing their miserable lot with the hope of better things.

<term>Sapientia: Wisdom</term>, <term>Pansophia: Universal Wisdom</term>, <term>Natura humana: Human nature</term>, <term>Vulgus: Common people</term>, <term>Thesauros: Treasures</term>

<summary>This page argues that all people, including the laboring classes, should be granted access to "Universal Wisdom" to elevate their minds above mundane chores and gossip. By understanding the true nature of the world, even those in the most difficult circumstances can find hope and spiritual fulfillment.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the benefits of "Universal Wisdom" for the common people to its specific impact on the "Learned"—the scholars and theologians of the era. # OUTLINE

### XXXVII.

As far as the status of the **Learned** <note>original: Eruditorum; referring to the scholars, academics, and clergy of the time.</note> is concerned, a rightly established **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia; Comenius’s concept of a unified system of all knowledge.</note> will be able to benefit them in various ways.

### XXXVIII.

First, it is certain that among those who either pride themselves on the name of **Learning** <note>original: Eruditionis</note> or are held in high regard by others, very few rely on their own foundation or on the foundation of the things themselves. Instead, they follow the opinions of one another; and since these opinions diverge both from each other and from the **Reality of Things**, the result is that there is no end to the hesitations, contradictions, and conflicts within the Church and the school. By these disputes, the intellects of the learned and the consciences of the unlearned are endlessly tormented. 

If a remedy equal to this evil is sought, it can be nothing else than that all should be taught to follow—not the leaden <note>In this context, "leaden" refers to something soft, easily bent, or unreliable, like human opinion.</note> rule of this or that teacher—but the adamantine <note>Meaning diamond-like; representing an unbreakable, unchangeable, and objective standard.</note> rule of God and of the things themselves. Through this, they may learn not merely to hold opinions, but to truly know. This will be achieved not by chasing after or settling for the external appearance of things <note>original: verisimilitudo; literally "truth-likeness" or probability.</note>, but by searching out the most intimate, solid, and immovable **Truth** of things through reliable methods, and by placing what has been unearthed and solidly demonstrated in the open. This is precisely what **Universal Wisdom** seeks to achieve.

->**UNIVERSAL WISDOM**<-

### XXXIX.

Next, if we consider those who study the **Arts and Sciences** <note>original: Artium & Scientiarum; in the 17th century, "Arts" referred to the liberal arts like grammar and logic, while "Sciences" referred to structured bodies of knowledge.</note>, several observers correctly note that the **diversity of methods** <note>original: varietatem illam methodorum</note>—whereby every Science, Art, and Language is taught differently from the next, and even the same subject is taught one way by this author and another way by that one—is not only a delay to their intellects, but a **cross and a torment** <note>A vivid metaphor common in this era to describe something that causes great mental anguish or exhausting labor.</note>.

The result is that everyone progresses with great difficulty. Indeed, many students, having developed a literal "nausea" for their studies and being deterred by so many new "crosses" constantly appearing, leap away from the course of their studies into other ways of life. Others, exhausted by such a "rough and rocky" path, lose their eagerness; they leave many subjects entirely untouched or merely scratch the surface. 

For indeed, **Herculean minds** <note>A reference to the mythological hero Hercules; Comenius suggests that only those with legendary strength and endurance can survive the current chaotic education system.</note> are rare—those for whom a new courage returns to their hearts and a new vigor arises whenever they encounter a new "monster" or an imminent new struggle! And this is why those who are **solidly Learned** are so rare. Who, therefore, would not think it useful and even necessary that this obstacle be removed? 

For the **scholars of Basel** <note>original: Basileenses; likely referring to the influential circle of Hebraists and theologians in Basel, Switzerland, such as the Buxtorf family.</note> correctly stated (in their **Discourse** <note>original: Diatribâ</note> on the shortcut method for learning the **Hebrew** and **Chaldean** <note>term: Chaldean; in this period, "Chaldean" was the standard term for the Aramaic language, particularly the portions found in the Bible.</note> languages, in the year—)

<summary>Comenius argues that the lack of a unified teaching method creates an exhausting "torment" for students, leading many to abandon their studies. He suggests that "Universal Wisdom" is necessary to provide a consistent, simplified path to knowledge that does not require "Herculean" effort.</summary>

->**OUTLINE OF THE WORK**<-
<note>original: DIATYPOSIS; Comenius uses this Greek term to describe the structural "plan" or "delineation" of his project.</note>

...in the year 1637, joined to the *Four-Language Mercury* <note>original: Mercurio quadrilingui; likely referring to the *Janua Linguarum Reserata*, a famous language textbook by Comenius published in multiple languages.</note>) they write: *It cannot be stated enough how great a shortcut of labor and boredom it is to be able to use the same author, the same instructions, and the same method everywhere.* And yet, **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> will be nothing other than such a shortcut for learning everything through one continuous, perpetual method.

### XL.

And because there is no shortage of those who—though burning with love for the **Sciences and Wisdom**—are nevertheless distracted by the business and occupations of life, and cannot find the time to search through **Libraries**; these people absolutely need some kind of summary "provisions for the journey" <note>original: viatico; usually referring to money or supplies for a trip, but here used metaphorically for a portable handbook of knowledge.</note> regarding their education, but one that is robust. 

By the strength of this provision—just as **Elijah** <note>A biblical reference to 1 Kings 19:5-8, where the prophet Elijah is fed by an angel in the wilderness.</note>, refreshed by a morsel of bread and a drink of water through the ministry of an Angel, proceeded through the desert all the way to the mountain of God—they too may be able to preserve their spiritual life amidst whatever noise of business surrounds them. 

Indeed, this is exactly what **Universal Wisdom** strives for: a way for the idle to be usefully occupied, and for the busy to be swiftly advanced toward their primary goals and ends. For it will teach them to attend to and act upon the "first things first" and the "more important things more urgently" <note>original: priora prius, & potiora potius; a rhythmic Latin phrase emphasizing logical priority and efficiency.</note>: with a certain [knowledge] of those...

### XLI.

Again, those who sail through the vast seas of *Authors* <note>original: Authorum; referring to the massive body of classical and contemporary literature that students were expected to navigate.</note> have a need—just as those tossing about in the wilderness of the Ocean—for some sort of magnetic guide <note>original: magnetico directorio; a metaphor for a compass.</note>, by which they can better protect themselves from errors, sandbars, and reefs. 

For just as before the discovery of the use of the **Magnetic needle**, those sailing the seas could only know where they were being carried, and where the prow should be directed, when the sky was clear or the shore was in sight; but when the sky was cloudy and they were driven into the deep, they were left without a plan. In the same way, those who enter the sea of Authors without being equipped with any rules (except for the common "sun" of common notions <note>original: communium notitiarum; a philosophical term for basic, self-evident truths or "common sense," which Comenius suggests are often obscured by confusion.</note>—which is often clouded over—and the "shores" of preconceived doctrines) cannot help but be uncertain at every turn and anxious about the outcome. 

Therefore, because **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> strives to establish a universal guide of this kind—one which, if kept at hand, allows a person to disregard all depths, nights, and darkness, being certain of the world's cardinal points and secure in the harbor toward which he travels—it aims to establish (namely, a universal...

# A SKETCH
<note>original: DIATYPOSIS; a Greek term used to describe a draft, outline, or formal description of a work.</note>

### XLII. 

If we look specifically at *Philosophers* (who, being focused on the investigation of all things and the <unclear>causes</unclear> by which they are held together, ought to be the masters of everything that is done rationally anywhere), we will find that their condition, above all, is in need of improvement. For whatever philosophy the schools currently possess and cling to so tightly has been drawn from the Greeks and Arabs <note>In the 17th century, "Arabs" referred to the great Islamic scholars like Avicenna and Averroes, whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to European university education.</note> (Pagans and Muslims); and therefore, although it has been freed from the superstitions once mixed into it, it remains nonetheless greatly imperfect. 

There are three certain proofs of this. First, because those thinkers—not being instructed by divine Revelation (by which they might have corrected the errors of the Senses and of Reason)—could not help but be blind or stumble in almost all the most important matters. For they were ignorant of the world's beginning, and ignorant of its end; they knew neither the makeup of human Nature, nor its corruption, nor its restoration, nor the method by which that restoration is achieved. What, then, can they offer that is healthy or...

<summary>Comenius argues that current philosophy is flawed because it relies on non-Christian Greek and Arabic sources which lack the "divine revelation" necessary to correct human error. He asserts that without understanding the world's origins and human nature's corruption and recovery, classical philosophy remains "blind" to the most important truths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of classical philosophy, specifically addressing why Greek thought (Socrates, Plato, Aristotle) is insufficient for a complete "Pansophia" or Universal Wisdom. It argues that while the "mechanical arts" (technology) progress, philosophy has remained stagnant and argumentative.</meta>

# PANSOPHIA

...regarding Nature, or regarding Morals, could they hand down? Second, that Greek Philosophy, at the time when it was first established (namely, in the age of Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle), was a new thing in the world, <unclear>la</unclear>cking sufficient practical experience. It snatched up whatever was at hand, taking one thing from this person and something else from another; and everything was then diverted into professional rivalries and disputes. In these, they entangled one another—and continue to entangle their followers to this day—leading human intellects away from the Things themselves toward themselves and their own private concepts. They ensnare minds as if in shackles, so that they are unable to observe Reality freely. Finally, the matter itself proves that this pagan <note>original: *gentilitiæ*; referring to non-Christian, ethnic religions of antiquity</note> philosophy lacked, and still lacks, true and living roots. Had it been nourished by such roots, it would have grown into an ever-expanding tree, bringing forth more and more abundant fruit. We see that this is how it ought to have happened by the example of the mechanical arts <term>mechanical arts: the practical sciences and crafts, such as engineering, clockmaking, or metallurgy</term>, which, as if endowed with a vital spirit, grow and are perfected every day. Indeed, if their first inventors were to come back to life, they would find it necessary to become students themselves, so crude were their first attempts compared to the level to which those who followed...

<meta>catchword: followed-</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that Greek philosophy is flawed because it was built on insufficient experience and devolved into intellectual "shackles" of circular disputes, unlike the mechanical arts which show true progress.</summary>

# DELINEATION
<note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek term meaning a sketch, outline, or structural description of a plan</note>

...with skill, they have advanced to such perfection.

The opposite, however, is what we see in the **Philosophical Sciences**: they flourished most under their first Authors, but thereafter they languished. It has come to the point that modern thinkers consider it enough if they can even follow the footsteps of the ancients from afar. This is openly admitted by those who heap such praise upon the author of this philosophy, **Aristotle**, that they grant him a virtual dictatorship over wisdom—the very Empire and Tribunal of thought—never daring to stray from his path.

Thus it happens that for so many centuries, the sciences have remained stuck, motionless, in the footprints of the men of old. They have gained no growth worthy of the human race; like statues, they are worshiped, but never moved forward. Indeed, if anything new has been discovered in the meantime—such as the **Science of Magnetism**, **Algebra**, **Logarithms**, **Sine Tables** <note>original: *Canones sinuum*; mathematical tables used for trigonometry</note>, **Clocks**, the **Art of printing books**, and so forth—it was certainly not discovered through the guidance of that ancient Philosophy. Rather, these things came about either by chance or because more excellent minds, by penetrating "further beyond" <note>original: *plus ultrà*; a likely reference to the motto of progress and discovery popularized by Francis Bacon</note>, dared to attempt something of their own.

Would that those who devote themselves to the contemplation of things might be persuaded that regarding the principles...

<meta>catchword: regarding-</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that while technology and practical arts progress through experimentation, academic philosophy has stagnated due to a dogmatic and "statue-like" worship of Aristotle. He lists modern inventions like the printing press and logarithms as proof that discovery happens outside of traditional Aristotelian methods.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, philosophy vs technology, progress, magnetism, algebra, logarithms, printing press, trigonometry, plus ultra, scientific stagnation</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 41, urging thinkers to stop relying solely on ancient texts and instead investigate the world directly. It uses the metaphor of "exhausted mines" to describe the state of traditional philosophy.</meta>

# PANSOPHY
<note>original: *Pansophiæ*; the genitive form of Pansophia, or "All-Wisdom," referring to Comenius's project to unify all human knowledge</note>

...provided only that, being rightly disposed, they bring their minds to investigate the hidden **Truth** of things in the things themselves, rather than in books or the concepts of others. Oh, how much hope shines forth that ever-richer treasures of the **Wisdom of God** might be unearthed! These treasures still lie hidden, and will remain hidden, if men persist in being satisfied with what has already been discovered and remain tethered only to the thoughts of others. 

For what new thing, I ask you, can he find who keeps himself only to those paths where countless others perpetually travel? What gold can he extract who binds himself to a vein already exhausted by others down to the very crumbs? Or who re-smelts metallic earth, already smelted a thousand times, in the hope of still drawing something out? 

It is therefore in the interest of **Philosophers** that, leaving behind those exhausted mines, they open for themselves new paths to investigate the treasures of **Nature** and **Art** <note>original: *Ars*; in this context, it refers to human ingenuity, technology, or "the mechanical arts"</note>. Since **Pansophy** both teaches this and, with God’s help, largely achieves it, no good or wise man who has the improvement of human affairs at heart should feel inclined to envy it, much less to hinder it.

**XLIII.** Those among the learned who devote themselves to the investigation of things, and who [examine] **dogmas**...

<meta>catchword: ami- (likely for "amice," meaning in a friendly or companionable manner)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that true wisdom is found by investigating "things themselves" rather than old books, comparing traditional philosophy to a gold mine that has been worked until nothing remains.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophy, Wisdom of God, Truth, Nature, Art, experimental philosophy, progress, mining metaphor, dogmas</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 42, transitioning from a critique of "exhausted" philosophical traditions to a specific critique of the argumentative and polemical methods used by the scholars of the time.</meta>

->**SKETCH** 43<-
<note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek-derived term meaning an outline or schematic description, used here as the title of Comenius’s plan for universal wisdom</note>

...ing <note>Completing the word *examinando* ("by examining") from the catchword of the previous page</note> them, set up disputes, the matter speaks for itself that they have need of better aids. For what result is produced that is worthy of such great efforts as those with which **polemical books** <note>original: *Libri polemici*; works written specifically to argue against opposing religious or philosophical viewpoints</note> have been written until now? They dispute for the sake of disputing: not by ending lawsuits, but by establishing and increasing them. For those accustomed disputes are mere circles, a work without an exit: as is shown by the continuation of opinions and sects clashing in our own century. All are attacked, but none are overcome: some marvel at the obstinacy of others, yet no one yields. 

What is the cause? First, **controversies** <note>original: *Controversiae*</note> are stirred up without necessity, out of a mere itch of the intellect and a lust for novelty or for contradicting others; this distracts, entangles, and complicates minds on both sides. Then, once stirred up, controversies are exaggerated beyond measure: any slight disagreement is treated as a **heresy** <note>original: *haeresi*; a formal accusation of deviation from established religious doctrine</note>, which offends, alienates, and irritates the spirit. Finally, the very method of **untying knots** <note>original: *enodandi*; a metaphor for solving complex logical or theological problems</note> which we have used thus far is ineffective. Specifically, we use it to draw out our own conclusions and to strike down opposing ones,

<meta>catchword: prin- (likely for *principia*, meaning "principles")</meta>

<summary>Comenius critiques the "endless circles" of contemporary academic and religious debates, arguing that current polemical methods only increase division rather than finding truth. He identifies the root causes of these failures as a desire for novelty, the over-exaggeration of minor differences into "heresy," and ineffective logical methods.</summary>

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: *Pansophiae*</note>

...[to] **principles** <note>The text continues from the previous page, likely completing a thought about relying on faulty foundations.</note> (**Canons and Rules**; **Distinctions and Limitations**) snatched from wherever, or even recently fabricated by ourselves—acting as hypotheses to serve our own causes—or certainly not yet granted by the opposing side. When an adversary sees himself attacked by such things, he either laughs or treats them with contempt. 

Then, from these—as well as from true principles granted by the adversary—**Conclusions** are for the most part drawn out not by the art of **Logic**, but by the art of **Rhetoric** <note>original: *Rhetoricâ arte*; here referring to the use of persuasive speech to win an argument rather than using strict logical proof to find the truth</note>: that is to say, not by a solid **demonstration** <note>original: *demonstratione*; in a formal sense, a proof that is logically certain</note> by which agreement is wrung from a person whether they are willing or not (as happens among **Mathematicians**), but by certain persuasions that invite agreement without necessarily forcing it. 

Finally, the end of a **Disputation** <note>original: *Disputationis*; a formal academic debate</note> usually corresponds to its beginning, so that it ends in prejudice and emotion: while those who disagree are condemned, and hatred of them is instilled in others. This gives the opponent the opportunity to shield himself with a similar buckler, and to protect himself against emotion with emotion, and against hatred with stubbornness. To these evils, however, **Pansophy** <note>original: *Pansophia*; Comenius’s proposed system of "Universal Wisdom" intended to unify all knowledge into a single, harmonious whole</note> promises a remedy: while it teaches that only solid things should be handled solidly...

<meta>catchword: cet:</meta>

<summary>Comenius critiques the circular and emotional nature of contemporary intellectual debates, contrasting their rhetorical "persuasions" with the absolute certainty of mathematics. He introduces Pansophy as the corrective method that will rely only on "solid" and universally accepted principles.</summary>

<keywords>Logic, Rhetoric, Mathematics, Disputation, Pansophy, Scholasticism, Demonstration, Certainty</keywords>

# OUTLINE
<note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*; a Greek term used by Comenius to mean a "general plan" or "sketch" of his work</note>

...[This method] <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing the description of how Universal Wisdom functions compared to the polemics mentioned previously.</note> acts so that it places no burden of prejudice upon anyone, but rather invites everyone on an equal footing. By establishing **principles** that are truly universal, truly true, and truly clear—which all will admit of their own accord and with pleasure—it leads their minds step by step, without any gaps and within perpetual boundaries, slowly to the very **summits of Truth** <note>original: *Veritatis fastigia*</note>. It does this not by forcing those who are unwilling, but by leading those who are spontaneous and willing. 

And so, while it does not embitter souls but rather soothes them, it shows ways of bringing even the opinions of those who contradict each other (provided they contain something true and rational) into **consensus** <note>original: *consensum*; a core goal of Comenius’s work was "irenicism," or the pursuit of peace and agreement between warring religious and philosophical factions</note>. It ensures that all people, being carried from the same principles through the same means to the same conclusions, may assent to the final points as much as to the first. This is achieved not because of some persuasive likelihood, but because of the very clarity of the unshaken truth itself.

*XLIV. Likewise, if we wish for the well-being of our Schools—as we certainly ought—<term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>original: *Pansophia*</note> will also be able to contribute significant effort toward that goal. Specifically, because it is in the interest of **Youth** that, from the very beginning of life, they be formed by right...*

### UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: *PANSOPHIÆ*</note>

...and to be imbued with healthy convictions that grow along with them. This can hardly be achieved by any better or more certain means than if a book is at hand, common to all schools, which sets forth all beneficial things in a single, continuous method. Under its guidance, nothing that pertains to the useful cultivation of minds would ever be passed over, omitted, or neglected; instead, all things could be carried out and kept consistent everywhere. For even if not all youth are capable of grasping everything, teachers will nevertheless find a standard there, so that they neither ignore nor forget the goal toward which all things must be directed. Furthermore, certain summaries of <term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>The Latin *Pansophia* refers to Comenius's project to organize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note>, adapted to the grasp of early childhood, can easily be established for the use of lower schools.
