### OUTLINE
<note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*, a Greek term meaning a sketch, draft, or descriptive model.</note>

...saying, nothing is truer, if true **Philosophy**—which is the true contemplation of all things—is understood. For since the foundation and bond of the safety of the **State** <note>original: *Reipublicæ*; literally the "Republic" or "Common Wealth," referring to any organized political community.</note> (just as it is of all things) is **Order**, which governors watch over to maintain among all people and things: it is evident that the better someone understands the logic of order, the better they can lead in achieving that order.

Moreover, he understands the order of things best who understands it **universally**, just as it is the "soul" of all things, and who knows how to distinguish **fundamentals** from **secondary details** (where the former are necessarily the same everywhere, while the latter vary). Now, **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: *Pansophia*</note> aims precisely at this: to reveal **Order** as the foundation and bond of all things. Therefore, it will be able to produce good governors for Republics.

But it is also of great importance that those who are subjects should be submissive not by compulsion, but of their own accord, out of a love for justice. Why should this same **Universal Wisdom** not be able to provide this, if the common people themselves are also admitted to it, so they may learn and understand that the public safety of all, and the private safety of each individual, [consists] in...

XLVI. **If you look at the Church**, Universal Wisdom <note>original: *Pansophia*</note> will also be able to bring, with God’s help, no small momentum to its safety and its growth. For it is altogether good for all **Theologians** to understand how exactly the **Words of God** <note>Scripture</note> agree everywhere with the **Works of God** <note>the physical world and nature</note>, so that they may know how to better clarify the mysteries of salvation through the mysteries of common providence. 

It is also useful for the Christian people to possess a faith that is not commanded or extorted in the manner of the Pharisees, nor an "implicit" faith <term>Implicit faith, or "the coal-miner’s faith" (*fides carbonaria*), refers to a blind belief where a person accepts doctrines without understanding them, simply because the Church says they are true.</term>, but rather a faith that is certain and explicit. This will happen if they are taught to understand that which they believe, and to know that our **Faith** not only contains nothing absurd (as unbelievers suspect, or even blasphemously claim), but is actually a matter of all...

<note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*; a Greek term for a draft, outline, or descriptive sketch of a project.</note>

...slaves to opinion, are so pleased with themselves that they believe they are all but **Solomons** <note>A reference to King Solomon, the biblical figure traditionally regarded as the wisest of all men.</note>; and they do not seek truer learning, because they dream that they already possess it, and that there is nothing further left for them to strive toward. Hence it is that in this "learned age," there is a great scarcity of the truly learned: while common studies have devolved almost entirely into a kind of chatter and **sophistry** <note>original: *Sophisticationem*; here referring to the use of clever but fallacious arguments or empty intellectual posturing.</note>. It would therefore be beneficial for these opinion-mongers to be led out into the **Ocean of Universal Wisdom** <note>original: *Universariæ Sapientiæ Oceanum*; a central Pansophic metaphor representing the vast, interconnected totality of knowledge.</note>, and for the vast deserts of human ignorance to be shown to them through the discovered and mapped shores of **unknown lands** <note>original: *Terrarum incognitarum*; Comenius uses the imagery of the Age of Discovery to suggest there are still vast "continents" of knowledge yet to be explored by the human mind.</note>. Thus, realizing how much more there is that we do not know compared to what we do know, they may grow accustomed not to **raise their crests** <note>A Latin idiom meaning to act with pride or arrogance, like a rooster displaying its comb.</note>, but to lower them.

LI. But if men can be liberated from this **presumption** <note>original: *Præsumtione*; the overconfidence in one's own limited knowledge.</note> and brought back to a due modesty and an ardent zeal for true wisdom, it will simultaneously be necessary for the matter to be organized a little differently than it has been until now: namely, so that whoever is truly hungry for it may be able to advance toward true learning by more certain and more **concise** <note>original: *compendiosis*; referring to efficient methods or "shortcuts" that allow for faster and more comprehensive education.</note> paths. For those ways which hither—

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM</header>

...[methods] we have had until now are full of detours and are uncertain—indeed, they are dangerous. This is because in this "learned age," as people wish it to be called, **Books**, **Schools**, **Methods**, and various **Opinions** on various subjects have multiplied to a staggering degree. Students are utterly confounded; it is to be feared that we may all be overwhelmed, so that in the end we either read nothing or believe nothing, unless a barrier is placed against this era—an age as lush in conceiving new opinions as it is in multiplying books. Students must be freed from the weariness of reading so many things, from the tumult of conflicting opinions, and from the crossroads—or rather the "many-roads"—of error encountered everywhere. Anyone who truly perceives the true purpose of this work will recognize and admit that this can be achieved in no other way than through a **Pansophic** <note>original: *Pansophicâ*; referring to "Universal Wisdom," the author's project to synthesize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note> approach. Namely, that all things should be derived from a foundation that cannot deceive—that is, from the **Things themselves**, from **Divine Testimonies** about those things, and from the **Notions inscribed in our Minds** (in which three, as if in its triple palace, eternal truth resides). All things should flow into our minds by such a method that is easy and moves through a clear light.

# OUTLINE
<note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek term often used in titles to mean a draft, sketch, or general description of a plan.</note>

**52.** This same work can simultaneously serve as a remedy for **human curiosity** <note>original: *Curiositati humanæ*; in the 17th century, "curiosity" was often viewed as a vice if it led people to seek "forbidden" or useless knowledge rather than practical wisdom.</note>, by which some burn uncontrollably with a disordered desire for knowledge regarding things that are less necessary or forbidden. It achieves this by designating the legitimate boundaries for human industry and by discovering the goals and limits of the power granted to the human mind over **Reality** <note>original: *Res*; literally "Things," referring to the objective world as opposed to mere words.</note>. Finally, it uncovers and places in a clear light all the modes of possibility and impossibility, so far as they can be known. Thus, those who are occupied with things that are unbecoming, useless, or impossible may be called back from a vain and irrational impulse into the paths of reason, and others may be taught to avoid similar opportunities for straying.

**53.** *Dissensions and battles—indeed, even sects—have also grown too strong in Philosophy and Theology*, in which the learned and the unlearned alike are entangled. They have reached a point where they either do not understand one another or, even though they could, they neglect to understand because of partisan zeal. This situation is the source of various confusions. For as long as we do not understand one another’s **Words**, it follows that we do not understand **Things** <note>original: *Res*</note> either, except in a confused and incorrect way; and while some [attribute] absurdities to others im-

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM
->PANSOPHIA<-

...we depict [errors in others], we ourselves are either seen, or found, to cherish far greater ones; nor is there anyone who can effectively settle these disputes, so long as we do not build for the common good. Instead, having fallen into factions, each person is busy only with fortifying his own positions and undermining those of others by whatever tunnels <note>original: *cuniculis*; a metaphor for secret undermining or siege warfare tactics.</note> he can dig. By this very process, **sects** are not diminished, but become fixed; **dissensions** are not removed, but multiplied; **hatreds** are not calmed, but sharpened, without end. 

O, would that God might therefore have mercy on us and gather us back from this dispersion, and restore to us (as He promised through the **Prophets** <note>Refers specifically to biblical passages like Jeremiah 32:39 and Zephaniah 3:9 which promise a restoration of unity and a "pure speech" to the people.</note>, and as I have already touched upon) *one heart, and one way, and a chosen lip; so that we all may think the same things, speak the same things, and do the same things, and serve the Lord with one arm.* <note>original: *cor unum, & viam unam, labium-que electum... brachio uno*; a composite of biblical phrases emphasizing total unity in spirit, language, and action. "Serving with one arm" implies a unified, shoulder-to-shoulder effort.</note> 

Moreover, if this is to be hoped for just as it is desired, it can be obtained by hardly any other way than the **Pansophic** <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century concept of "Universal Wisdom" that aimed to synthesize all scientific, philosophical, and theological knowledge into one harmonious system to end human conflict.</term> way. Specifically, if the minds of all men are led out into the open field of **Reality** <note>original: *Rerum ipsarum*; literally "the things themselves," emphasizing objective truth over subjective interpretation.</note> itself, and there, setting aside prejudices, they may freely contemplate not **opinions** about things, but the things themselves: with no other guide than **Sense** <note>original: *Sensu*; here referring to the "senses" or empirical observation as the primary way to understand the world.</note>, and no other...

# OUTLINE
<note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek-derived term used in Latin to mean a sketch, model, or foundational description of a system.</note>

By the light of sound reason, with no other arbiter and judge than God. For then, because **Things** <term>Res: In this context, "Things" refers to objective reality or the natural world as created by God, which the author believes should be the foundation of all knowledge.</term> are the same for everyone, and everyone possesses the same **Senses**, and the same **Reason** which judges similar things in a similar way, and finally since the same God witnesses the same truths about those same things: why should we despair that there will one day be the same perception, assent, and consensus among all people regarding those same things?

And once we have agreed upon the **Things** themselves, **Words** will not be able to distract or pull us apart so much: for it will no longer be Words, nor our own private concepts, but the **Things themselves** (which are the same for all) that will be the basis of our thoughts and our Discourses. It can be provided, with the help of GOD, that the **Thing**, and the understanding of the Thing, and the **Speech** <note>original: *Sermo*; refers here to structured discourse or the linguistic expression of thought.</note> which interprets that understanding, run parallel to one another through the entire Universe.

And then disagreements, and the triggers for those disagreements, will be happily removed, with no one intent on anything other than the truth of the things themselves. Then contradictions will cease, as the appearances of conflict vanish, and there will be no need for those common excuses <note>original: *subterfugiis*; literally "subterfuges" or "evasions."</note>: *This is true philosophically, but not theologically;* <note>The author is criticizing the "Double Truth" theory, a medieval and early modern idea that allowed for a proposition to be scientifically or philosophically "true" while being religiously "false."</note> *I speak astronomically, not*

*Physically*, etc: nor will anyone in Philosophy call themselves a *Platonist* or an *Aristotelian*, nor in Theology a *Lutheran*, or a *Calvinist*, or a *Papist* <note>original: *Pontificium*; referring to those loyal to the Pope, or Roman Catholics, in the context of the intense religious divisions of the 17th century.</note>, but all will be *Philosophers* and *Christians*. For just as Christ is common to all, so too *Paul*, and *Cephas* <note>original: *Cephas*; the Aramaic name for the Apostle Peter.</note>, and *Apollos* <note>This is a reference to the New Testament (1 Corinthians 1:12), where the Apostle Paul rebukes the early church for dividing themselves into factions based on different teachers.</note>, and *Plato*, and *Aristotle*, and whoever has any truth or goodness anywhere—it shall be ours in common, drawn from the common Treasury of truth. Thus, who does not see that the *Sciences* and *Arts* among themselves, and *Philosophy* with *Theology*, and the minds and tongues of men—though they disagree in their opinions—can be reconciled to one another through the *intervention of the Things themselves* <term>Rerum ipsarum interventu: The idea that objective reality, created by God, acts as a neutral "mediator" that can settle human arguments.</term>? Who, therefore, would not even wish for this? He is a savage enemy who, when he could achieve victory peacefully, prefers a bloody one. We are therefore savage if, when the way of peace and harmony shows itself, we prefer wars without end.

Namely: the foul confusion of religions, the horrendous flood of crimes, and the unchecked license of intellects. 

Therefore, if the glory of God moves us, if the security of our faith and hope is dear to our hearts, and if compassion for our perishing neighbors affects us, it is only right that we think again and again about remedies. <page-num>64</page-num>

# PANSOPHIA

...perish, rather than that some *universal Restoration of Minds* <note>original: "Instauratio animorum." This term refers to a spiritual and intellectual renewal, a concept famously championed by Francis Bacon but here applied to the internal state of humanity.</note> should occur: and toward this, likewise, a universal **Peace and concord** should be spread, as it were, over the whole human race. 

By peace and concord, however, I mean not so much 그 external variety between Princes and peoples (which is a slippery thing and changeable for slight reasons), but rather an internal peace of Minds, by reason of the Judgments and Opinions occupying those minds. From such a peace—if it could be obtained—the human race would rightly have much to promise itself. For just as opinions regarding what things are to be pursued or avoided occupy our minds, so too do they stir our emotions, whether turbulent or calm. And if these opinions are the same, they give birth to the same interests, desires, and efforts, toward either good or evil, according to the nature of the opinions themselves. 

Therefore, we would hope in vain that the minds of Men could be brought over to good and calm emotions in any other way than through the kindled study of piety, humanity, and wisdom. Wise antiquity beautifully expressed this in that fictional narrative *about the theater of Orpheus* <note>In Greek mythology, the music of Orpheus was so harmonious that it could tame wild beasts and make trees and rocks move. Here, it serves as a metaphor for how "Pansophia" (universal wisdom) can harmonize the conflicting natures of humanity.</note>: where various Beasts and Birds were gathered together, [forgetting] their innate appetites...

In the 17th century, this referred generally to stringed instruments like the lyre or harp, symbolizing the "music" of divine order.</note>. Whenever its sound ceased, the living creatures immediately returned to their wild nature. By this fable <note>original: *Apologus*</note>, the ancients taught that human beings—who are by nature most greedy for gain, pleasure, and vengeance—will cultivate peace and society among themselves for as long as they lend their ears to the precepts of Religion and Wisdom. But if these voices fall silent, they slip back into seditions, tumults, and savagery.

Nor does Holy Scripture teach otherwise. It records that the turbulent spirit of the wicked *Saul* <note>King Saul of Israel, who according to the Book of Samuel (16:23), was refreshed and made well when David played the harp.</note> was calmed in no other way than by the lyre of *David*; and similarly, that the spirit of the pious *Elisha* <note>The prophet Elisha, who in 2 Kings 3:15 requested a harpist to play so that he might receive a divine vision.</note>, when stirred by zeal, could only be recalled to tranquility through a similar harmonic concert.

Therefore, for a world in turmoil to return to its senses, what does it need but some harmonic "Lyre"? And because these seditions have not merely occupied a few neighboring regions, but have seized the entire world—so that all the kingdoms of the Christians, and whatever remains of the non-believing <note>original: *infidelium*</note> nations, rise up against one another, and the East is collided against the West,

LVI. <page-num>67</page-num>

# ->DESIGN<-
<note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*; an outline or structural description of a work.</note>

...all the monuments of antiquity, unearthed from the darkness, have been shared with the intellects of our age; and through the discovered mode of Navigation, the commerce of all the nations of the World is happily beginning. From which, through the rivalry of intellects stirred up by both these means, a great labor has begun to be expended in tracking down new inventions during the most recent decades. <note>original: *lustris*—a period of five years; here referring to the recent periods of rapid intellectual growth.</note> But even that has been done almost more by the private industry of certain individuals than for the common good.

What, therefore, forbids us to try, if somehow the observations of the whole World (both those already made and those yet to be made) and the inventions (both those already found and those yet to be found) might finally become common to all? Namely, so that all who have been admitted, and are yet to be admitted, into the **amphitheater of God’s wisdom** <note>A metaphor common in 17th-century thought, viewing the natural world as a stage where God’s work is performed for humanity to observe.</note> may be taught to pay attention to what is happening, and to stimulate one another with mutual eagerness toward observing always more and greater things. For the wisdom of God will never fail in presenting His "plays" across His own world. 

This must be attempted with the blessing of God, the protector of our affairs: for it concerns His glory that the plays which His wisdom presents in His **Theater**, as...

<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM</header>

...men desiring **progress** for the better, who are driven by a certain hidden and noble instinct to act so that, no matter how scattered the sciences and arts may be, they may reduce them into **summaries** <term>original: *compendia*; concise abridgments of larger works designed for quick learning</term>, free them from obstacles and thorns, and, by applying a skillful method, make them attractive to as many people as possible, thereby making men more learned through such concise knowledge. We are urging the creation of one single thing out of all of these—something even more general, more common, and better suited to universal ends than all those others. LXI. **However, we must respond** to the objections of certain people who do not correctly perceive the goals and methods of our **Universal Wisdom** <term>original: *Pansophici*; referring to the "Pansophia" project, a systematic organization of all human knowledge</term> proposal, lest they be able to cast a shadow of doubt <note>literally "spread a mist or cloud" (*nebulam*)</note> over the work.

LXII. **Someone might say** that these great efforts <note>original: *molimenta*; implying a massive or monumental undertaking</note> involve much strangeness and savor of recklessness <note>original: *temeritatem*; boldness or rashness</note>.

**Response.** To think about, desire, and strive for the **reform** <note>original: *emendationem*; the improvement or correction of the world</note> of human affairs is not so strange; for ever since the beginning of our confusion, **God**—and following God’s example, all good and wise men—have been accustomed to do that very thing, as necessity required, as opportunities invited, or as the matter itself allowed. How, therefore, can that which is done following such great authors be accused of recklessness? And if it is not reckless to behold the works of God; to observe the traces of the **wisdom of God**, which disposes all things sweetly <note>a reference to the Book of Wisdom 8:1, a favorite concept of the author regarding how God's order is both powerful and gentle</note> toward their proper ends; and finally to arrange all of one's own affairs according to the **will of God** (for God commands all those things, reproaching those who do otherwise for their dullness):

...now adding stubbornness to our **rashness**. **65.** They say: *“Nevertheless, the title of **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: πανσοφίας (*pansophias*)</note> is presumptuous. It is not suitable for human knowledge, regardless of how far that knowledge extends; it should be left to God alone.”* 

**Response:** We will not argue over a name once the substance of the matter is agreed upon. However, we believe there is no reason why anyone should be rightly offended. We do not attribute something to man in order to take it away from God; rather, we do so that the **glory of God** may shine all the more in Man, who is His **image**. <page-num>78</page-num>

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: *PANSOPHIÆ*</note>

...describes him as **wise** (1 Timothy 1:17; Ecclesiastes 1:7), why should the addition of the mark of **Universality** cause such offense? Indeed, our reasons for putting this term forward are no less significant than those of **Pythagoras** when he changed the word **wisdom** into **philosophy**. <note>original: *sophias* into *philosophiam*. Pythagoras famously refused the title "wise man" (sophist), preferring "lover of wisdom" (philosopher) to show humility before God.</note> It is said that he had two considerations in mind: First, **God**, for whom he wished that glorious title to be reserved alone, so that He alone might be called **Wise**. <note>original: *sophos*</note> Second, the **Sophists** and **Triflers**, who by abusing the word counted themselves among the Wise. He, therefore, desiring to call men back from the mere reputation of wisdom to the actual study of wisdom, added the mark of "study"—**philosopher**. <note>original: *philo-sophos*, literally a "lover of wisdom."</note> 

As for us, the reasons why we believe another mark, that of **Universality** (namely, that prefix **pan-**), should be placed before the word are different, but based on similar concerns: *The neglect of wisdom, and the abuse of the terms "philosophy," "theosophy," and others commonly accepted.* <note>The author argues that existing labels for knowledge have become degraded or too narrow.</note> 

First, I say, because most mortals are so sluggish that, while they over-confidently leave the title and the reality of wisdom to God, they themselves become like beasts and act in all things rashly, tastelessly, and stupidly. Furthermore, those who *do* wish to be wise seek wisdom only in fragments, focusing on certain particular objects of study, while the whole of wisdom—

<meta>Catchword: [of] wisdom (entiæ)</meta>

<summary>This page justifies the term "Pansophia" by comparing it to Pythagoras's coining of "philosophy." The author argues that adding "universal" (pan-) is necessary to combat the modern tendency to either ignore wisdom entirely or study it only in disconnected fragments.</summary>

# OUTLINE
<note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*</note>

<sig>D 4</sig>

...ignorant and sluggish regarding the embrace of wisdom; or they seek to be wise without God, for they neither aim toward God nor conform themselves and their affairs to His standards. They must, therefore, be called back to the laws and norms of the All-Wise God—without whom there is no essence, no intellect, and no wisdom, and nothing is true or coherent. This is so that all people, being knowledgeable in all things (and especially in divine things), may be made truly like God, as far as is possible. 

If, then, a method of Science <note>original: *Scientiæ modus*</note> has been discovered by which **ALL PEOPLE** are taught to be wise concerning **ALL THINGS**, and in **ALL WAYS** <note>original Greek: *pantes, panta, pantōs*</note>, why should it not deserve to be called a kind of human <term>Pansophia|Universal Wisdom</term>? This follows the pattern of terms already accepted, where a more complete preparation of a thing is expressed by adding the prefix "all" (*pan*): as seen in *panoply* <note>original: *panoplia*, a complete set of armor</note>, *panstratia* <note>original: *panstratía*, a full military mobilization</note>, *panacea* <note>original: *panakeía*, a universal remedy</note>, *pandects* <note>original: *pándektai*, a complete body of laws or writings</note>, *pantheon* <note>original: *pántheion*, a temple to all the gods</note>, and so on. 

To this is added the abuse of the name "philosophy" <note>Greek: *philosophia*</note> as opposed to "theosophy" <note>Greek: *theosophia*, or divine wisdom</note> or "theology," and vice versa. These are commonly distinguished as if the one had no business with the other, which is a clear injury to the Truth. Where, therefore, these are joined together, they are taught... <note>The text continues on the next page; the catchword "tur-" completes the Latin word "docentur" or "taught."</note>

<summary>Comenius justifies the term "Pansophia" by arguing that true knowledge must be universal and rooted in God, reuniting the often-separated fields of philosophy and theology.</summary>

<page-num>80</page-num>

<header>UNIVERSAL WISDOM</header>

...and so that humans—as *Augustine* says—may both *philosophize in sacred matters, and be consecrated in Philosophy* <note>original: *in sacris philosophari, & in Philosophia consecrari*</note>, the name is rightly and deservedly changed, so that the subject does not lack its own suitable label. Finally, if the name *Pansophia* <term>Pansophia|Universal Wisdom</term> should entirely displease the Church, let it be changed to something else, or even its opposite, so that instead of *Omniscience* <note>original: *Omniscentia*, all-knowing</note>, we might say *Nulliscience* <term>Nulliscience|No-knowledge</term>. For we must eventually arrive at this point: if we have established a true *Pansophia* (which brings even opposites into a single identity), then the more a person sees, the more defects they notice—both their own and those of others—according to that wisest witness in *Ecclesiastes 1:15* <note>The author likely refers to the idea that "he who increases knowledge increases sorrow," or the realization of what is lacking.</note>.

### LXVI.
They also object: by calling *Pansophia* a kind of ladder to God, are we transforming the work of converting humans—which belongs to God alone—into a mere art or craft? The answer is: as if it were truly absurd either to add external aids to the internal operation of the Holy Spirit, or to use a certain prudence and skill in administering these aids! On the contrary, it is the one who denies this who is being absurd. For God ordinarily acts through means; among which means are humans themselves—whom God [uses] as...

<meta>catchword: admi- (likely "administers" or "instruments")</meta>

<summary>This page defends the name "Pansophia" by arguing that true wisdom leads to humility and "nulliscience," while also asserting that human systems of learning are necessary tools (or "ladders") that the Holy Spirit uses for human conversion.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Augustine, Nulliscience, Ecclesiastes, Holy Spirit, divine grace, educational methodology, humility in learning</keywords>

<header>OUTLINE <note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*, a Greek term often used for a sketch, plan, or descriptive outline of a work.</note></header>

<sig>D 5</sig>

...servants, or fellow-workers <note>original: *cooperatores*</note> (1 Corinthians 3:9), as well as human effort, which is guided by the art of method. For this reason, when God promises a beautiful reformation of the Church, He promises *Shepherds, who will feed the people with knowledge and understanding* (Jeremiah 3:15) and *Teachers with a learned tongue, knowing how to speak a word in season* (Isaiah 50:4). 

Furthermore, the practice of the Prophets, the Apostles, and Christ Himself shows that they did not usually approach those they wished to awaken, move, convert, or convince without certain strategies <note>original: *strategematis*, referring to tactical or skillful planning</note>. Thus, it becomes clear that this work consists not only of diligence and labor, but also of a specific prudence and skill. Therefore, if we take all these elements, gather them into one, and adapt them for ready use through the benefit of a systematic Method so that they may serve God—what are we doing that is not inherently pious and just?

### LXVII.
They say: *There are already enough books, and what progress is being made? The world remains as it is, and the world will remain.* 

**Response:** Let [piety] teach us never to despair, never to stop hoping for better things, and never to stop providing opportunities and aids...

<meta>catchword: us [nos]</meta>

<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: *PANSOPHIÆ*</note></header>

...us, the patience and kindness of God, which is always renewing itself and revealing itself in more and more glorious ways. Indeed, He promises an increase of <term>Knowledge</term> <note>original: *Scientiæ*; here referring to a systematic, provable understanding of the world</note> in the final times and light at the very evening of the world (Daniel 12:4; Zechariah 14:7). Therefore, let us seek this increased knowledge—as far as God wills to work through us—by reforming not only books but also people, training them in the skillful use of books. This is what <term>Pansophia</term> <note>Universal Wisdom</note> will accomplish, for it teaches not only all other things but also the universal use of itself.

### LXVIII.

*But how can the same book serve such different people? For different things help different individuals; some things are suitable for the learned, others for the unlearned; some for the faithful, and others for the unfaithful.*

**Response:** The <term>Alchemists</term> <note>original: *Chymici*</note> praise their <term>fifth essence</term> <note>original: *quintam essentiam*, or quintessence; in early modern science, this was a purified substance believed to contain the medicinal power of all elements combined</note> because it is of such an exquisite balance that, when applied, it provides exactly what is needed for each individual: cooling for those who are hot, warmth for those who are cold; moisture for the dry, and dryness for the moist. Because of this, it becomes a ready remedy for every disease. Something similar can be said about this Book, if it is properly prepared

<meta>catchword: prepared [nato]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that God's plan involves a late-stage increase in human knowledge and that "Pansophia" acts as a universal intellectual "quintessence" capable of benefiting all people regardless of their background or faith. It emphasizes the transformation of the learner's skill, not just the production of new texts.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, alchemy, quintessence, biblical prophecy, Daniel 12:4, Zechariah 14:7, education reform, medicine, knowledge</keywords>

<page-num>83</page-num>

<header>DESIGN <note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*; a Greek term for a sketch, outline, or descriptive presentation of a subject.</note></header>

...prepared, it will be permitted to pronounce (as that which will be a kind of <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: *quinta essentia*; in alchemy, the purest, most concentrated essence of a substance</note> of Books) that it will benefit everyone, with its own fruit returning to each person. For because of a fitting balance of simplicity with sublimity, and of truth with <term>precision</term> <note>original: *ἀκριβείᾳ* (akribeia); refers to exactness or rigorous detail</note>, it will come to pass that it commends itself to the simple by the simplicity of its Method, while feeding the learned with the sublimity of its Subject Matter. And since nothing is more reasonable than clearly demonstrating True Faith, it will provide delights to the faithful, while disposing the unfaithful toward faith—or at least ensuring they are without excuse.

### LXIX.

However, at this point, someone might object:
> *Those who appeal to Reason in matters of Faith are heretics. Therefore, Pansophia, attempting the same thing, is rightly viewed with suspicion.*

**Response:** If that were so, then what of *Justin Martyr*, *Athenagoras*, *Lactantius*, *Ludovicus Vives*, *Mornay*, *Grotius*, <note>A list of famous Christian apologists and scholars from the early Church through the Renaissance who used logical arguments to defend the faith.</note> and those others who furthermore fought for the Faith against the unfaithful using reasons? For different people can do the same thing, yet it is not the same thing if they do it with a different purpose and in a different manner; and that is the case here. The heretic applies Reason to divine testimonies as a **judge**; the <term>Pansophist</term> <note>One who seeks "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia)</note> applies it as a **witness**...

<sig>D 6</sig>

<meta>catchword: witness [original: *testem*]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Pansophia is a "quintessence" of books, balancing simplicity for the unlearned with depth for the scholar. It also defends the use of Reason in matters of Faith, distinguishing the "Pansophist" use of logic as a witness from the "heretical" use of logic as a judge.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Reason vs Faith, Quintessence, Apologetics, Justin Martyr, Hugo Grotius, Logic, Witness vs Judge</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the heretic and the Pansophist regarding their use of Reason and Faith, concluding with a defense against the inevitability of disagreement.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>

<header>OF PANSOPHIA</header>

<meta>catchword: blind (original: cæcis)</meta>

Likewise: the former <note>referring to the Heretic mentioned on the previous page</note> commands Reason to go before and mark out the paths along which he drags divine Revelation; the latter <note>the Pansophist</note> wants God to go before, and desires for Reason to follow. Therefore, the Heretic uses Reason to overturn the dogmas of Faith, while the Pansophist uses it to establish them. The former pits the Creature against the Creator; the latter subordinates the one to the other. The former teaches Reason to speak against Faith; the latter makes Reason speak for Faith. 

His goal is that those who do not admit the testimonies of God (*the Atheists and the unfaithful*), or those who corrupt them with their own perverse reasonings (*the Heretics, the Sophists, and the Smatterers* <note>original: *Scioli*; those with a superficial or "smattering" of knowledge who pretend to be experts</note>), might be constrained by the force of their own reason. Thus, they may be led to a point where they are compelled to stand for God and His truth even against themselves—that is, to acquiesce to the Word of God rather than fighting against it. In short, *our God is worthy that the mouths and hearts of all men should bear witness—and be compelled to bear witness—to His own word.* <note>original: *cujus ori attestentur... omnium hominum ora*; a play on "mouth" meaning both God's speech/revelation and man's testimony.</note> This is what Pansophia seeks.

### LXX.

"Do you hope, then," they say, "that there will be no contradictions? For heresies will always exist." 

**Response:** I believe that the impious will always be *rebels against the light* <note>original: *lumini rebelles*</note> as long as the world exists. (Job 24:13). Yet shall we therefore neglect to light lamps because...

Therefore, it is fitting to overcome whatever darkness we can struggle against through the power of light; although the prince of darkness will never lack his own opportunities to cast gloom even over a new light. For even that very thing, Christ, the Light of the World, will turn into an opportunity for uncovering a new light once again; as it is His custom to transform all the efforts of His enemies—even their very rages—into an occasion for manifesting His own glory (Psalm 16:10) <note>While the text cites Psalm 16:10, the concept of God turning the "wrath of man" to His praise is more famously found in Psalm 76:10; in the 17th century, biblical citations occasionally followed different numbering or memory</note>. LXXI. Finally, there are those who object to our insignificance, saying: *“How few are you, who dare to set such great things in motion?”* 

**Response:** We must not look at how few we are who give this advice, but how great He is in whose name we advise; and the things themselves that we advise; and those for whose sake we advise. He in whose name we dare to exhort mortals to seek a <term>universal remedy <note>original: *remedium universale*; a central theme in Comenius's work, suggesting that a unified system of education and wisdom could cure the world’s religious and political conflicts.</note></term> for their confusions is **God**, the Lord of all, who is **ready to confirm His mercy upon all and to establish His Truth forever.** He is the one for whom it is possible even to open the mouth of a ***donkey*** <note>A reference to the story of Balaam’s ass in Numbers 22:28, where God gives speech to a beast of burden to rebuke a prophet.</note>; or to stir up stones to cry out, if others should remain silent <note>A reference to Luke 19:40.</note>. It is His custom to use contemptible means, so that the glory may be His, and not the glory of those who—even if they seem to be someone of importance when considered on their own—forget they are merely instruments in the hand of God. The matter itself, which we wish to see promoted, is the ***glory of God*** <note>original: *gloria Dei*</note>, which must be made as clear as possible through all people and all things here under heaven: so that from the rising of the sun even to its setting, from this moment...

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: *PANSOPHIÆ*; the genitive form of *Pansophia*, the author’s term for a complete, integrated system of all human and divine knowledge.</note>

<warning>Small brown stain on the center of the page, slightly obscuring the Psalm reference.</warning>

> *Send forth, O Lord, Your **LIGHT**, and Your **TRUTH**! let them lead us, and bring us back, to Your **HOLY MOUNTAIN**, into Your dwellings.*

(Psalm 43:3) From these very words, we repeat over and over three essential requirements for <term>Universal Wisdom <note>original: *Pansophiæ*; a concept popular in the 17th century that sought to organize all knowledge into a single, harmonious framework reflecting the mind of God.</note></term>. Namely, that there be observed:

1. *The **LIGHT** of Method, by which the minds of all people may be led through all things without stumbling, and without any obscurities or rugged paths <note>original: *salebris*; literally "potholes" or "ruts." The author argues that learning should be a smooth road rather than a difficult struggle.</note>.*
2. *The **TRUTH** of Doctrine, so that we may take care that nothing of vanity mingles in, neither in the whole, nor in any part.*
3. *The subordination of all things to the **Ultimate End**, which is the dwelling together of **GOD** on His holy **MOUNTAIN**.*

# J. A. L. TO THE READER

->Greeting.<- <note>original: *S.*; an abbreviation for *Salutem*, a standard Latin greeting wishing the reader health or prosperity.</note>

Most kind Reader, when I read these words in Sacred History: *David gave to Solomon the description of the Porch, and of the Temple, and of the Storehouses, and of the Upper Room, and of the Inner Chambers, and of the House of the Mercy Seat, as well as all the Courts and Side-rooms he had planned for the surroundings, for the Treasuries of the House of the Lord, and for all the Vessels of service: All these things (he said) have come to me in writing by the hand of the Lord, so that I may understand all the works of the MODEL.* (1 Chronicles 28:11, 12, 13, and 19.) <note>original: *Exemplaris*; in this context, it refers to a divine architectural blueprint or pattern.</note> From this, I obser—

<margin>1. First.</margin> First, *that consultations regarding matters must altogether be placed before the matters themselves.*

2. Second, *nor should these consultations be merely extemporaneous,* <note>original: *extemporales*; meaning "done without preparation." The author argues that complex projects require deep, premeditated planning rather than impulsive action.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author uses the biblical account of David providing Solomon with divine plans for the Temple to justify the necessity of a rigorous, pre-planned "model" for his own system of universal knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Biblical precedent, 1 Chronicles 28, architectural metaphor, divine blueprint, systematic planning, King David, King Solomon</keywords>

...not only, but far preceding the deeds themselves: just as David was weighing the building of the Temple of Jerusalem in his plans for twenty-odd years (even though the entire work was material <note>original: *materiale*; referring to physical construction as opposed to spiritual or intellectual "building."</note>); but Solomon, his son and successor, although most highly equipped with every preparation and with unusual wisdom, did not set about it until the fourth year of his reign: so that about thirty years passed in deliberations and in the gathering of necessities. <margin>3.</margin> Third, that consultations about weightier matters ought not to be superficial, but penetrating all the way to the innermost and smallest details of the Works. <margin>4.</margin> Fourth, that all those things can not only be thought out in advance, but also described and sketched: so that the entire works of the Model <note>original: *Exemplaris*; David’s divine blueprint mentioned in 1 Chronicles.</note> (as David says) may be better understood. <margin>5.</margin> Fifth, Ideas

*of things, that those are the best which come from God himself. For behold, Solomon, although the wisest of mortals, is not permitted to fashion the form of the Temple by his own whim! ->DESIGN OF THE UNIVERSAL TEMPLE<-

### I.

With those words by which the wisest Solomon closed his work, which he composed on the Vanities of the World, it is our pleasure to begin the outline of this Work, which we hope will be an antidote to those very Worldly Vanities: *Of making many books there is no end, and much reading is a weariness of the flesh. The conclusion of the matter is: Fear God, and keep his commandments: for THIS IS THE WHOLE OF MAN. For God will bring every work into judgment, including every hidden thing; whether it be good, or whether it be evil.* <note>original: *Faciendi Libros multos nullus est finis...* This is the famous conclusion to the biblical Book of Ecclesiastes, specifically 12:12–14. Solomon was traditionally regarded as the author of Ecclesiastes, a book exploring the futility of human endeavor apart from God.</note>

II. Here, because he counts among the vanities of mortals the "itch" both for writing many books (which he calls a matter of infinite labor) and for reading them (which he calls a weariness of the flesh), he gives advice to keep our eyes on the highest aim of all Life. Thus, before all things and after all things, those matters should be sought and performed through which we are advanced in the fear of God, and toward those things which—after this life is finished...

### III.

I say that we desire a book to be built that might stand as one in place of all others, a most true treasury <note>original: *repertorium*, meaning a comprehensive index or storehouse of information</note> of all divine and human wisdom. In this book, all things should be presented *Easily*, such that they could not be easier; *Briefly*, such that they could not be briefer; and yet *Sufficiently*, such that they could not be more sufficient. Through all things that exist and can be known, the minds of all people should be led back to Him *from whom, through whom, and for whom* all things exist <note>original: *Ex quo, Per quem, & Propter quem*. This is a common theological formula, derived from Romans 11:36, characterizing God as the origin, the sustainer, and the final purpose of the universe.</note>—He who is both the beginning and the end of our wisdom. ### IV.
If we approach our sacred Philosophy with this end in mind, it will happen at the same time that all lower things—through which, as if by a raised ladder, we elevate ourselves toward that sublime One who inhabits His own eternity—shall not indeed hold us fast within themselves; yet they will delight, nourish, and in their own way satisfy us more and better than they do those whom they currently hold captive. For if the vastness of our kingdom, **of the universe** <note>original: *τοῦ παντός* (tou pantos)</note>, be presented in its entirety to our minds with skillful **Brevity**: and if minds are introduced into the possession of that same kingdom with a skillful **Ease** <note>original: *Facilitate*</note> that unlocks the reasons for all things: and finally, if through a skillful **Solidity** of treatment it is achieved that the minds of men—away from the infinite, winding, and wandering labyrinths of opinions and preoccupations—are bound to the simple and perpetual principles of **the One, the True, and the Good** <note>original: *Unius, Veri, Boni*. These are the "transcendental" properties of being in scholastic philosophy, representing the ultimate goals of the intellect and will.</note>: and in that way

### VII.
Therefore, in imitation of this, we place before all else the **four hinges** <note>original: "cardines"</note> of the **Temple of Wisdom**, toward which all its parts shall look: **COMPLETENESS**, **TRUTH**, **EASE**, and **METHOD**, all executed with precision.

### VIII.
Indeed, certain critics of philosophy seem to have observed that these four qualities are, as it were, cardinal points in the structure of human science. For those who list the advantages of **Peripatetic Philosophy** <term>Peripatetic Philosophy: The school of thought following Aristotle, so named because he often taught while walking through the "peripatos" (covered walkways) of the Lyceum</term>, boast of these four things:

1. *That no other philosopher possesses a greater abundance of materials and subjects than **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC), the Greek philosopher whose logical and scientific works were the standard curriculum in European universities for centuries.</note> has in his books.*
2. *That he investigated the reasons and causes of things.*
3. *That he employed an elegant, well-rounded, clear, and effective style of speech.*
4. *That, finally, he observed **Method** <term>Methodus: the systematic arrangement of ideas to ensure logical flow and ease of learning</term> more strictly than his predecessors.*

(Thus **Alsted** <note>Johann Heinrich Alsted (1588–1638), a German polymath and professor whose *Encyclopaedia* (1630) was a major precursor to the pansophic movement.</note> has it in his *Encyclopedia*, book 4, chapter 8). If these qualities are pleasing in Aristotle or any other good author (and they cannot fail to please clear minds), they must certainly be observed all the more... <note>The text breaks here at the catchword "magis" (more).</note>

### IX.
Therefore, the **Fullness of Things and Materials** will be the first **hinge** <note>original: "cardo"</note> of **Pansophia** <term>Pansophia: A "Universal Wisdom" that seeks to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</term>, upon which all its components shall first rest, both in the whole and in every individual part. In the whole, so that here the entire universe of things may be viewed: namely, the **World** <note>original: "Mundus"</note> with all its fullness. And not only as it is now, but also as it was before it existed, and as it will be after it ceases to be; together with its admirable and adorable President, **God**.

For it must be provided for **Pansophia** what **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher and advisor to Nero, whose writings on ethics and nature remained influential in the 17th century.</note> vainly desired for **Philosophy**: *that just as the face of the whole world comes into view, so it might appear to us, a spectacle most similar to the world.* (**Epistle 90**) For what he adds—*But to us, whose sight fails in close proximity, individual things can be more easily shown, since we are* **NOT YET CAPABLE OF THE UNIVERSE**—is testimony that this sagacious man saw, with a [Uni-]versal genius...

->DELINEATION<-

...[the uni]verse must be mastered, but that it was not yet possible. He <note>Referring to Seneca, the Roman philosopher mentioned on the previous page.</note> does not touch upon the causes of this impossibility, perhaps because he did not understand them; to us, however, they are manifest. Specifically, there was a lack of principles: especially of **Divine Revelation** <term>Divine Revelation: Truths communicated by God—specifically through the Bible—that human reason or the physical senses could not discover on their own, such as the origins of the world or the nature of the afterlife.</term>, by whose help those things which are beyond the senses—both those that existed before human memory and those that will exist after us—could have become known. Then, too, there was a scarcity of **Sensory Observations** <note>original: "observationum Sensualium"</note> from the ages that came before. 

But since God has supplied that lack of principles to us through His Word, and since the observations of experiments continued through so many centuries have increased our storehouses of knowledge, why should we not now have hope of grasping the capacity of the entire **Universe**? Therefore, let us no longer contemplate it only in fragments, but in its full scope, seeing how all things flow out from eternity and flow back into eternity, through paths and reasons adapted to the laws of eternity itself. Let us reveal this to the World, if we can, so that men may begin to be wise not piecemeal <note>original: "frustulatim"</note>, but from the whole: filling the entire area of the mind with the complete solidity of all teachings.

XIV. We completely avoid—as if they were dangerous reefs—those obscure and tangled terms of what are called <term>second notions<note>In traditional scholastic logic, "second notions" are abstract concepts that refer to other concepts (like "predicate" or "genus") rather than to real-world objects (like "tree" or "man").</note></term>. Almost all books of common Philosophy and Theology are not merely filled with these, but are stiff and bristling with them. For why is it necessary for me to speak in such a way that no one, or only a very few, can understand, if I am able to speak so that everyone understands? And why shouldn't I be able? I will provide an example. Someone wishing to describe the duty of <term>Logic<note>original: "Logica," the study of reasoning and argument.</note></term> accurately writes as follows:

*Logic’s subject is that of information, of treatment, and of use. The subject of information is the <term>Innate Talent<note>original: "Ingenium," referring to the natural capacity or character of the human mind.</note></term>; primarily indeed **Reason**, but secondarily partly **Memory** and partly **Speech**. The subject of treatment is the manner of arguing well. It is otherwise called the Subject of the art, and also of doctrine and the internal. The subject of use is being and non-being, which is otherwise called the Subject of the craftsman and the external, etc.*

Who, I ask you, understands these things, unless they have become accustomed to these thorns through several years of practice? If I were to say: *Logic shapes and di-*

XV. I do not wish to depart from this <term>pivotal point<note>original: "cardine." Comenius uses this word to signify the central hinge or crucial part of his argument.</note></term> yet: because there is hope for great progress if the rubbish of those obscure terms—which have been used until now and are deservedly worn out by useless practice—can be cleared away. Wise men certainly congratulated <term>Brahe<note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), a famous Danish astronomer who paved the way for modern astronomy by providing highly accurate observations of the stars and planets.</note></term> because he had removed from Astronomy *Real Orbs, Eccentrics, Epicycles, Deferents, Equants*, and the rest of that useless furniture, teaching the celestial motions through simpler hypotheses. Why then do we not attempt the same for all <term>knowable things<note>original: "scibilia." This refers to everything that is capable of being known or studied.</note></term>? This would remove so many superfluous, obscure, rough, and even monstrous terms. To those approaching the green fields of the sciences, these terms are like thorns through which they must walk; most people, stung by them, either shudder and withdraw, or they walk through only when wounded, their mental concepts torn to pieces. But indeed, if we can do without such things, and explain matters that are plain in themselves with plain words that are intelligible to everyone, what need is there for <term>trifles<note>original: "trica." A term for intricate nonsense or trivial complications.</note></term>? Seneca said it rightly:

<page-num>110</page-num>

# THE TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY

<meta>Catchword: Whom-</meta>

*Just as it is a sign of luxury to desire delicate things, so it is a sign of madness to flee from common things that can be obtained without great cost. Philosophy demands frugality, not punishment.* (Epistle 5.) Therefore, let us either ensure that all <term>knowable things<note>original: "scibilia." This refers to the sum of all knowledge that can be categorized and taught.</note></term> are handed down in such a way that they are grasped by every human intellect promptly, spontaneously, and without an interpreter—or we are doing nothing at all. Because we have taken pity on the **youth**, for whom so many <note>original: "cruces." Literally "crosses," used here as a metaphor for the mental tortures and unnecessary difficulties students face.</note> are fixed everywhere beyond necessity, we are attempting to remove them (or, if usage requires some terms to be retained, to soften them with a suitable placement and a good definition). How correctly we do this, the learned will be able to judge—if only they can do so without prejudice. If, however, we do not obtain their <note>original: "calculos." Literally "pebbles," referring to the ancient practice of using stones to cast a vote or reach a verdict.</note> approval from them, we will appeal to <term>the simple ones<note>original Greek: "τοὺς νηπίους." This refers to "babes" or the "little ones," a biblical allusion to those who lack formal training but possess an open mind.</note></term>, the small children and the unlearned; if they grasp our ideas better, **Truth** herself will rest in their judgments. For it is not a new thing for children to applaud the teacher of eternal Truth while their elders persist in their usual sluggishness. *And this will be the third main point that observers will watch for: whether these works of ours are so clear that they may be understood promptly by anyone?*

<vocab>Philosophy, knowable things, youth, Truth, the simple ones, Seneca</vocab>

<summary>Comenius argues that for education to be effective, it must be presented in simple, accessible language that even children and the unlearned can understand. He cites Seneca to support the idea that philosophy should be practical and frugal rather than an arduous punishment.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophy, simplicity in education, Seneca, clarity, pedagogy, the simple ones, accessible knowledge</keywords>

-># SKETCH<-
<note>original: "DELINEATIO." This serves as a header for the outline of the project.</note>

For this matter (as I have already said) everyone will be a suitable judge; indeed, perhaps the more unrefined and simple a person is, the more suitable they are.

XVI. The fourth hinge of our undertaking, which will provide a wondrous strength to the entire building of <term>Pansophy<note>original: "Pansophia." A term popularized by Jan Amos Comenius referring to a "Universal Wisdom" that integrates all human knowledge, religious and secular, into a single pedagogical framework.</note></term>, is the ***harmony of Method***. We desire this to be so great that those who proceed through these things may feel their own mental <term>concepts<note>original: "Conceptus." In this philosophical context, it refers to the mental representations or ideas formed in the mind.</note></term> to be as interconnected as they see the Things themselves connected in the <term>Universe<note>original: "Universo." Referring to the entire created order or the totality of things.</note></term>. It was long to be wished for that this could finally be achieved by the benefit of Method, lest we always teach in such a way that ***students remain students and teachers remain teachers*** (as <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558) was a prominent Renaissance scholar. This quote suggests a stagnant education system where no real progress is made toward mastery.</note> Scaliger says), or even so that we do not perpetually prolong the disputes and disagreements that have crept in, and never <unclear>ter</unclear>minate them. What we are attempting here—and with what harmony we are arranging to connect Things to Things, and weave them into human minds—must now be set forth. And this will be the fourth thing that observers will principally notice.

XVII. There are for us three highest and essential virtues of the ***Pansophic METHOD***:

-># OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-

1. **COHERENCE** *of all things, perpetually.*
2. **GRADATION** *perpetually.*
3. **UNIFORMITY** *perpetually.*
Through the benefit of these, we hope to achieve that here all things may be taught and learned easily, and with the highest evidence and certainty of Truth—as if it were **mathematical**. <note>Comenius frequently compared his pedagogical method to mathematics because he believed education should follow a logical, undeniable sequence where each step proves the next, much like a geometric proof.</note>

XVIII. Perpetual **COHERENCE** consists in this: that all things, from the greatest to the least, from the first even to the last, will be linked together, and some will depend on others in such a way that nothing of all that exists anywhere can either escape [our system] or appear to be seen in any place other than its own. Hitherto, because <term>Things and Words<note>original: "Res & Verba." A central theme in 17th-century "Realist" education. From these, **Rules** were constructed, and through various <term>Disciplines<note>original: "Disciplinarum." Referring to specific branches of learning or academic subjects.</note></term> (of which the number was neither certain, nor...

# OUTLINE

...order) they were scattered into heaps. But once the **marriage of Things and Words** <note>original: "Connubio Rerum & Verborum." A core principle for Comenius. He argued that students should not learn words in isolation, but in tandem with the physical objects (Things) they represent, ensuring language is rooted in reality.</note> has been found and established through the intervening chains of legitimate Concepts—provided that those Concepts, being accurately and precisely abstracted from **Things**, then accurately and precisely imprint themselves upon **Words**—it will be impossible for anything to escape being brought into order.

XIX. This is precisely what is sought: that just as all things that exist, and can be thought or spoken of, constitute one **World** <note>original: "Mundus." In the 17th century, this referred to the "Cosmos" or the entire ordered universe.</note> in its own fullness, connecting itself on every side; so too should the things we think and say about that whole **universality of Things** be nothing other than a single **system** of our **science**. This system must be undivided in itself, connecting its parts together on every side, so that there is no need to omit anything nor to repeat anything twice, and every single thing may be seen standing properly in no other place than its own. Only then will this work become most like the World itself: a world from which nothing can be removed without causing ruin, or at least a shameful gap, and to which nothing can be added or moved without creating a noticeable monstrosity. And just as it was once said that it was [impossible] for a verse to be stolen from **Homer**... <note>original: "Et quod olim Homero versum subtrahi posse im-". XX. Such a **chaining together of Things and Concepts** can be achieved if one traverses the **Universality of Things** <term>Universality of Things (Universitatem Rerum): The totality of everything that exists in the created world</term> through **continuous analysis** <note>original: "Analysis perpetuâ." A method of logical breakdown where a large concept is systematically divided into smaller and smaller sub-categories.</note>. This begins from the first and highest concept of **Being** <note>original: "Entis." In 17th-century philosophy, "Being" is the most general category possible, encompassing anything that exists in any way.</note>, proceeding through all the distinctions of things, down to the final and **indivisible** <note>original: "insectibiles," literally "uncuttable." Comenius is looking for the "atoms" of logic—the points where a concept cannot be divided any further.</note> tips of those differences, as far as they can be noted; in this way, whatever needs to be said about each individual thing is said right there in its proper place.

XXI. However, there is a need for a **completely new Analysis of Things**. Those systems we have used until now are insufficient for this purpose—especially those famous **ten Aristotelian Categories** <note>original: "Prædicamenta." These are the ten classes (such as Substance, Quantity, and Quality) defined by the Greek philosopher Aristotle to organize all possible subjects of a human statement.</note>. It is commonly believed that all things can be reduced to these as the highest "genera" or types of things. Yet, neither Aristotle nor anyone else showed how those broad types could be led down into the most specific species. Consequently, those who contemplate reality have wandered through the universality of Things and Concepts as if in an **infinite Chaos**; and those who...

<summary>Comenius argues that a true system of universal knowledge (Pansophy) requires a new, continuous method of analysis that maps everything from the broadest concept of "Being" down to the smallest detail, moving beyond the limitations of Aristotle's traditional categories.</summary>

<page-num>115</page-num>

# OUTLINE
<note>original: "DELINEATIO." This refers to the outline or "sketch" of Comenius’s plan for universal knowledge.</note>

When they tried to reduce **themes to the Categories** <term>Categories (Prædicamenta): The ten basic classes of being (such as Substance, Quality, and Quantity) defined by Aristotle, which were the standard for organizing knowledge in the 17th century.</term> (as we were ordered to do in the Schools), it either failed to work or happened only with hesitation and force. This serves as proof that the **veins of Things** <note>original: "venas Rerum." A metaphor suggesting that nature has a natural "anatomy" or "grain" that a scholar should follow, much like a butcher follows the joints of an animal.</note> were not correctly identified. This is also proven by the fact that no reason is given there, nor is it self-evident, why so many classes of Things are established—neither more nor fewer—and why they follow each other in that particular order. Yet, if we had learned to correctly identify the **joints of the members**, the **Universe of Things** <term>Universe of Things (Rerum Universitas): The totality of everything that exists.</term> would spontaneously resolve itself into its own limbs. It would then be clear that Things proceed in that specific order and number, and no other.

XXII. Therefore, the task must be attempted **entirely from scratch**. This is necessary despite the fact that **Juan Luis Vives** <note>Ludovicus Vives (1493–1540): A Spanish humanist and educator. Although Comenius admired him, he here challenges Vives's skepticism regarding the possibility of a perfect system of natural science.</note> despaired that anything perfect could be achieved in natural matters, when he wrote:

> *To describe the **orders of forms**, to call them to account in classes, as it were, and to organize them in our own contemplation just as they are organized in **Nature**—this, truly, only **He who created them** can do. For the properties of Things, their powers, and their excellences are unknown to us, unless...*

<summary>Comenius argues that traditional Aristotelian categories are an artificial way to organize knowledge and proposes a new system that follows the "natural anatomy" of the world. He acknowledges the skepticism of the philosopher Juan Luis Vives but insists that a perfect classification of nature is a goal worth pursuing.</summary>

<page-num>116</page-num>
->**OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE**<-
<note>original: "TEMPLI PANSOPHICI." This is the title of the work, referring to Comenius's "Temple of Universal Wisdom."</note>

...perhaps in general. (*Book 1 of First Philosophy*) <note>original: "Lib. 1. de Prima Philof." This likely refers to the "First Philosophy" or Metaphysics of Vives or Aristotle.</note> Let it be so; even if we do not everywhere achieve a full and exact **order of Things**, what forbids it from being established somewhat better? For we are confident that, even if our diligence fails in the arrangement of **Substances** <term>Substances (Substantiæ): In 17th-century philosophy, these are fundamental entities that exist on their own, like a person, an animal, or a stone.</term> according to their forms, we will nonetheless demonstrate a new skill regarding **Accidents** <term>Accidents (Accidentia): The non-essential qualities of a thing, such as its color, weight, or location, which cannot exist without a "substance" to hold them.</term> and other kinds of Beings. In this way, an **analysis of things** <note>original: "Rerum analysis"</note> may be obtained that, if not perfect, is at least very close to perfection.

XXIII. We set forth these conditions for a **Perfect Analysis of Things**:

**First**, that it be entirely universal and exhaustive, so that nothing can ever be encountered that does not find its proper place here among its neighbors.

**Second**, that it not be violent or forced, but rather arrange Things member by member, according to how they spontaneously resolve themselves (with the senses and the intellect never in conflict).

**Third**, that the reasons why there are a certain number of classes of Things—and why no more or fewer can be imagined—be either provided everywhere or be self-evident. This, finally, will bring forth both the **beauty** and the **security** of Universal Science.

<meta>catchword: XXIV.</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that while a perfect classification of all reality is difficult, it is possible to create a "new craftsmanship" for organizing knowledge that follows the natural "joints" of the world. He defines a perfect system as one that is exhaustive, natural rather than forced, and logically self-evident.</summary>

<page-num>117</page-num>

XXIV. Furthermore, just as the World—although in itself one and undivided—is nonetheless clearly distinguished by its parts for the sake of order, so too it will be necessary for **Universal Wisdom** <term>Universal Wisdom (Pansophia): Comenius’s concept of a single, unified system of knowledge that connects all fields of study (theology, nature, and art) into one coherent whole.</term>, although it is one continuous **system**, *to be either divided or distinguished into certain parts, Books, and Chapters.* This, however, is not to be done according to our own whim or by some artificial order, but rather by the sequence which the intention of the ultimate goal itself prescribes (namely, our blessedness in God, to be promoted or illustrated through all things that exist anywhere). If we follow this path, a Three-part Work will emerge, which is further resolved into **seven sections, or Books.**

XXV. For before all else, it is necessary to provide a certain preparation of Minds for that which is being undertaken: what they commonly call a **preliminary theory** <note>original: "προθεωρίαν" (protheorian)</note>, or in Latin, **Fore-knowledge** <note>original: "Præcognita"</note>. Then will follow the **body of Universal Wisdom** itself, presenting the **contemplation** <note>original: "θεωρίαν" (theorian)</note> of all things that exist anywhere. Finally, the **after-theory** <note>original: "ἐπιθεωρία" (epitheoria)</note> will conclude the work, showing the true **Use** of all things that are truly known.

XXVI. Regarding the Preliminary Theory, or Fore-knowledge,

[of the mind], there is a need, because in the production of every work, it is necessary to prepare the material beforehand so that it becomes fit to receive the form. Why then should human beings not also be made teachable before they are taught? Indeed, unless we wish to waste our labor and pour the precious liquid of wisdom into leaky jars <note>original: "pertusis... doliis." This is a classic metaphor for a mind that cannot retain knowledge, often referencing the myth of the Danaids who were punished in the underworld by having to fill leaking vessels.</note>, it is necessary to begin with preparations rather than with the main work itself. That is to say, we must help people to wake from the lethargy common to mortals and to be touched by a concern for those truer and better goods—the things that distinguish us from the beasts. This is what the **benefit** <gloss>benefit <note>original: "ὠφέλεια" (opheleia)</note></gloss> of Universal Wisdom will earnestly strive to achieve.

XXVII. *But the Theory itself will deal with Things themselves*, in the order in which they were produced, continue, and will finally come to an end. This consideration will provide the *five Essential parts of Universal Wisdom*. Namely, because God (1), before He founded the World, held stored in His divine mind the *Ideas of Things* (that is, the principles and forms according to which things were to be formed, and indeed were formed). And then (2), according to those ideas, He constructed the machine of the World <term>Machine of the World (Mundi machina): A common Renaissance and Early Modern term for the universe, viewed as a complex, orderly system designed by a divine architect.</term>, and gave to it that power, which Na- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the word "Nature" on the next page.</note>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>THE OUTLINE</header>

...Nature, He instilled it to continue its motions and operations even without end (if He were to permit it). And because (3) He had concentrated the power of Nature especially in the human being, and that power exerted itself in various ways, so that wonderful works issued from human **Ingenuity** <term>Ingenuity <note>original: "Ingenio humano." This refers to the creative and inventive capacity of the human mind, which the author sees as a unique force capable of producing things that do not exist in the "natural" world, such as art, technology, and culture.</note></term>—works which Nature herself would never have produced, and of which the world is full. 

And because Man, however, (4) in a matter of the greatest importance—the **governance of himself**—wandered most greatly, and through this plunged himself into eternal destruction: God once more reached out His hand and restored man, and to the restored man prescribed new laws. If he keeps these, he escapes death; if he does not keep them, he eventually plunges fully into the abyss of perdition. 

And it will finally happen (5) that God, having removed this visible scene <note>The author uses a theatrical metaphor here (Latin: "scenâ"), suggesting that the physical world is a temporary stage for the drama of human existence.</note>, will reveal His invisible majesty and glory to the chosen **Creatures**, and make them sharers in a blessed eternity. This will be the final boundary of things, in which God will be, and will be seen as, all in all. 

The contemplation of all these things will provide, in the same order, the five parts of *Universal Wisdom* <note>original: "Pansophia"</note>. In the first of these, those general and common **Principles** <note>original: "Rationes." This refers to the underlying logic or governing laws of reality.</note> of things...

<summary>This page concludes the description of the five stages of existence—Nature, human ingenuity, moral governance/restoration, and the final eternal state—which form the framework for the author's system of Universal Wisdom.</summary>

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE <note>original: "Templi Pansophici"</note>

<sig>f</sig>

...notions <note>original: "tiones", completing the word "notiones" from the previous page.</note>, according to which all particular things exist and are brought about, we collect and contemplate. In the second part, we are to observe the course of all **Nature**; in the third, the works of human **Ingenuity** <note>original: "Ingenii". This refers to the creative and inventive capacity of the human mind.</note>—both those already discovered and those yet to be found. From there, a transition is made to considering the mysteries of our **perdition** and **restoration**; these conclude in the state of eternity.

XXVIII. Upon the **Theory of Things** completed in this way, the work will place a crowning touch <note>original: "colophonem imponet." A colophon was traditionally the finishing stroke or the final identifying inscription in a book; here it means a final completion.</note> by means of a **Review** <note>original: "ἐπιθεωρίᾳ" (epitheōria). A Greek term for a final inspection or supplementary contemplation.</note>, demonstrating the true Use of true Wisdom.

XXIX. *And so, the whole of Universal Wisdom <note>original: "Pansophia"</note> will consist of seven books.* The first of these will contain the **PREPARATORY**; the second, the **IDEAL**; the third, the **NATURAL**; the fourth, the **ARTIFICIAL** <term>Artificial <note>original: "Artificialia". In the 17th century, this refers to "Art" in the sense of human skill, technology, and crafts—things created by human hands rather than by nature.</note></term>; the fifth, the **SPIRITUAL**; the sixth, the **ETERNAL**; the seventh, the **PRACTICE** <note>original: "Praxin". The practical application of knowledge.</note> of all these, so that we may know how to transfer what we know into distinguished uses.

XXX. By this reasoning, Universal Wisdom will resemble a certain beautiful **Tree**, rising from its own roots; resting on its own trunk—one that is firm and sufficient for supporting the weight—spreading into [branches]... <meta>catchword: "mos", which completes "ramos" (branches) on the next page.</meta>

<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE <note>original: "TEMPLI PANSOPHICI"</note></header>

the richest things were preserved in the innermost sanctuary <note>original: "penetrali"</note>; so in the Temple of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia <note>A term used by Comenius to describe a system of "universal wisdom" that integrates all knowledge—scientific, philosophical, and theological—into a single, harmonious whole.</note></term>, everything ought to echo God, without whom nothing exists, is made, or is referred to anything else than Him. Namely, because He alone is the one from whom, through whom, and in whom all things exist <note>A reference to the New Testament, Romans 11:36: "For from him and through him and to him are all things."</note>, it would be impious to keep silent regarding any of His praises. This must be provided for, not merely so that in the end all things resolve themselves into His glory (which will certainly happen anyway), but so that wherever one goes, from the very entrance of this sacred Temple, religious ears might hear and understand the **silent cries of all things**. This will be achieved if, to those surveying all things, that **wisdom reaching from end to end and disposing all things sweetly** <note>original: "Sap. 8. 1." – A reference to the Wisdom of Solomon 8:1 from the Apocrypha.</note> is shown and held up to be seen with all things and in all things: so that wherever we are, all things—even when we are occupied with other matters—may raise and direct our minds toward her.

XXXII. There will be no need to hunt for opportunities for this. They will offer themselves as readily in this actual distribution of things to be known <note>original: "scibilium"</note> as the things themselves meet our senses everywhere. For in the **vestibule** <note>The "vestibule" or entryway represents the foundational or introductory stage of learning.</note>, considering...

XXXIII. *These things concern the Coherence of the parts of **Pansophia** <note>Comenius’s term for "Universal Wisdom."</note>, where it is also pleasing to mention one thing regarding their number for the sake of the Critics.* It might seem that the first and last parts, as they have been listed, are not truly parts of Pansophia: since the first only prepares, and the last only seals, the minds of those to be imbued—and those already imbued—with the knowledge of things.
