manner <note>original: "dammodo"</note> perform a preparatory and sealing function. For **Metaphysics** <term>Metaphysics: The branch of philosophy dealing with the first principles of things, such as being, knowing, and identity.</term> delivers nothing in particular; it only lays the general foundations of all things. Indeed, the **contemplation of God** outside of created things is nothing but the pinnacle of that knowledge which had previously been collected from created things; and yet the perfection of that knowledge is deferred to the heavens. So it remains for only the three middle parts (those three **Courtyards of the Pansophic temple**) to fully encompass all those things which we are granted to understand in this life: *the entire apparatus of our Natural, Rational, and Spiritual life.* This is certainly so. But in truth, the **Universe of Things** is one thing, and the **knowledge of the Universe of Things** is another—that is, **Pansophia** <note>Comenius's term for "Universal Wisdom," a system intended to harmonize all human knowledge.</note>. The former [the Universe] needs none of our preparations; the latter [Pansophia] requires them completely. For since the knowledge of things is a certain transplantation of those same things into our minds, we must not be ignorant of the manner in which that transplantation is performed, nor the use of it once performed. Therefore **Solomon**, when he began to learn the ways of acquiring Wisdom, believed that very thing to be already a part of Wisdom (Wisdom 8:21) <note>A reference to the *Book of Wisdom* (also known as the Wisdom of Solomon), where the author realizes that wisdom is a gift from God that must be sought with understanding.</note>. <note>The sentence likely begins with a negation from the previous page: "For no one] is truly wise who knows something, and yet is ignorant of the use of what he knows."</note> is truly wise who knows something, and yet is ignorant of the use of what he knows. Therefore, those parts—the first and the last—can by no means be cut off from the body of **Pansophia** <note>original: "Pansophiæ"; Comenius's term for "Universal Wisdom."</note>: much less the second and the sixth. For just as a building without a foundation, and a tree without its trunk, cannot stand: so also the **Order of Things**, in which all particulars are contained, cannot be known without the basis of Order, which the **First Wisdom** <term>First Wisdom: A reference to Metaphysics, which deals with the primary categories and principles of being.</term> reveals. Finally, one could not be said to know all things if he were to contemplate God in **Created Things** <term>Created Things: "Creaturis" in Latin, referring to anything made by God, including nature and humanity.</term>, and Created Things in God, but did not also understand what a Created Thing is outside of God, and what God is outside of every Created Thing. Because Pansophia (under the guidance of God and His Word) intends to piously meditate upon this in its final and deepest recess, it must not be deprived of this final inner sanctuary.

XXXIV. ***The second virtue of the Pansophic Method is GRADATION***: by which things are so connected to one another that, always and everywhere, later things seem to be born from earlier ones, and the more unknown things seem to arise spontaneously from those known things already set before them, without a leap and

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Gradation" (the second virtue of the Pansophic Method) which ensures that learning progresses logically without gaps. It completes the sentence from page 126 and introduces the idea that this method prevents students from falling into error.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
### SKETCH.
->F 4<-

without any gap. For just as we see happening in a **Tree**, where the **Trunk** rises from the **Root**; the **Branches** from the Trunk; the **Twigs** from the Branches; and from these the **Buds**, **Leaves**, **Flowers**, and **Fruit** emerge in an unbroken order—an order in which the later parts are always produced, strengthened, and given life by the earlier ones. By such a gradual linking <note>original: "concatenatione"; a chain-like connection</note> of things, we seek to ensure that the minds of students are not only capable of raising themselves step-by-step to all knowledge, but also (having no fear of the danger of falling back or wandering astray) may even do so with delight. Moreover, for those who must be freed from crooked opinions absorbed elsewhere and led back to right views about things, their minds—held by such gradation as if between barriers and boundaries—may wish to slip away out of love for their preconceived opinions, yet out of fear of a precipice, they must of necessity move forward and be carried right up to the summit of clear **Truth**.

XXXV. That this is the only legitimate way to lead minds accustomed to the darkness (of ignorance or error) safely into the clear light, was seen and taught by the great craftsman <note>original: "artifex"; the catchword "tifex" at the bottom confirms this refers to "artifex," likely a reference to a specific philosopher like Francis Bacon or perhaps a divine reference.</note> of teaching Truth—

...craftsman. <note>original: "tifex"; the completion of "artifex" from the previous page.</note> Augustine <note>Aurelius Augustine (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian philosopher whose works influenced the "Pansophic" educational theories of the 17th century.</note> says in these words:

> *Sore eyes are struck by the very brilliance they long to see, and having barely caught sight of it, they return with pleasure to the shadows. It is dangerous to want to show such people (even though they might now be called "healthy" in a way) what they are not yet strong enough to see. Therefore, they must first be exercised; and their love <note>The student's desire for the ultimate truth or wisdom.</note> should be usefully postponed and nourished. For first, certain things should be shown to them that do not shine by themselves, but can be seen by means of light: such as a garment, or a wall, or something similar. Next, something that—though not shining by its own light—nevertheless glitters more beautifully because of that light: such as gold, silver, and the like; yet not so radiant that it hurts the eyes. Then, perhaps, this earthly fire should be shown in a modest way; then the stars; then the moon; then the brightness of the dawn and the glow of the whitening sky. In these things (whether more quickly or slowly, whether through the whole sequence or by skipping some parts), as each person grows accustomed according to their own strength, they will eventually see the sun <note>In this allegory, the "sun" represents the highest form of Truth or God.</note> without fear and with great delight. Such a process is needed for those who are most eager for wisdom, yet do not yet see it clearly...* <note>original: "sapientiæ studiosissimis, nec acutè eam tamen vi-"; the text transitions from the Augustine quote to the author's application of it to students of Pansophia.</note>

### XXXVI.
For the very series of the parts of *Pansophia* <term>Universal Wisdom; the 17th-century ideal of a system containing all human knowledge</term> is graded in this way. ...the examination of general things: namely, Common Notions <term>fundamental, innate ideas shared by all people</term>, through which the eternal light, God, has imprinted certain beginnings of His craftsmanship upon the minds of all humans. These will be surveyed together here (in the first part of *Pansophia*); in doing so, they will begin to notice certain rays of that architectonic Wisdom <note>original: "Sapientiæ illius architectricis"; referring to Wisdom as the master builder or designer of the universe.</note> scattered through all things. 

Then they will be led forward to behold the more illustrious works of God in the courtyard of Nature <note>The second stage of the author's system, moving from abstract ideas to the physical world.</note>: where they will see with what vivid colors the Wisdom of God has illuminated those first outlines of His in created things. 

From there, admitted into the Courtyard of Art <term>the realm of human skill, science, and technology</term>, they will consider the rays of that eternal light—reflected in a thousand ways, refracted, and variously blended with one another—in a pure and astonishingly wonderful harmony. 

Having progressed deeper from there, there will be shown to them, first indeed, the horrendous chaos and the endless abyss of our own darkness, into which we have fallen when left to ourselves; then, however, even more radiant rays of eternal light sent down to swallow up this darkness <note>Likely referring to divine revelation or grace as the solution to human ignorance and sin.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the metaphor of the Sun from the previous page, completing the sentence about contemplating the "SUN of eternity" (God/Truth). The author then explains the pedagogical necessity of a step-by-step approach to learning to avoid intellectual vertigo or hostility toward new truths.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>SKETCH.</header>

[THE SUN], just as He is in Himself, and as He will be beheld in His majesty forever.

**XXXVII.** By this technique of gradual progression <note>original: "Gradationis... artificio"; referring to the step-by-step educational method developed by Comenius.</note>, we hope to achieve this: that minds may be led toward any height, gradually and almost imperceptibly, leaving no opportunity either for the weak to wander astray <note>original: "hallucinandi"; literally to wander in the mind or dream.</note>, or for the pretenders to knowledge <term>sciolists: people who have only a superficial or "smattering" of knowledge but act as experts</term> and the prejudiced to argue against the truth. 

Indeed, it is well known that those unaccustomed to heights, if suddenly carried to the peak of a tower, find their vision disturbed, so that they either tremble to look down or even suffer from vertigo. If, however, they become accustomed to looking out from less lofty places and raise themselves step-by-step, they can maintain a steady gaze equally in high places as in low. 

This also is known: anything contrary to what one is accustomed to, if it comes unexpectedly, violently strikes and disturbs the senses. Thus, even Truth, when presented too suddenly to those accustomed to some error, strikes horror into their minds, so that they feel they are being harmed; they set themselves in opposition, contradict the Truth itself, and quarrel with it. 

Therefore, let this not happen in this place—where it is not a school for dueling <term>Palæstra: an ancient Greek wrestling school, used here as a metaphor for a place of intellectual combat or aggressive debate</term> that is being prepared, but a Temple for contemplation.

<sig>F 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: præ-</meta>

<note>Vocabulary: Gradation (Step-by-step progress), Truth, Contemplation, Wrestling-School, Temple</note>

<summary>The author explains his "step-by-step" educational method, arguing that jumping straight to complex truths causes intellectual "vertigo" and hostility. He aims to create a "Temple for contemplation" rather than a place for aggressive debate.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, gradual progression, intellectual vertigo, truth vs error, contemplation, education method, Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 131, explaining how a step-by-step pedagogical method prevents intellectual conflict. By starting with "Metaphysics" as the foundation, Comenius argues that even the most stubborn critics will be forced to agree with the system's conclusions once they have accepted its basic, self-evident premises.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLE OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM</header>

Such a <term>ladder of things: a systematic progression of ideas from the simplest to the most complex</term> will prevent this. On its very first and lowest step—at the very Gate of <term>Pansophia: Universal Wisdom; the Comenian ideal of a single system encompassing all knowledge</term>, which is <term>Metaphysics: the branch of philosophy dealing with the first principles of things, such as existence and identity</term>—universal principles will be established. These principles are so clear in themselves that once they are granted (and they cannot *not* be granted, unless one has cast off all shame or wishes to appear to be "insane with reason" <note>original: "cum ratione insanire"; a paradoxical phrase suggesting someone using logic to reach absurd or denialist conclusions.</note>), everyone must necessarily accept everything else throughout the entire system of Universal Wisdom. This holds true provided that the subsequent points proceed step-by-step from one to another. This is because the reader will see that the general forms and rules of things have already been accepted, and that these are merely being applied everywhere, never newly invented.

Furthermore, this method will serve to bring those who disagree on specific details back into agreement, and will even allow those who wander from the path to correct themselves. For once the general standards of Truth are accepted, they will not dare to speak against them in defense of their own specific errors. This technique will be a kind of imitation of that divine <term>strategy: a clever plan or maneuver, often used in a military or rhetorical sense</term> which the Prophet Nathan used so successfully to convert his King <note>Referring to the biblical story in 2 Samuel 12, where the prophet Nathan uses a parable to lead King David to condemn his own actions before David realizes the story is about him.</note>. For just as David, held to a general principle <note>original: "in thesi"; a logical term for a general proposition or case.</note>, [judged] against himself—

<meta>catchword: itself</meta>

<summary>Comenius explains that his "ladder of things" starts with self-evident metaphysical principles that no rational person can deny, effectively trapping the reader into agreeing with the entire system. He likens this to the biblical story of Nathan the Prophet, who used a general parable to lead King David to admit his own specific guilt.</summary>

<keywords>Metaphysics, Universal Wisdom, Pansophia, logic, Prophet Nathan, King David, pedagogical method, first principles</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 132, concluding the point that by establishing self-evident universal principles, a teacher can lead a student (or an opponent) to recognize their own errors by showing how those errors contradict the foundations they have already accepted. It then introduces the next characteristic of the Pansophic method: Uniformity.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>DELINEATION.</header>

...pronounced it himself, so that once he was brought back to the <term>hypothesis: the starting premise or fundamental principle</term>, he was silenced, his error having been recognized. In the same way, whoever admits the general Truth here and confirms it with their own agreement will, by that very act, solidify the foundations. Later, when their own straying opinions strike against these foundations, they will spontaneously shatter.

XXXVIII. **The third virtue of the <term>Pansophic: relating to Universal Wisdom; a system of knowledge that encompasses all things</term> Method will be Uniformity.** This means that one subject will not be treated in one way and another subject in another; rather, all things will be handled in the very same manner. Indeed, just as the structure of the **Temple of Solomon** and the **Temple of Ezekiel** <note>original: "Templi Salomonici & Ezechiliani"; Comenius refers to the biblical descriptions of these temples as models of perfect architectural symmetry and divine order.</note> consisted of nothing but parallelograms—so that everything throughout was either square or rectangular—so too in this **Temple of Wisdom**, everything encountered will be explained through four **cardinal questions**:

->*WHAT? BY MEANS OF WHAT? HOW? and OF HOW MANY KINDS?*<-

These will be accompanied by their causes wherever the subject matter requires it. For through these four questions, everything that is essential becomes known. Consider:
(1) The **Thing** itself.
(2) The **Requirements** of the Thing.
(3) The **Modes** <note>In philosophy, "modes" refer to the specific ways or qualities in which a thing exists or expresses its nature.</note> of those requirements.
(4) And the **Modes of the Modes**, or the differences, which are called the **Genera** and **Species** <note>original: "Genera & Species"; these are the standard logical categories for classifying things into broad "kinds" and specific "types."</note> of Things.

The first question ex-

<sig>F 7</sig>
<meta>catchword: pedi-</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that a system built on universal truths forces critics to realize their own errors and introduces "Uniformity" as the method's third virtue. He proposes that all knowledge should be structured like a temple, using four specific questions (What, By What, How, and Of What Kind) to categorize everything from basic objects to complex species.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Uniformity, Temple of Solomon, Temple of Ezekiel, logic, cardinal questions, Genera and Species, architecture of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 133, where Comenius began listing the four "cardinal questions" or steps that ensure uniformity in his Pansophic method. The previous page ended while introducing the specific components of this fourfold approach.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>

...is provided by a **Definition**, which explains what the **Thing** <note>original: "Res"; a central term in Comenius's philosophy referring to any object, concept, or reality being studied.</note> is in its essence. The second is provided by the entire **Structure** of the Thing, or its **Idea**, which resolves the thing into its essential requirements. The third is provided by **Axioms** <note>original: "Axiomatibus"; self-evident truths or principles that require no proof.</note>, which state all essential Truths regarding both the Thing and its requirements. The fourth is provided by the **Distribution** <note>original: "Distributione"; the systematic classification or sorting into sub-categories.</note> of the Thing through new specific differences, should it have any further sub-categories beneath it. And then, each of those sub-categories is taken up again as a new theme, to be led through the same four questions.

XXXIX. This will be the perpetual form of our Method—a kind of <term>Pansophic: universal wisdom</term>, truly ingenious **Squaring of the Circle** <note>original: "Circuli Quadratura"; a classic mathematical problem. Comenius uses it here as a metaphor for achieving perfect, stable proportions in a system of knowledge.</note>, by which the wandering volatility of other methods may be reduced to a firmer stability. For what **Geometers** <note>original: "Geometræ"; mathematicians specializing in geometry.</note> proclaim regarding the **Square**—that it is the measure of all figures—can rightly be said of this four-sided Method: that it will be the measure of all Methods. It is our pleasure, lest we seem to speak in riddles, to illustrate this with a first, a second, and a third example, taken from the **Natural**, the **Artificial**, and the **Moral** realms.

XL. And indeed, it pleases us to take three examples from nature: the Substantial, the Accidental, and the Defective or Privative. <note>In the logic of Comenius’s time, these categories represent the essence of a thing (Substance), its non-essential qualities (Accident), and the absence of a quality, like darkness being the absence of light (Privative).</note> *Let the substance be the **SUN**: of which a brief, vigorous, and scientific <term>Pansophic: universal wisdom</term> treatment, "squared" according to our method, would be as follows.*

**Definition.**) The Sun is the greatest star of the heavens, an instrument of Nature destined for the illumination of the Earth through its circuit, and thereby for the enlivening of all things upon it and the measuring of the changes of the seasons.

**Idea.**) It is therefore constituted of three parts: 1. A massive globe of the most brilliant light gathered into one. 2. A certain life-giving power, flowing out with its rays. 3. A perpetual circular motion.

(NB. If anything here should seem doubtful, it would have to be proved by providing the reasons for each assertion, so that the reader may proceed to the following points without offense. Such is the case here with what is said regarding the **motion of the Sun**, which others attribute to the **Earth**. <note>original: "Solis motu, ab aliis Terræ attributo." Comenius is acknowledging the Copernican heliocentric theory, which was highly controversial at the time. While he was aware of the argument that the Earth moves, he chooses the traditional geocentric model for this specific exercise.</note> However, this dispute cannot be decided in this partial treatment of this theme, where the necessary premises have not been set forth. Let it be admitted in the meantime 

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>Comenius demonstrates his systematic "squared" method by defining the Sun and its essential properties. He notably acknowledges the scientific debate between geocentrism and heliocentrism, though he defers the resolution of that conflict to a later section.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Pansophic method, astronomy, geocentrism, heliocentrism, light, life-giving power, Nature's instruments</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 135 regarding the astronomical debate of the era. Comenius concludes that for his logical "Axioms" to work, the mechanics of the solar system matter less than the observable result: light must reach all sides of the globe.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>

(—take this as certain: that for the Sun to illuminate the Earth on every side, circular motion is required, whether that motion belongs to the Sun or to the Earth.) <note>original: "hoc ut certum, ut undique Terram collustret Sol, Motu circulari esse opus, sive ille Soli infit, sive Terræ." Comenius is sidestepping the Copernican-Ptolemaic debate to focus on the logical necessity of circular motion for global illumination.</note>

***Axioms:***) 1. The Sun is the primary source of light: [For indeed, if we relied only on the other stars, we would live in perpetual night.]

2. The Sun’s essence is light. [For light cannot be taken from it without the Sun ceasing to be the Sun.]

3. The Sun’s light emanates through rays. [This is obvious to the eye and is proven by reason.]

4. The Sun’s light flows out in every direction along with its rays. [That is, not only toward the Earth, but also to the sides: as is demonstrated by the illumination of the Moon, wherever it may be located.]

5. Therefore, the body of the Sun is spherical. [For the rays could not be scattered across a circumference except from a circular shape.]

6. Wherever the Sun reaches with its rays, it provides light and heat.

7. And through both of these, it imparts vitality to things.

8. The Sun always illuminates one half of the Earth; the other half remains unilluminated.

<summary>Comenius presents eight logical axioms defining the Sun's nature as a spherical source of light and heat that provides vitality to the world through circular motion.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, light, circular motion, axioms, spherical body, solar rays, Earth, Moon</keywords>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>SKETCH.</header>

9. That presence of the Sun above the Earth creates **Day**; its absence creates **Night**.
10. The more **vertical** the Sun is above the Earth, the more it shines and burns; the more **slanted**, the less it does so. <note>original: "collateralior." Comenius is describing how the angle of the sun affects temperature and brightness.</note>
11. The **vertical** rotations of the Sun produce **Summer**; the **slanted** ones produce **Winter**.
12. The Sun’s return to the same **verticality** constitutes a **Year**. And so much for what remains to be said on this.
*Distribution*) There is none, because the sun is a unique creature, a single Individual. <note>In Comenius’s logic, "distribution" refers to dividing a category into subtypes. Since there is only one Sun, it cannot be divided into species.</note>
XLI. *Let **LIGHT** be an example of a natural accident.* <term>accident: a philosophical term for a quality or property that exists within a subject (like light in the air) but is not the subject's essential nature.</term> Of which the:
*Definition)* **Light** <note>original: "Lumen"</note> is the rebounding of rays of light <note>original: "Lucis"</note> from the surface of an opaque body, and their dispersal through neighboring bodies. <note>Comenius follows the classical distinction between *Lux* (light at its source) and *Lumen* (light as it fills a space or reflects).</note>
*Idea)* Therefore, to produce Light, three things are required: 1. A certain **shining body**. 2. A **ray** cast out from it. 3. An **opaque body**, upon which the striking ray scatters and illuminates the surroundings.
*Axioms:*) 1. Without source-light, there is no **ray of Light**.
2. Without an opaque object, there is no **rebounding** and **scattering** of the Ray.

<meta>catchword: 3. Ra-</meta>

<summary>Comenius explains how the Sun's position creates the seasons and the year, then defines "Light" (Lumen) as a phenomenon requiring a source, a ray, and a surface to reflect it.</summary>
<keywords>day and night, seasons, year, Light, Lux, Lumen, reflection, opaque bodies, logic of accidents</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of light as a "natural accident," moving from its definition to the specific behavior of light rays and their interaction with different surfaces.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>

3. A ray is invisible in itself; it is only seen upon a physical object.
(This is evident in a ray sent through a narrow opening into a dark room, where the ray is not seen except on the opposite wall. If there is also a hole in that wall through which the ray falls, it will not be seen at all—unless perhaps dust motes <note>original: "corpuscula atoma." Comenius is referring to the "atoms" or tiny particles of dust that become visible in a sunbeam.</note> are floating in it, or you place your hand in its path.)

4. Opaque bodies with a smooth or polished surface merely reflect the ray and send it elsewhere; however, those with a rough surface dissipate and scatter it.
(For if you place a mirror in the path of a ray, the entire ray is reflected elsewhere and transfers the image of the light <note>original: "lucis imaginem." Here, light refers to the source-light.</note> entirely to that spot. But if you use a wooden board or anything less polished, the ray leaps apart and scatters the image of the light into general illumination <note>original: "lumen." Following his earlier definition, "lumen" is the secondary light created by reflection.</note>.)

5. The clearer the ray, the more brilliant the illumination.
(Therefore, a greater illumination comes from a solar ray than from the ray of a candle, even if both are sent through the same opening.)

6. More rays, more light.

*Distribution.*) Light is either Natural, from the sun and the stars; or Artificial, from our own fire and candles; or **Dead** <note>original: "Mortuum." This refers to bioluminescence or phosphorescence—light that appears to have no heat or active source, such as that found in certain minerals or decaying matter.</note>, proceeding from shining gems, the scales of certain fish, rotting wood, and similar things.

## XLII. *The nature of SHADOW is explained Pansophically <note>original: "Pansophice." In the context of Comenius's work, this means explaining a concept through his system of "Universal Wisdom," which seeks to harmonize all physical and spiritual knowledge.</note> as follows:*

*Definition.*) Shadow is a diminished light on an illuminated body, arising from the intervention of an <term>opaque</term> <note>original: "opaci." A material that does not allow light to pass through it.</note> body.

*Concept.*) It is therefore constituted by three bodies: First, a luminous one, scattering light rays; second, an Opaque one, intercepting a portion of the rays; third, likewise an opaque one, representing on its surface the light both scattered and intercepted. Take away one of these, and you have removed the shadow.

*Axioms.*) 1. Everything luminous radiates.

2. Everything opaque placed opposite a light source casts a shadow.

3. Everything opaque placed opposite an opaque body <note>specifically, the body casting the shadow</note> is shadowed.

<vocab>Light, Shadow, Pansophically, Opaque, Axioms, Nature, Artificial, Dead</vocab>

<summary>This page categorizes types of light—natural, artificial, and "dead"—and provides a systematic definition of shadows as the interaction between a light source and two opaque bodies.</summary>
<keywords>optics, bioluminescence, shadow, Pansophy, axioms, light distribution</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Axioms" section regarding the nature of shadows and transitions into the next major topic within the "Pansophic Temple"—Artificial things, using the clock as the primary example.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
->TEMPLE OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM<-
<page-type>text</page-type>

4. Shadow is the shadow of an intervening body <note>original: "corporis intermedii." This refers to the object situated between the light source and the surface where the shadow falls.</note>.

5. Shadow is a certain image of the intervening body, representing all its primary features <note>original: "omnia ejus majora repræsentans." This suggests that while a shadow lacks detail, it accurately captures the overall proportions and silhouette of the object.</note>.

6. The greater the light and the denser the <term>opaque</term> bodies, the denser the shadow; and vice versa.

7. Shadow always extends in the direction opposite to the light source.

8. If the shadowing body is equal in size to the light source, an equal shadow is always extended into infinity <note>This describes a cylindrical shadow where the rays are parallel.</note>.

9. If the shadowing body is larger than the light source, the shadow grows larger into infinity.

10. If the shadowing body is smaller than the light source, the produced shadow decreases until it ends in a <term>cone</term> <note>original: "conum." In optics, this is known as the "umbra cone," where the shadow eventually tapers to a point.</note> and vanishes.

(Note: All these things can be sketched with painted diagrams for the sake of easier understanding.)

*Distribution.*) Finally, the differences between shadows, if there are any, must be explained.

(For the Heavens themselves are a natural Clock <note>original: "Naturale enim Horologium ipſum Cœlum eſt."</note>, which measures the greater ages of the World by the unending rotation of the stars; but we have sought instruments to accurately distinguish even smaller spaces of hourly time. These have been found, primarily of three kinds. Our discussion now concerns all these in general; whoever wishes to do so will be able to apply it to the specific types with the same ease.)

*Concept*) The foundation of a Clock is motion (because Time itself, or the duration of things, is a certain motion. Furthermore, the measure and the thing measured must agree in their nature <note>original: "Menſura autem & menſuratum genere conveniunt." A philosophical principle: you cannot measure time, which is movement, with something static.</note>.) Therefore, every Clock requires three things: 1. Something mobile. 2. Something that moves it. 3. Spaces marked by hourly intervals.

(These three things occur equally in sundials, hourglasses, and mechanical clocks.)

*Axioms*) 1. Every Clock has motion. (Because

(Because without motion there can be no measurement of time, as we have already seen. 3. Every Clock has something that moves it.

4. Every Clock has designated intervals of hours.
(Otherwise it would not be a Clock if it did not indicate the hours. Therefore, designated lines and numbers indicate them.)

5. The intervals of these are accurately distinguished.

### XLIV. *Finally, let us add an example from Morals concerning the* **MAGISTRACY**.
### *Whose Pansophic treatment would be as follows.*

*Definition*) The <term>Magistracy</term> <note>original: "Magistratus." This refers to the office of government or the person of the ruler/civil authority.</note> is the legitimate pre-eminence of certain individuals within human society, instituted for the sake of maintaining that same society in order.

*The Ideal Concept.*) The requirements of this eminence are:

1. That the person be suitable for such a great office; that is, to have the power, the knowledge, and the will to rule others and to keep them in order.

2. To be legitimately established: whether by right of election, of inheritance, or of <term>legitimate war</term> <note>original: "belli legitimi." In 17th-century political theory, this referred to the right of conquest following a "just war."</note>.

3. In actual practice, to rule and administer all things with gravity, vigilance, and constancy. And this is done by: (1) Prescribing the Laws of Order, and ensuring that once prescribed, they are not ignored. (2) Observing how Order and the Laws are being maintained.

<meta>catchword: (3)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the classification of timekeeping devices and introduces the "Pansophic" definition of government (Magistracy), outlining the qualifications and duties of a legitimate ruler.</summary>
<keywords>sundials, hourglasses, mechanical clocks, Magistracy, governance, legitimacy, rule of law, Pansophy</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Magistracy, specifically focusing on the third requirement for actual practice and the foundational axioms of social order. It flows from the definition of the magistrate's duties toward the legal and philosophical justification of authority.</meta>

(3) By protecting the laws, defending those who observe them, and punishing those who violate them.

*Axiom.*) 1. Wherever there is human society, there is a need for the bond of society: **Order**.

2. Wherever there is order, there is a need for the bond of order: **Laws**.
(So that no one may be ignorant of their place and their duties.)

3. Furthermore, wherever there are laws, there is also a need for a law-giver and a guardian: the <term>Magistracy</term> <note>original: "Magistratu." The office of civil government or the person of the ruler.</note>.

4. The Magistracy must not be entrusted to anyone except those who are suitable.

5. A person is suitable to hold the office of magistrate who **can**, **knows how to**, and **wills** to rule others.
(All of these are required, because if even one is missing, it removes their suitability. <note>The author argues that a ruler needs power, wisdom, and the moral desire to serve; without all three, they are unfit for the office.</note>)

6. The Magistracy must not be taken up unless it has been legitimately commissioned.
(For since all men are by their own nature free, gifted with the privilege of <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Rationis."</note> and <term>Free Will</term> <note>original: "Arbitrii." The capacity to make one's own choices or judgments.</note>, they do not wish to be ruled or to obey except freely, and only where and to what extent they judge it to be beneficial for themselves and for human society. If they think otherwise—as when they see someone force themselves into the Magistracy, and...

It emphasizes that because humans possess reason and free will, they only naturally submit to authority that is beneficial and legally established.</summary>

...they judge that the ruler wishes to abuse their power) they do not obey, except by force. But where there is coercion, there all things are violent, and hostile to order and permanence. <note>The author warns that rule by force is inherently unstable and contrary to a well-ordered society.</note>)

7. The <term>Magistracy</term> <note>original: "Magistratus." The office of government.</note> is legitimately commissioned to one to whom it is entrusted by the consent of the people, or by nature, or by God.
(That is, either by legitimate election, or by legitimate inheritance, or by legitimate war <note>original: "bello legitimo." This refers to the historical "Just War" theory where a territory might be acquired through a lawful conflict.</note>; all of these have their own laws and conditions, which shall not be touched upon here.)

8. It is the duty of the Magistrate to bring laws to the people.
(But only just laws, and those which it would be in the **Public Interest** <note>original: "Usu publico."</note> to have observed.)

9. It is the duty of the Magistrate to provide that no one may do wrong through ignorance of the laws.
(Therefore they are bound to proclaim the laws, both those passed by themselves and ancient laws, whether divine or human. Therefore, they must also erect <term>Schools</term>, <term>Temples</term> <note>original: "Templa." In this context, places of religious and moral instruction.</note>, and <term>Courts</term> <note>original: "Curias." Administrative or senate houses.</note> where such things may be taught: they must preserve those already built, and ensure that everyone there may learn what is to be done and what is to be avoided.)

10. It is the duty of the Magistrate to watch over all things so that they are done according to the prescription of the Laws.

### XLV.
From these examples, it is clear that all <term>Pansophic</term> <note>original: "Pansophica." Referring to "Pansophia" or Universal Wisdom, a 17th-century movement aimed at organizing all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</note> matters will be brief, linked, gradual, and self-evident. For if these examples perhaps do not yet seem so to someone, I would wish him to consider that something is missing here which cannot be missing in the body of Pansophia itself; namely, that **continuous guidance of the mind**, raising it step-by-step from one thing to another. Now we fall into these matters, as it were, abruptly: and these things, handed down in fragments, savor of that "piecemeal breaking" <note>original: "frustillationem." From the Latin for "scraps" or "morsels." The author argues against teaching facts in isolation, which he believes ruins the appetite for true understanding.</note> of the sciences against which we always protest. I hope the more discerning may be able to "scent" the convenience of this method from the example given: namely, if a **Definition** exactly circumscribes the essence of a thing, and the **Idea** resolves it into its principles; then from both...

<header># OUTLINE</header>

Let **axioms** <note>original: "Axiomata." Self-evident truths that serve as the foundation for further reasoning.</note> flow naturally; and finally, let the **Distribution** <note>The systematic division or classification of a subject into its constituent parts.</note> signal that we must proceed in the **Analysis of Things** <note>original: "Rerum Analysi." The process of breaking down complex concepts into their simplest parts to understand their true nature.</note>. In this way, all things will be easy to perceive, yet solid in their practical use. For when the **Intellect** <note>original: "Intellectus." The human faculty of understanding and rational thought.</note> sees that everything arises from within itself and that nothing is introduced from outside, it meets the truth of things with delight. Just as Things determine their own nature, the Intellect confirms them by its own testimony—certain that if things were not exactly as they appear to be, they would not even exist. By this method, everything shines by its own light, and there is no need to beg for or rely on uncertain belief.

### XLVI.
It will not be out of place to add the rules we have established for ourselves to observe in collecting and constructing these *Definitions, Ideas, Axioms, and Partitions* <note>original: "Definitionibus, Ideis, Axiomatibus, & Partitionibus." These four categories represent the structural building blocks of the author's philosophical system.</note> of things. We do this so that if we are not permitted to finish the work—perhaps prevented by fate or hindered by the duties of our ordinary calling—those who wish to follow us will not be ignorant of our intentions. For I hope that **Christ** will send others to enter into our labors and bring our sowing to the harvest; so that both he who sows and he who... <note>The text cuts off here, but the author is referencing the biblical proverb: "that both he that soweth and he that reapeth may rejoice together."</note>

### XLVII.
**Generally, in all these (Definitions, Ideas, Axioms, and Partitions), the following must be achieved: 1. That all things be plain and clear in both word and meaning, needing no commentaries as far as possible, and illustrated only by examples where necessary. 2. That they be of the highest Truth, strictly according to the laws of *true of all*, *true by its very nature*, and *universally first* <note>original Greek: "κατὰ παντὸς, κατ' αὐτὸ, καθόλου πρῶτον." These are the three conditions for a perfect demonstration in Aristotelian logic: a premise must apply to every case, be essential to the subject, and be stated at the highest level of generality that still applies.</note>; so that they cannot be attacked except by sophistry <note>Deceptive or fallacious reasoning that sounds plausible but is intended to mislead.</note>, nor do they need to be defended by any exceptions, distinctions, or limitations (whether fictional or real). This will be achieved if care is taken so that nothing is attributed except to that in which it exists primarily and by itself. For Amandus Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610) was a Swiss Reformed theologian whose works on logic and theology were widely influential in the 17th century.</note> (in the preface to his *Logic*) rightly writes: *The common rules of the Arts have so many exceptions because, against the law of the "universally first," <note>original Greek: "καθόλου πρῶτον."</note> specific things are presented as generalities.* Therefore, he says, let it be observed which things agree and which disagree: and let those that agree be referred to one category, and those that disagree to another; in this way, there will be no exception...**

but all things shall be legitimately encompassed by proper distributions. On the other hand, against the necessity of Distinctions and Limitations, a good remedy will be to employ nothing ambiguous or <term>homonymous</term> <note>words that sound the same but have different meanings</note>, but rather to express all distinct Things with distinct Words during the very analysis of the Subject Matter, so that nothing is left to be determined later by a commentary. Thus it will happen that, just as things already collected do not need to be collected, and things already ordered do not need to be ordered; so too things already distinct will not need to be distinguished, and things already limited will not need to be limited. 3. XLIX. Let an example of the latter be the utterance of that blind man in the Gospel: *God does not hear sinners.* <note>A reference to John 9:31</note> Whether this be true must be judged from the definitions of GOD, SINNER, and HEARING; if these are true, it will be judged most conveniently. For because (1) God is the Being of beings, that is, the supreme Being, and consequently supremely one, true, and good—that is, holy. (2) Sin, however, is a turning away from the good and the holy. (3) To hear, moreover (in this place), is to grant a prayer—that is, to admit someone graciously and to fulfill their will. How could the most holy and most jealous God love and promote those who turn away from Him (as long as they remain such)?

For truly *Vives* <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540), a Spanish humanist and educational reformer.</note> (in his book *On the Explanation of Essences*, after he had taught that a good definition is one that is brief, clear, and reciprocal <note>A "reciprocal" definition is one where the definition and the term defined are perfectly interchangeable.</note>) writes: *The duty of defining belongs to a great and excellent man, who has not only explored the entire nature of the thing he is about to define, but is also not ignorant of all other things surrounding it (if they are to be correctly limited): otherwise, it is easy to be confused and to confuse others. I maintain that a plurality of words should be used whenever the evidence and fullness of the meaning can be better served. Therefore, those definitions of ours, set down a little earlier, are somewhat more wordy. Thirdly, whenever a **Difference** <note>In logic, the "differentia" is the specific characteristic that distinguishes one species from another within the same category.</note> cannot be derived from the **form** <note>The "form" refers to the essential nature or "soul" of a thing; the author admits that in the natural world, these internal essences are often hidden from us.</note> (for we admit, along with others, that these are sometimes unknown, especially in natural things), it must be taken from the **Purpose**. Bellarinus <note>Likely a reference to a scholastic commentator or textbook author of the period.</note> gathers this from Aristotle himself and demonstrates it with the example of the **Horse** (*Practice of the Art*, Book 3, Distinction 2, Number 5). Since the purpose of the horse is perceived from its effects and its use—namely, to serve man by transporting him or his goods from place to place, swiftly and comfortably—he constructs a definition from this. (He adds these two qualities—swiftly and comfortably—to distinguish it from other animals that provide a similar use. For the Ox and the Donkey also transport things, but not swiftly; likewise the Camel, but not as comfortably.) From this, he completes the following definition:

->**A Horse is an animal ordained for transporting a man from place to place swiftly and comfortably.**<-

Fourthly, it seems, however, that sometimes the **final** <note>The "final cause" or ultimate purpose.</note> or the **proximate efficient** <note>The "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings something into being.</note> cause of a thing can be added to the formal difference.

...therefore, so that the **Essence** <note>original: "Essentia"; the fundamental nature of a thing</note> of a matter may be more fully expressed, and may more easily resolve itself into its own **principles** <note>original: "principia"; the basic components or starting points of a concept</note> (following the **Idea**). To that end, in our definition of the **Sun** and the **Magistrate**, we added the final cause; but in the definition of **Shadow**, we added the efficient cause. Likewise, in the definition of a **Clock**, we placed a distinction drawn from both the purpose and the maker. If this is not pleasing to the reader, they must be elaborated more accurately so that they are strictly concise. But I foretell that we shall become obscure if we wish to be too brief: a reef which I especially avoid, and advise others to avoid.

LI. No less carefully must the **Ideas of Things** be established: because their use for a true, full, and distinct knowledge of things is no less significant. For although the **Definition** itself may encompass the entire essence of a thing, it does so only in an involved manner. The complications of things are so varied that the unskilled—not knowing how to distinguish **essentials** from accessories—often seize upon that in which nothing of substance resides, while omitting the **fundamentals**. This is an error most fertile in producing other errors. To firmly hold the essentials in every matter is the most beautiful [exercise] of the mind...

will be the light. Exactly what Horace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus, a leading Roman lyric poet</note> pronounced regarding the necessity of foreknowledge of Matters for Eloquence:

But to find the true Ideas of Things is an art of no less difficulty than to define Things truly: accordingly, we shall provide some advice on the method by which they are to be tracked down.

LII. It is necessary that all things which are made, and which exist, are made and have been made according to Ideas. For unless something existed first in an Idea (that is, unless it held some reason or form through which it could be made and exist), it would not exist at all. Furthermore, the Ideas of Things are threefold: Artificial, Natural, and Divine. **Artificial** Ideas are a certain image <note>original: "ectypus," referring to a copy or impression of an original</note> or imitations of natural things; **Natural** Ideas are imitations of Divine things; **Divine** Ideas exist from themselves, the true archetype <note>original: "archetypus," the primary original pattern</note> of all things that are rightly made. These, however, because they are hidden in eternity, are not known to us except insofar as they are in natural things—

LIII. Furthermore, all natural things are made according to the same Ideas, because they are from the same Creator <term>Opifex: the divine Craftsman or Architect of the universe</term>. Because He is one, He preserved one order in all things; and because He is the best, He preserved the best; and because He is true, He did so truly, and not in an imaginary way. Therefore, since order is truly implanted in Things, it can truly be found, if it is sought by true ways. However, the true ways are not those which our Intellect—too fickle and prone to errors—fashions for itself and attaches to Things, but those which the Things themselves show by their own process. For from the actions and passions <note>In this context, "passions" refers to how a thing is acted upon or affected by external forces</note> of Things, their qualities are known; from their qualities, their essence; and from all of these, their essential requirements become known. # THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

We shall find a wonderful harmony of things, according to a certain likeness of the First Being <note>original: "Entis primi," referring to God as the primary source of existence</note>. For we shall discover that everything found in the highest being is also found in the lowest being <note>original: "Ente infimo"</note>, but at the lowest level, as is customary in the first rudiments of things, it is enfolded. Thus, the more specific and always more perfect development of creatures is nothing other than a greater and more perfect unfolding of those things which are within, toward the outside; our Metaphysics <note>The branch of philosophy dealing with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, and substance</note> will make this clearly known. Therefore, all creatures, down to the smallest specks of dust, are our masters and teachers here, if we look upon what is done by them and about them not with the eyes of brutes, but with rational and attentive consideration.

LIV. For example: *In order that the Ideas of Things may be perfectly discovered, I think we must proceed as follows:* 1. Whatever truly essential requirements occur in all species of a certain thing, a general Idea of those same things must be placed under the very genus of those species. For example: Because in every particular motion I see three boundaries—From where, By what way, and To where <note>original: "Unde, Quâ, Quô"</note>—why should I not establish these as the general requirements of all motion? And why not also these same

# OUTLINE

What should I look for in every Being <note>original: "Ens"; a philosophical term for anything that exists</note>? Certainly, every Being comes from somewhere: that is, it has some origin. And it proceeds by some means, that is, it has some form, through which it is carried toward something, that is, toward its own end <note>The author is using the Aristotelian framework of four causes: efficient (origin), formal (form), and final (end/purpose)</note>.

2. Whatever requirements are discovered in the general Idea of Things <note>original: "Idea Rerum"</note>, those same things must be sought in every species placed under that genus. For either all the same things will be found (though under another, that is, a specific form and name: just as the journey of Christ in Mark 7:31 is described by these three: from Tyre, to Galilee, through Decapolis); or it will serve as proof that the general Idea was not accurately made, which will provide an opportunity for correcting it; or finally, it will be clear that it is not a true species of that genus, which will serve to rectify the classification.

3. Whatever is present in one species, something analogous to it should be sought in the opposite species. For it is necessarily present, although in some different manner, which is introduced by that difference which constitutes that new species. LV. *As for Axioms <note>fundamental principles or self-evident truths</note>, since no learned person is unaware that the primary strength of Truth consists in them, no one will deny that exquisite care must be given to establishing them correctly in all respects (that is, collecting, organizing, and solidifying them to an immovable strength).* For they are indeed what they are called: the norms of reasoning, the principles of all conclusions already gathered or yet to be gathered through true reasoning. The number of these is greater, their order more elegant, and their power more potent for both inquiring into and finding the invincible Truth of Things—as well as for liberating and defending it from error—than people commonly understand. Since, up to this point, they have neither been collected nor reduced into an order (they only shine forth sporadically from human thoughts, doctrines, discourses, and actions), and since men do not sufficiently know how to rely upon them, they allow themselves to be carried away by every wind of opinions and sophisms <note>clever but false arguments</note>.

Always and everywhere certain <term>presuppositions</term> <note>foundational assumptions that are taken for granted before an argument begins</note> are encountered, upon which the reasoning itself rests as if on a base. For plainly, just as whatever is moved, is moved around something immobile; so whatever is inferred by <term>reasoning</term> <note>original: "ratiocinatio" - the mental process of moving from known premises to a new conclusion</note> is inferred by the force of some immovable Truth. Let this be made clear by examples.

If someone threatens a servant with a club because of a loss he caused, and the servant cries out, *I did not do it willingly*: behold, reasoning on both sides from presuppositions! For the master presupposes this <term>Axiom</term> <note>a self-evident truth that requires no proof</note>: *He who causes damage is liable to punishment*; You have caused damage. Therefore [you are liable]. The servant, on the contrary, argues: *The innocent man is not liable to punishment*. I am innocent. Therefore [I am not liable]. And beneath his major premise, he silently lays another, as if it were well known: *Harm is voluntary. My deed was not voluntary*. Therefore it is not harm, etc.

Behold, such things occur in the words and deeds of all men, even the most dull-witted, like the remaining traces of a <term>wisdom</term> <note>original: "sapientia"</note> created together with man, and indelible (although [covered by] folly...

This power is present even in lower creatures, though in a lower degree, and manifests itself more obscurely. From this, therefore, I gather a supreme metaphysical axiom <term>axiom: a self-evident truth that serves as a starting point for reasoning</term>: ***Whatever exists, rejoices in its being.*** Or: ***Whatever exists, protects itself in its own being for as long as it can.*** And by this path, from all particular axioms (whether they be Natural, Artificial, Moral, Political, or Theological, provided they are solidly true), we can draw out universal metaphysical principles. LXVI. Once, the Pyrrhonian philosophers <note>Followers of Pyrrho of Elis (c. 360–270 BCE), the founder of Greek Scepticism.</note>, called Sceptics, used to argue that all things could be debated on either side of a question—even this very question: whether all things could be debated on either side? They believed the truth of their assertion could be most clearly demonstrated if they wrapped even this claim of theirs in doubt. Whether they played at these things in jest or in earnest does not matter to us; however, as we press a serious matter seriously, and assert that necessary Demonstrations <term>Demonstrations: logical proofs that lead to an inescapable conclusion</term> ought to be and can be introduced in place of slippery disputes, a certain necessity is imposed upon us by their example to demonstrate this very assertion in such a way that no one can resist it. Therefore, for the honor of the always unconquered Truth, and of God, the eternal protector of truth, we now approach the task, *intending to demonstrate these three things with mathematical certainty*:

1. *That everything True is by its nature demonstrable.*

2. *That every Pansophic <term>Pansophic: relating to "Pansophia" or universal wisdom, the idea that all knowledge can be integrated into a single system</term> principle is demonstrable by the power of Method.*

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

### 3. *That this mystery of Method consists in the accurate Graduation of Truth.*

LXVII. This is first demonstrated by the following apodictic <note>A logical term for a proposition that is demonstrably or necessarily true.</note> Syllogism:

> Whatever possesses true, proximate, and immediate Causes, Effects, and properties, that thing is truly demonstrable.
>
> But every Truth possesses true, proximate, and immediate Causes, Effects, and properties.
>
> Therefore, every Truth is truly demonstrable.

The Major proposition is necessarily true; because the firmest demonstrations are drawn from proximate causes and effects, and from Essential properties: nor has any Logician ever required, or been able to require, anything else.

The Minor proposition is likewise certain: because if a thing does not have true causes, actions, passions, or properties, then it does not truly exist—that is, it is not true. Therefore, whatever is true must necessarily have true causes, properties, and effects (whether these are already known to us or not yet known). According to this reasoning, only the False is undemonstrable;

<vocab>Method, Truth, Syllogism, Cause, Effects, Proposition, Logic, Falsehood</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a logical argument asserting that all truth is inherently demonstrable through its causes and effects, forming a cornerstone of the author's pansophic method. It uses a formal syllogism to prove that only falsehood lacks the necessary properties to be demonstrated.</summary>
<keywords>logic, syllogism, pansophy, theory of truth, causality, demonstration, philosophical method</keywords>

First, concerning the whole: consider the *Definitions*, *Hypotheses*, *Theorems*, and *Problems*. For whatever comes to be treated in a Pansophic <note>original: "Pansophicè"; referring to "Pansophy" or Universal Wisdom, a 17th-century movement to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system</note> manner, that thing is immediately and before all else *defined*. Then, an *Idea* is constructed: this is like a *Hypothesis* (not a fictional one, however, but one based in reality), from which, once granted, all inquiries regarding that subject can be satisfied. Or it is like a *Problem*, which declares that something can be done, provided this or that is given—namely, the essential requirements of the Thing. Finally, what are *Axioms* but *Theorems* announcing some true and necessary attribute of the Subject? They are of such evident Truth that they are like pure principles, known through themselves. Therefore, from a good preparation of good Definitions, Hypotheses, Theorems, and Problems, why should it not be permitted to weave together good demonstrations?

...they are not able. Yet it is not that they are unwilling; for they do wish for it, and they strive for it. Now, they are unable either because strength is lacking, or because they are prevented, or because the necessary instrument is missing (for <term>Pansophia<note>Universal Wisdom; a system of knowledge aiming to organize all human learning into a single coherent whole.</note></term> has identified those three things as the impediments to any work). But Strength—that is, health and vigor—is not lacking, which is evident from their walking and their exertion. Nor are they prevented by anyone, whether by decree or by force. Therefore, the instrument by which one climbs to sublime heights is missing: namely, a *Ladder* or *Steps*. For since no man’s feet (the instrument given by nature for moving oneself from place to place) exceed a length of two <term>cubits<note>An ancient unit of measurement, roughly the length of a forearm (18–22 inches).</note></term>, nor can they be spread further than that while walking, no one can move himself beyond a distance of two cubits without the help of some intermediary means. Since, therefore, the height of that Tower is 50 cubits, but the feet of those walking about reach only two cubits or less, it is evident that it cannot be climbed without intermediate steps. And because the ascent is not made, such an instrument (STEPS...

# DELINEATION.

LXXI. Let the steps be constructed, and let them be properly strengthened and secured with railings, so there is no fear of falling; and if there are those present who wish to ascend, are able, and know how (namely, by raising themselves from one step to the very next, without jumping), the result will follow. Even if the height were a thousand times greater, it would be impossible otherwise. I shall demonstrate this by the following <term>Apodictic</term> <note>a logical term for a proposition that is demonstrably certain or necessary</note> <term>Sorites</term> <note>a form of argument consisting of a series of propositions in which the predicate of each is the subject of the next</note>:

1. *Whoever has feet in good working order is able to walk.*
(For given the primary cause and the instrument, the effect can follow. Feet are the instrument of walking.)

2. *Whoever walks moves their feet alternately.*
(For unless one moved them, one would not walk but stand still; nor can that movement happen without the alternating transposition of the feet.)

3. *Whoever moves their feet alternately is taking steps.*

4. *Whoever takes steps is moving forward or backward, upward or downward.*
(For to advance or retreat, and—

<summary>The author uses a logical "Sorites" argument to prove that any great height can be reached if one proceeds by small, secure steps rather than attempting to jump. This serves as a metaphor for a structured, incremental approach to education and mastery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the architectural and philosophical metaphor of the "Pansophic Temple," moving from general movement to the specific mechanics of climbing and the construction of stairs.</meta>

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

To move upward or downward is to walk. And just as walking on a level surface is called *Walking*; so walking upward or downward is called a *Step* <note>original: "Gradus"</note>.

5. *He who walks upward, ascends by steps.*

6. *He who ascends by steps, can ascend as far as the steps go.*

7. *Therefore, whoever has feet can go wherever the Steps lead.*
Which was to be demonstrated.

LXXII. Moreover, it is evident that Steps can be built to any height, provided that a firm medium is given joining the base to the summit. For nothing prevents steps from being built from the earth to the clouds, other than the fact that the intervening medium—the Air, or Smoke rising thither—is of too weak a consistency; it cannot be carved, nor is it equal to bearing a load. But if a mountain, or a cliff, or a tower should rise to such a height, nothing prevents Steps from being built within or against it, by which a man may even reach that point. Whether one chooses to construct them of wood or of stone, there is no lack of (iron) tools <note>original: "instrumenta ferrea"</note> with which they may be hewn: provided that one is not reluctant to apply both labor and wisdom.

Indeed, the Steps <note>Latin: *Gradus*; the author uses the metaphor of a staircase to describe the stages of a curriculum</note> should (1) begin from the very bottom, (2) and continue to the very top, (3) and this must be done coherently and without any gap, so that where one ends, the next begins. (4) Furthermore, they must be proportioned to the stature of those for whom they are prepared. (For a child's ascent, small intervals between steps are necessary; for a man's, they can be further apart.) (5) Finally, if one continuous ladder is not sufficient to reach the height, let several be made until it is reached. For example, if ladders are to be erected inside a Tower of 50 cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>, and wood of equal length is not available, let broken ladders <note>meaning a series of shorter flights of stairs</note> be made from one floor to the next. For instance: let beams of ten cubits be taken, and from them let ladders of five steps be constructed, each to be fitted to its own floor in this manner: (*see the following page*). Therefore, given a firm middle ground between the base and the summit, the Steps can be built. This is what was to be demonstrated.

# SKETCH.

three elements converge: the *Leader, the Led, and the Leading* itself. Within this last element, there are further points of reference: *From where, To where, and By which way*. So it is in *Instruction*: there is the *Teacher, the Student, and the very act of teaching and learning*. In this act, there are again specific points: first, the point *From which*—namely, those principles <note>original: "principia"</note> in which the teacher and student first find common ground; second, the point *To which*—namely, the Conclusions <note>original: "Conclusiones"</note> which are as yet unknown to the student, but toward which the teacher takes up the labor of leading him; and finally, the *way by which* he is to be led there. This way is likewise unknown to the student, but the Teacher must not be ignorant of it. Furthermore, unless this path both tends toward the proper goal and is so clearly explained that the one being led can progress without stumbling, he will not arrive at the destination. Therefore, wherever there are those who wish to *lead and be led, to teach and be taught*, and yet the desired effect does not follow, it is certain that the reliable means are lacking—that is, a well-explained path and Steps <note>original: "Gradus"</note> correctly fitted for the elevating of the mind. This I shall demonstrate through a graduated Method <term>Methodus: a systematic way of teaching or investigation</term>, by a continuation of several Axioms <term>Axioma: a self-evident truth or established principle</term>, as follows:

LXXIV. Axiom 1. *All men by nature desire to know.* This is a saying of Aristotle <note>A reference to the opening line of Aristotle's Metaphysics.</note>.

It is a point that no one has ever called into doubt, because the matter is manifest. For certainly, a certain varied pleasure of inquiring and experimenting <note>original: "sciscitandi & experimentandi"</note> breaks forth from earliest infancy and accompanies us through our whole life, so that there is no one (I speak of living men, not of human corpses) who does not daily long to hear, see, taste, touch, and altogether come to know something new. This is evident even by the examples of the most idle people and those sluggish with leisure; although they do not transfer this natural impulse toward serious matters, they nevertheless devote themselves to any trifles whatsoever, rather than being willing to deny or able to conceal the stings of nature.

**Axiom 2. *He who desires to know, desires to know the truth.***

For unless knowledge is true, it is not knowledge, but ignorance and error <note>original: "inscentia & error"</note>: which no one can truly want. For to what end would he want to err—that is, to be deceived? Rather, the mind by its own nature shrinks from this, and therefore if it detects that it has erred anywhere, or thinks it has detected it, it immediately changes its opinion, whether openly or secretly. They are able...

# SKETCH.

For causes may indeed arise why someone might wish not to *appear* to think differently, yet they cannot help but *actually* think differently once an error has been noticed.

Axiom 3. *He who desires to know the truth, rejoices in the truth.*

This is a characteristic of the human heart, and inseparable from it: that if the hope of what it earnestly desires is offered, it rejoices.

Axiom 4. *He who rejoices in the truth, embraces the truth when it arrives.*

Nor can this be absent from the nature of desire and love: that it pours itself out toward the beloved object which it has expected with joy, and joins itself to that object, and that object to itself.

Axiom 5. *Having embraced the truth, he holds the truth fast.*

It is also a property of love not to let go of the beloved thing. This is why men are so tenacious in their opinions (whether they be true or false, provided they are smeared with the <term>varnish of truth<note>original: "veritatis fuco illitæ" - referring to something that has the outward appearance or disguise of truth.</note></term>) that they would even prefer to die rather than cast away the views they have drunk in under the name of Truth.

Axiom 6. *Holding the truth, he wishes others to hold the same.*

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

It is also most natural, and inseparable from the human mind, that whatever a person believes they truly perceive, they desire that others perceive the same. One considers it almost an injury to their own mind if someone else chooses to think differently, and by that very act, seems to silently reproach them for being in error. From this arises that offense of the spirit and silent hatred among those who disagree in their opinions (especially regarding matters of faith). This happens because the mind feels ill at ease and is restless unless it has everyone in agreement, and no one contradicting, so that it may possess the Truth with total security.

->Axiom 7. *Wishing others to hold the truth, one moves others toward the same.*<-

In every discrepancy of opinion, we see it happen that people draw others into their own way of thinking by whatever means they can; this is especially true of those who believe they understand more than others, or who burn with a greater zeal. Hence, everything resounds with disputations, while this man labors to win over that man, and that man this one—not only person to person, but sect against sect, in both Religion and Philosophy—striving to lead them over to their own opinion.

<note>The author is describing the psychological root of religious and philosophical conflict: the "restless" need for external validation to feel secure in one's own grasp of truth.</note>

# DELINEATION

**Axiom 8. *Advancing toward the truth is achieved through teaching.***

For other methods of spreading the sciences, aside from formal instruction <note>original: "doctrinam"</note>, are illegitimate and useless for our purpose. For example, if someone should persuade, order, command, or compel another to believe or feel this or that in a certain way. To truly know or believe is to understand; however, understanding is not a work of the Will <note>The author distinguishes between the 'Will' (desire/choice) and the 'Intellect' (reasoning)</note>, which is usually swayed to want something through persuasions, commands, threats, or coercion. Instead, it is a work of the Intellect, which by its own nature is nothing but a Mirror of Reality <note>original: "speculum Rerum"</note>, receiving into itself whatever you present appropriately and through a clear light. Therefore, present your subject through teaching, clearly and appropriately, and the mind will grasp it.

**Axiom 9. *Many, however, teach commonly, yet do not advance where they wish.***

For the world is full of Books and outcries, by which men labor strenuously to persuade others of their own opinions; but with what progress? Certainly very little, and at a level least worthy of such great contentions. For among so many ranks of those clashing, if by chance one single person is converted, a hundred others remain fixed in their place, refusing to be moved from their opinion. And those who are moved,

<summary>This page argues that true knowledge can only be achieved through clear instruction directed at the intellect rather than through coercion of the will, noting that most contemporary teaching fails to achieve its goals despite great effort.</summary>

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

They are just as easily led from the straight path into a mistaken one, or from one error into another, as they are from Error into Truth. *Axiom 10. Therefore, something is necessarily lacking in the common method of those who teach.*

LXXV. For what is the cause that from such good premises, such monstrous outcomes follow? That all are greedy for the Truth, and yet do not find it? Or that they have indeed found it (in some lucky course) and hold it certainly, yet are unable to show the same to others! It is more certain than certainty itself that we deal with one another unskillfully, and that the true way and method of propagating the truth is not yet commonly held. For truly those principles <note>Latin: *principia*, referring to the fundamental innate ideas of the mind</note> which we have already seen were implanted in each person by the good God, and are common to all, are good: To wish to see the truth; To wish also to follow it if one sees it; To wish likewise to have others as partners in that same Truth, and to desire it, and for the sake of that matter to spare no labor; and yet—

What progress can be made? Truly, the true **Ladder of the Human Intellect** <note>original: "Scala Intellectûs"; a metaphor for a step-by-step method of reasoning</note>, through which both leaders and the led might certainly and safely ascend and descend, is still desired. LXXVI. *For if such a Ladder were constructed, every human mind would ascend to the very summit of all things: which now remains to be shown, by a ladder of several Axioms.* <note>Axioms are self-evident truths that serve as the starting point for reasoning.</note>

1. *Every human being possesses a Mind.*

Indeed, one destitute of a mind would not be a Human, but a brute animal.

2. *Whoever possesses a mind, thinks.*

For just as it is the property of the Eyes to see, the Ear to hear, the Tongue to taste, and the Nerves to touch; so to think is the proper and essential function of the Mind.

3. *Whoever thinks, passes from one thing to another.*

For all thought is a certain motion of the mind, by which it goes from one thing to another,

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the nature of human perception or the initial movement of the mind; the catchword "Therefore" (Ergò) connects the previous reasoning to the following conclusions about mental discourse.</meta>

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

And from that, the mind passes again into something else. From this, it is called the **Discourse of the Mind** <note>original: "Discursus mentis"; this refers to the "running" or movement of thought from one idea to another</note>. For just as the Eye, when it has many things to look upon, cannot see those many things all at once and at the same time, but rather one after another; so the Mind, whatever it thinks, thinks *something*, *about something*, *through something*, one after another.

4. *He who passes from thing to thing, passes step by step.*

For thoughts cohere like a chain, in which one link pulls the next. Even when something comes into the mind unexpectedly, appearing very remote from the previous thought, it nevertheless comes from some occasion; whether that occasion arises internally from some thought, or externally from some object of the senses. Just as in Nature there is no vacuum or gap, so there is none in the Intellect; and just as one cannot proceed on foot except from the nearest point (whether by step, or pace, or footprint) to the next, so it is impossible to arrive at any Thing in thought, except from some other thing.

5. *He who passes step by step can go as far as the Steps lead.*

<term>Discourse of the mind (Discursus mentis): the process of reasoning where the mind moves logically from one premise to a conclusion</term>, <term>Intellect (Intellectus): the faculty of understanding</term>, <term>thought (cogitatio): the act of mental reflection</term>, <term>step by step (gradatim): proceeding by degrees</term>, <term>Pansophia: universal wisdom or the system of all knowledge</term>

<summary>This page explains the "discursive" nature of human thought, arguing that the mind moves logically and sequentially from one idea to the next, much like a chain or a physical journey. It asserts that there are no gaps in human understanding, as every new thought is triggered by a preceding internal or external stimulus.</summary>
