Therefore, every human being is able to ascend in Mind as far as they can see the Steps of Things before them. This was the point to be demonstrated.

LXXVII. *Now it must be demonstrated that a Ladder of the human Intellect <term>Intellectus: the faculty of understanding or reasoning</term> can be constructed.* However, when I say "constructed," I do not mean some arbitrary labor, as is usually the case when ladders are prepared for physical heights—built this way or that, just as it pleases the craftsman, who determines the position, the angle, and the measurement of the steps from his own mind or from the needs of those for whom he prepares them. This *Mental Ladder*, because it will be merely an image of the *Ladder of Things* <note>original: "Rerum scala"; the author refers to the "Great Chain of Being" or the natural hierarchy of the universe</note> woven by divine craftsmanship, will not be subject to our whim. The Things themselves will here provide the measure both for themselves and for the Intellect. *They err dangerously who think it sufficient for Things to be considered as they appear to us, and not as they are in themselves; setting our Senses as the standard for Things, rather than Things as the standard for the senses.* Quite the opposite must be done: Things (in which Truth resides as if in its own Palace) must be established as the standard, both for themselves and for us, if we wish at some point to struggle out of the tumult and collisions of opinions toward harmony, so that just as that Thing

<summary>The author argues that a "Mental Ladder" of understanding must be a direct reflection of the objective "Ladder of Things" created by God, rather than a subjective human invention. # THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

just as it connects within itself, so let it connect our Mind to Reality. Our senses, our imagination, and the Mind itself—or Reason—have nothing of their own to impose upon or bring into Things. Instead, it is the Things themselves that, by impressing themselves upon the senses, the imagination, and the mind, form true images of themselves when perceived correctly; yet they form distorted images when perceived incorrectly. Therefore, absolutely nothing should be attributed to the Senses or the Mind on their own; rather, they must be bound most tightly to Reality, unless we wish to be deceived and mocked without end or limit. <note>The author argues for an empirical approach where the mind acts as a mirror to nature rather than a source of its own truth.</note>

*LXXVIII. It is clear, therefore, that a ladder of the human Intellect can be constructed from the ladder of Reality: because all things are arranged according to steps, both the Universe itself as a whole, and every single Thing individually.* For:

1. *Whatever exists is finite.* <note>original: "Quicquid est, finitum est."</note> (For we are not presently dealing with the infinite, which is One—namely, God.)

2. *Whatever is finite has the boundaries of its own essence.*

3. *Whatever has boundaries of essence has within itself something first and something last, a lowest point and a highest point.*

4. *Where, however, there is a first and a last,*

<summary>This page argues that human knowledge must be strictly derived from the external world to avoid error, proposing that the human intellect should be structured as a "ladder" that mirrors the finite, orderly hierarchy of God's creation.</summary>

*...the middle, the lowest and the highest; there also exists the prior and the posterior, the inferior and the superior.*

5. *Where there is prior and posterior, inferior and superior, there are degrees.*

6. *Where, however, there are degrees, there is distinction; and where there is distinction, there is clarity.*

Therefore, whatever exists possesses distinct and clear degrees of its own essence: which, if rightly observed, lead the mind into the first and last, the lowest and highest aspects of every thing.

LXXIX. *We are confident that such a gradation, which leads the mind toward all things, is inherent in <term>Pansophia</term> <note>Universal Wisdom: a system of knowledge encompassing all things in their natural order</note>, both in the entire complex of things and in each thing individually.* For as much as pertains to the whole, Pansophia has a firm and immovable base, or a triple ray: *Necessity, Possibility, and Facility*; which are solidly established in the book of <term>Præcognita</term> <note>Preliminary Knowledge or first principles</note>. It also has a splendid summit, equally firm: Blessedness, or the rest of desires; which the final book of Pansophia will demonstrate in the delights of the Mind illuminated within itself, in the improvement of all human affairs for the better, and in the illustrious propagation of the glory of God.

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

<note>original: "Templi Pansophici"</note>

We have. It also possesses a firm center, suited to join the summit to the base and to support solid steps. On God's part, this consists of the truest Revelation, made through His Works <note>the natural world</note>, His Words <note>scripture</note>, and His internal Dictates (which are the **innate notions** <note>term: Notiones innatæ; the idea that certain truths are engraved on the human soul from birth</note> inscribed upon the soul of every human being). On our part, however, it consists of **Sense**, **Reason**, and the **Faith** to be applied to God’s testimonies. From this triple shaft, as it were, erected in parallel and connected at every point, that most solid ladder of things is constructed: a ladder, I say, that is *divided into five parts*, rising through five different stories.

For the first and lowest is the ***Metaphysical Ladder***; this raises the mind through all general principles, which are self-evident, as far as it is able to go. The second, in the second region of ***Natural Things***, uncovers the divine craftsmanship in objects through all the levels of created beings. The ***Third*** teaches us to contemplate the works of ***Man, the supreme Creature***, and shows how far the power of human genius can ascend regarding created things. The ***Fourth*** explains the ***intercourse of Man with God Himself, his Creator***, and thereby the mysteries of salvation and perdition.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical outline of the "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom), moving from the contemplation of the created world toward the contemplation of the Divine.</meta>

# OUTLINE

The fifth stage then ascends to the contemplation of God himself, the uncreated one, inhabiting his own eternity. From there, the mind has no higher place to climb; however, it does have a path to descend, toward the right use of the Creator, of Creatures, and of Oneself. This ensures that whatever remains of one's life is spent in wise thoughts and actions, so that at last, through the gate of death, a safe, secure, and joyful transition into blessed eternity may be made.

LXXX. **These things concern the tiered structure of the whole <term>Pansophia</term><note>Universal Wisdom: a 17th-century concept of a system of knowledge that harmonizes all things under God.</note>: so that it may be clear that no other foundation can be laid for human wisdom, no other end proposed, and no other way to reach that end, than the "From Where," "To Where," and "By Which Way" presented here.** All particular details can be arranged in this same tiered series; indeed, they must be, because they cannot be arranged otherwise if we wish to teach easily and powerfully—that is, <term>scientifically</term><note>In this context, "scientifically" refers to a systematic, demonstrable method of knowledge rather than modern laboratory science.</note>. Certainly, just as one is delirious who, standing at the summit of a Tower, expects someone to fly to him from the Earth, or to fly across from the top of a neighboring tower without being led up the steps: so too is he delirious who expects another to grasp his meaning

<summary>This page describes the final stage of universal wisdom—the contemplation of God—and argues that all knowledge must be taught in a systematic, step-by-step hierarchy to be effective.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Divine Eternity, systematic education, hierarchy of knowledge, preparation for death, pedagogical method</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 193: The author continues discussing the systematic structure of the "Pansophic Temple," specifically focusing on the pedagogical necessity of a step-by-step method.</meta>

->THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-

is thrust upon them, without its truth being gradually demonstrated.

LXXXI. *The use, therefore, of this graduated Method <term>Methodus</term><note>The systematic way of organizing knowledge to ensure it is easily understood.</note> will be: to easily teach the ignorant, and powerfully un-teach those in error.* The former should not be able to miss seeing the light of truth and rejoicing in it; the latter should not be able to avoid noticing their own error and being turned toward a love of better and truer things. It must be noted, however, that teaching the ignorant is a single and simple task; but for those in error to be taught, a double effort is required. For the ignorant, because they know neither the true nor the false, are capable of both, and can be taught only the truth without needing to be un-taught the false. But those in error, because they are preoccupied with false opinions, must first be un-taught their error so that they may be rendered both desirous and capable of the Truth, and only then can they be taught. This, as we have already seen, is not accomplished without gradation: because just as an ignorant person cannot be promoted to the summit of clear knowledge <term>cognitio</term> without steps—by some mere throw or a leap—so too the person in error cannot be dislodged from the height of their error, owing to the tenacity of their opinions,

<summary>This page discusses the pedagogical "graduated method," explaining that while teaching the ignorant is straightforward, correcting those in error requires a two-step process of un-learning and then learning.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, method, truth vs error, education, gradual learning, Pansophy</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion on the nature of human belief and the pedagogical methods required to correct deeply held errors. It follows the previous page's exploration of intellectual stubbornness.</meta>

# SKETCH.

which, wherever they have once taken root, cling tenaciously even unto death, unless they are removed with prudence. Indeed, the human mind (by a natural instinct) pays such veneration to Truth that it constantly adores even its shadow, and will not allow itself to be torn away from it unless it is taught truer things. Therefore, so that it may be able to be taught truer things, there is first a need for a certain "degradation"—that is, a reduction to absurdity <note>original: "reductio ad absurdum"; a logical method where a premise is disproven by showing it leads to an impossible or ridiculous conclusion</note>—so that the thinker may openly recognize that his own opinion fights against the principles of knowledge: Sense, Reason, and Faith. For then he will be judged to have been brought down from the height of Error to the level ground, namely, into the common field of principles. From there, he will not unwillingly allow himself to be led through better and firmer consequences to the heights of better and firmer opinions; and he will rejoice to have been led to a place where he feels himself to be better off than before.

LXXXII. This must also be noted: where the gap between the teacher and the learner (or rather, the one who contradicts) can be filled by one single middle term <note>In formal logic, the "middle term" is the element that connects two ideas in a syllogism</note>, there is no need for more; and the demonstration

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structure of logical proofs within the "Pansophic Temple," focusing on the efficiency of chained reasoning over fragmented arguments.</meta>

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

can be settled with a single *Apodictic* Syllogism <note>A logical argument where the conclusion follows necessarily from the premises; a "demonstrative" proof.</note>, as was done by us in Aphorisms 67 and 68. However, where the distance between concepts is greater, it requires more intermediate steps, and a chain must be connected from them, like an *Apodictic* Sorites <note>A "Sorites" is a string of syllogisms where the conclusion of one becomes the premise of the next.</note>, as we did in Aphorisms 71, 74, 76, and 78. This method of teaching represents a Ladder to the eye, and the truth of the chained consequence is revealed more evidently than in the dissected heaps of Prosyllogisms <note>A series of separate logical arguments where the conclusion of one serves as a premise for the next, but presented as distinct units rather than a continuous chain.</note>. For example, in section 71:

> (1) He who has feet in good working order is able to walk.
>
> (2) He who walks moves his feet alternately.
>
> (3) He who moves his feet alternately is stepping.
>
> (4) He who steps is tending upward, etc.
>
> (5) He who tends upward ascends by means of steps.
>
> (6) He who ascends by means of steps is able to climb as far as the steps go.

<summary>This page explains the use of the "Apodictic Sorites" or logical chain as a "Ladder" for teaching, arguing that continuous chains of reasoning are clearer than fragmented syllogisms. It provides a practical example of this step-by-step logic using the metaphor of climbing stairs.</summary>

<keywords>logic, apodictic syllogism, sorites, pansophy, pedagogy, ladder of knowledge, deductive reasoning</keywords>

# SKETCH.

(7) Therefore; whoever has feet can climb wherever the steps lead.

LXXXIII. *In order for this <term>Sorites</term> <note>A "Sorites" is a form of argument where a series of propositions are linked together like a chain, where the conclusion of one becomes the subject of the next.</note> to be truly <term>Apodictic</term> <note>Meaning "demonstrably true" or "beyond dispute."</note>, and a firm chain of Truth, the following must be observed.* (1) Just as in a Chain one link receives the next link; so here the propositions cohere, with the predicate of the preceding proposition always being taken up as the subject of the following one. (2) Just as in a Chain a link is essentially fastened into another link so that it coheres inseparably: so here every predicate must state something essential about its subject, so that it is true without exception. (3) Just as in a chain link is connected to link until the measure of length is completed: so in a Sorites step is bound to step, until one arrives at that which puts an end to the controversy. (4) Finally, just as in a Chain each individual link is solidified in itself so that it cannot be loosened (otherwise, if even one is broken, the chain is ruptured): so in each individual step

# THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

One must dwell upon the <term>Sorites<note>A form of argument in which a series of logical steps leads to a final conclusion; a "chain-syllogism."</note></term> (whenever someone is led by this ladder) until its **Truth** is established and no doubt whatsoever remains. Only then should one proceed.

LXXXIV. But enough. An end must be placed upon our deliberations and sketches, so that we may instead come to the Work itself. For whatever we say, that old proverb can always be leveled against us: *Where there is a need for deeds, words do not suffice.* <note>Original Latin: "Ubi factis opus est, verba non sufficiunt."</note> We, however, promise not to give words, but a work—even if it is not with that perfection which we conceive in the **Idea**. We protest again and again that this is not the task of one man, nor of a single Age; prudent judges of matters understand this for themselves. *Nature herself has willed that nothing great be produced quickly, and she has set a difficulty before every most beautiful work; she has also made it a law of birth that the larger animals are contained longer within the wombs of their parents.* <note>A reference to Seneca’s natural philosophy regarding the slow maturation of great things.</note> Why, therefore, should this great birth (not of our own talent, but of the century) not require both a proper time and many faithful midwives? The Temple of Solomon was not completed by one—

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the collaborative nature of the "Great Work" of universal knowledge, likening it to a grand architectural project.</meta>

# OUTLINE.

It was crafted by skilled artisans: carpenters, coppersmiths, stonemasons, goldsmiths, jewelers, sculptors, and painters all contributed their labor there. In the same way, philosophers, theologians, statesmen, physicians, and all talented individuals have their roles to play here. Therefore, let them hasten and not refuse to build up this common Treasury with their shared resources, and to increase it further through their collective care—for this is a task that concerns us all.

*85. Not only those present today, but also those who will come after us, are invited to pursue this same goal with equal zeal.* For although the perfection of this Work grows ever greater through continuous progress, its ultimate completion is never to be hoped for in this life. Just as numbers have a beginning in unity, yet find no end even as they proceed toward infinity, so too the Wisdom of God <note>original: "Sapientia Dei"</note> communicated to us rises from small beginnings and, spreading itself through infinite reaches, can never be entirely exhausted. Observing this, the son of Sirach <note>Referring to the author of the biblical Book of Ecclesiasticus</note> said: *The first man did not know her perfectly, and likewise the last shall not fully explore her* (chapter 24, verse 31).

<summary>The author calls upon experts from all disciplines to contribute to a collective treasury of knowledge, noting that the pursuit of divine wisdom is an infinite task that will continue through future generations.</summary>
<keywords>collaboration, universal knowledge, craftsmanship, infinite wisdom, Sirach, scholarly community</keywords>

## OF THE PANSOPHIC WORK
<note>Pansophic: Referring to "Pansophia" or Universal Wisdom, a 17th-century ideal of organizing all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note>
### AN ADMONITION.

After the *Ichnographic* <note>ground plan</note> and *Orthographic* <note>elevation drawing</note> *delineation* of the Pansophic Temple has been exhibited by us, I easily foresee that someone might say what Seneca said to his friend Lucilius: *I do not desire from you more than you were already undertaking. Your foundations have occupied much space. Only accomplish as much as you have attempted: and handle those things which you have carried with you in your mind.* (Epistle 31.) To these people, if I were to respond with the words of Ludovicus Vives: *Many things cannot be brought forth and polished at the same time: something sought for a long time CANNOT BE DISCOVERED AND PERFECTED BY THE SAME PERSON: life is not sufficient for so many things, nor is the power of the human intellect, being brief and weak.* And presently: *I forgive the fact that one man* <note>Vives is speaking of Aristotle</note> *did not digest and refine all those things—so many, so varied, so difficult—which he had unearthed: one man cannot perform what a hundred could scarcely provide.* (Book 1, On the Causes of the Corruption of the Arts.) I fear that this may not satisfy those who, in whatever way, hur-

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the challenges of intellectual labor and the expectations placed upon scholars. The catchword "nari" from the previous page completes the word "venari" (to hunt) found at the end of this page's text.</meta>

# ON SCENOGRAPHY

Promises are often treated as debts. Certainly, Verulam <note>Francis Bacon, 1st Viscount St. Alban, often referred to by his title Baron Verulam</note> is lashed by some (even those who consider themselves judicious) because he promised a "Great Restoration of the Arts" <note>original: "Artium Instaurationem magnam"</note> and did not deliver it. However, he never promised this from himself alone, nor did he ever presume to do so. For time and again he protests that he is providing the *Organon* <note>A "tool" or "instrument" for logical or scientific investigation</note> for restoring philosophy; he maintains that the restored philosophy itself cannot be given by the genius or industry of a single man or a single age, but only by the combined and reinforced labor of many, for as long as the work requires.

In the same manner, we also protest repeatedly that one should not expect anything "perfected" from us in that which we advise must be done. We offer only the opportunity and certain first principles of so great a Work. This is because it is better to eventually give a beginning—of whatever sort—to desired things, than to see something only in the mind as an idea and never actually undertake it. It appears, however, that the common crowd of men are merely imitators; they prefer to feed on what is already prepared and rest upon what has been acquired, rather than to track down and hunt <note>original: "venari"</note> the truth and the order of things, which remain hidden and fleeting.

<summary>The author defends Francis Bacon and himself against critics who demand finished results, arguing that the restoration of knowledge is a collective, multi-generational task rather than the work of one man.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Great Restoration, Organon, scientific method, collaboration, philosophy, intellectual history</keywords>

# ADMONITION ON UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: "PANSOPH. ADMONITIO." Pansophia refers to the "All-Wisdom" or universal knowledge system championed by Comenius.</note>

*...that cooked and prepared nourishment, the Stomach would then distribute to the rest of the Body. In exactly the same way, whoever considers the study placed in universal contemplations <note>original: "Contemplationibus universalibus"</note> to be empty and idle, fails to notice that the juice and strength are supplied from that very source to every individual profession and art. And I am certainly persuaded that this has been no small cause for why the more successful progress of learning <note>original: "Doctrinae"</note> has been delayed until now: because work has been devoted to these fundamental sciences <note>original: "Scientiis"</note> only in passing, and fuller draughts have not been drunk from them. For if you desire a tree to become more fruitful than usual, you will think in vain about treating the branches: the earth itself around the root must be worked, and a richer soil applied; otherwise, you have achieved nothing, etc. Later: It is of primary importance that such men (the Researchers of Things) <note>original: "Indagatores Rerum"</note> be chosen from the best and most well-equipped: seeing that their work is not employed for a transitory use, but to provide an offspring of knowledge for the ages. This cannot happen unless such rewards and conditions are established, by which every most eminent man in that art can be fully satisfied.*

<summary>This page uses the metaphors of digestion and arboriculture to argue that universal wisdom is the essential root of all specific sciences, requiring dedicated experts and proper funding to flourish.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, universal knowledge, scientific progress, education reform, researchers, fundamental science</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the advancement of learning, specifically focusing on the necessity of funding and resources for the sciences.</meta>

# ON SCENOGRAPHY

*...so that it may not be a burden for him to die in that same service, nor should he have to worry about the practicalities of survival, etc.* Otherwise, if sufficiently large and splendid rewards are not established for the Fathers of the Sciences, that saying will come true: *And the weak children reflect the hunger of their Fathers.* <note>original: "Et Patrum invalidi referunt jejunia Nati." A quote from Virgil's Georgics, used here to suggest that if teachers/researchers are starved of resources, their students and the fields of study themselves will be weak and underdeveloped.</note> After some other remarks, he adds: *Indeed, the generosity of men has not ceased entirely; for we see not only Books (Libraries) but also Spheres, Globes, Astrolabes, Maps, and other similar things, being acquired and offered for study as aids to Astronomy and Cosmography* <note>The study of the general features of the universe, including both earth and sky.</note>. *We also see some places dedicated to the study of Medicine that have Gardens for the inspection and knowledge of every kind of medicinal herb* <term>Simples: individual medicinal plants used in their natural state</term>; *nor are they lacking the use of dead Bodies for Anatomical observations. However, these things pertain only to a few areas. In general, let it be held for certain that great progress in unearthing the secrets of Nature can hardly be made, unless funds are abundantly supplied for experiments, whether of Vulcan* <note>referring to chemistry or metallurgy involving fire</note> *or of Daedalus* <note>referring to mechanical engineering and complex craftsmanship</note>. *And therefore, just as the Secretaries and Emissaries of Princes are allowed to submit accounts of expenses for their diligence in exploring...*

<summary>This page argues that scientific progress requires significant financial investment and physical resources, such as libraries, botanical gardens, and laboratories, to prevent the "starvation" of knowledge. It compares the funding needed for scientific experimentation to the expense accounts granted to political spies and secretaries.</summary>

<keywords>scientific funding, patronage, astronomy, cosmography, anatomy, botanical gardens, experimental science, Vulcan, Daedalus</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the necessity of funding for scientific inquiry; this page continues the argument by citing historical precedent and the author's personal commitment to the "Pansophia" project.</meta>

->PANSOPHIC ADMONITION<-

*...both in unearthing new things and civil secrets: likewise, the Explorers and scouts of Nature must be compensated for their expenses. Otherwise, we shall never be informed of the many things most worthy of being known. For if Alexander supplied a great sum of money to Aristotle, with which he might hire Hunters, Bird-catchers, Fishermen, and others, so that he might approach the writing of the History of Animals more well-equipped: certainly something greater is owed to those who do not merely wander through the thickets of Nature, but open a path for themselves (and others) through the Labyrinths of the Arts.* <note>The author is quoting Francis Bacon here, comparing the modern scientist to Aristotle, who was funded by Alexander the Great.</note> Thus far he has spoken: I recommend his words be weighed individually by those whom God has commanded to stand out and, as if stationed on a watchtower, to keep watch over the public good.

As for what concerns me, I will gladly do whatever God wishes to be done through my meager means; and as soon as it is granted to return to these studies, I shall prepare a Skeleton <note>original: "Sceleton"; here meaning a foundational outline or structural framework</note> of the entire Pansophia <term>Pansophia: A system of universal knowledge that seeks to harmonize all scientific, philosophical, and theological learning.</term>, and expose it to public judgment. This is either so that the current sequence may remain, if better things are not found: or so that it may be an occasion for persistently seeking even better things: until the best is reached,

<summary>The author cites the historical patronage of Aristotle by Alexander the Great to argue for the financial support of modern researchers and pledges to produce a foundational outline of his universal wisdom project.</summary>
<keywords>patronage, Aristotle, Alexander the Great, Pansophia, scientific funding, universal knowledge, research methodology</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory admonition or preface to the work. It follows the author's justification for his pansophic project, appealing to the potential of collective human effort over time.</meta>

# ADMONITION ON THE PANSOPHIC STAGE

...which is the very irrefragable force and light of Order and Truth, shall have been discovered. Certainly, so that these efforts of ours may stand as a testimony that all things should be considered possible and achievable, (allow me to conclude with the words of the Great Verulam <note>Francis Bacon, Lord Verulam (1561–1626), whose inductive philosophy deeply influenced the author.</note>, with which he concludes his aforementioned Preface): *whatever can be accomplished by some, though not by everyone; and what can be done by many together, though not by one alone; and what can be achieved in the succession of ages, though not in the same lifetime; and finally, what can be done by public care and expense, though not by the wealth and industry of individuals.* If, however, there be anyone who prefers to use that saying of Solomon, *The sluggard says, there is a lion in the path* <note>Proverbs 26:13; used here to describe those who make excuses to avoid difficult work.</note>; rather than that of Virgil, *They can, because they think they can* <note>original: "Possunt, quia posse videntur" from the Aeneid, Book V.</note>; it will be enough for me if my labors are held at least among the prayers or desires of a better sort. For just as one ought not to be entirely unskilled in a matter to pose a question appropriately; so too he should not seem lacking in sense who has desired things that are by no means absurd.

Farewell, look kindly upon us, and pray to God for us, most kind Reader. Given in my retreat, September 10th, in the year 1643.

->T H E  E N D.<-
